### [1] [When is a large language model a good calibration agent? Physics-labeled prompting for budgeted engineering simulator calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22379)
*Ruo-Qian Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Calibrating expensive engineering simulators requires tuning model parameters to match reference data, a process heavily constrained by computational costs. This study investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs), conditioned on physical diagnostic descriptions of simulation errors, can propose informative parameter updates more efficiently than conventional derivative-free optimizers. We test this on a cylinder-wake Large-Eddy Simulation and a damped-oscillator inverse problem. Physics-labeled prompts are evaluated against four text ablations (removed, anonymized, padded, misdirected) and standard optimizers (Nelder-Mead, Bayesian optimization, CMA-ES, and random search). Results demonstrate a strong early semantic advantage: the first physics-informed LLM proposal improves the initial design in 23 of 25 trials. After five evaluations, the median calibration error is 21.9 times lower than physics-free controls. However, this advantage is transient - disappearing by fifteen evaluations - and inherent LLM decoder variability yields a 9.8-fold performance spread. To address this, we introduce a hybrid workflow that uses the LLM as a warm-start, passing accumulated evaluations to a conventional optimizer. This two-stage numerical continuation policy reaches target accuracy in just 9 simulator runs, outperforming the LLM alone (12 runs), Nelder-Mead (17 runs), and Bayesian optimization (>60 runs). Supported by sensitivity analysis validating the test cases, this hybrid physics-labeled LLM scheme proves highly effective as a calibration agent under strict evaluation budgets.


### [2] [TRACE: Transformer Residual Activation Codebook Encoding for Communication-efficient Distributed Edge Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22435)
*Muhammad Azlan Qazi, Alexandros Iosifidis, Qi Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Distributed Transformer inference across co-located edge devices is fundamentally communication-bound, as exchanging activations between sequence-parallel partitions dominates inference latency under commodity edge bandwidths. Existing compression schemes reduce this cost but retain a structural bottleneck: the received data must still undergo key/value projection before attention can proceed, serializing computation behind communication. This paper presents Transformer Residual Activation Codebook Encoding (Trace), a framework that addresses these limitations. Trace places a Vector Quantizer (VQ) at the post-attention residual of every Transformer layer and precomputes codebook-indexed key/value representations. During inference, exchanged activations are encoded into compact codebook indices, allowing receivers to directly recover keys and values through table lookup instead of executing the downstream computations required to derive them. By eliminating the received data downstream computation, Trace enables communication--computation overlap and reduces a key latency bottleneck in distributed Transformer inference. A hierarchical clustering further structures the learned codebook into a multi-resolution hierarchy, enabling runtime adaptation of the communication bitrate without retraining. The framework supports two operating variants: one prioritizing accuracy and the other balancing accuracy and efficiency through buffer-compatible quantization. Experiments on ViT-Base (ImageNet-1K and CIFAR-100) and ViViT-B (UCF-101) show that Trace achieves up to 1.89% higher accuracy and up to 1.27x end-to-end inference speedup compared with the state-of-the-art VQ-based distributed inference framework. Compared with uncompressed sequence-parallel inference, Trace reduces communication overhead by up to 22,624x.


### [3] [Scalable Levenberg-Marquardt Training Framework for Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22925)
*Xianghao Wang, Johan Barthélemy, John Taylor*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The Levenberg-Marquardt (LMA) algorithm is a widely used nonlinear least-squares solver known for its rapid convergence. For small-scale neural networks, LMA provides better convergence and architectural robustness than first-order optimizers like Adam. However, LMA imposes prohibitive memory and computational costs. These costs are primarily from the Jacobian matrix, whose dimensions scale with both the number of samples and parameters. This bottleneck prevents its use in large-scale neural networks. Recent efforts scale LMA using preconditioned conjugate gradient (PCG) solvers to avoid materializing the Jacobian. However, PCG only approximates the solution, requiring many network propagations and a task-specific preconditioner. Other studies optimize LMA to reduce costs, but they restrict their methods to specific tasks or network architectures. As a result, LMA remains impractical for training general large-scale neural networks. In this paper, we propose LeMA, a distributed LMA algorithm for large-scale neural network training on multi-GPU platforms. Our contributions are twofold: First, an exact dual formulation of LMA removes the memory dependence on the parameter dimension. Second, a Jacobian sharding strategy computes Jacobian shards on the fly and in parallel to avoid materializing the full Jacobian. For large-scale neural networks, LeMA reduces the memory footprint of LMA from quadratic in the number of parameters to quadratic in the batch size. This makes exact LMA steps feasible for billion-parameter models without approximate PCG solves. Furthermore, for a convolutional neural network with 1,000,022,632 parameters on an 8-GPU node, LeMA achieves a 7.97x speedup over a single GPU, corresponding to a 99.7% strong scaling efficiency.


### [4] [TALLOP: Joint Optimization of Local Training and Communication Compression for Time-Constrained Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22996)
*Honoka Anada, Yafei Ou, Shinya Takamaeda-Yamazaki*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Minimizing the wall-clock time required to reach a target accuracy is crucial in federated learning (FL), where many applications require frequent model updates as data distributions evolve over time. Nevertheless, in heterogeneous environments, slow clients with limited computational and communication capabilities can delay global synchronization, forcing faster clients to wait and increasing the overall training time. To mitigate this straggler effect, recent studies have introduced per-round time constraints and adapted client workloads within the available time. However, these approaches primarily focus on local computation, whereas communication can become a major bottleneck in communication-limited environments, motivating the joint optimization of training workload and communication compression. Although previous studies have explored such joint optimization, determining an effective training--communication balance under a fixed per-round time budget remains challenging, as the resulting model performance depends on both training progress and compression-induced degradation. We therefore propose TALLOP, which jointly optimizes the local training workload and communication compression under per-round time constraints. TALLOP predicts the training loss achievable under different computation budgets from the early-stage mini-batch loss trajectory (LENS), and estimates the degradation caused by communication compression using a compression-error model (CLEN). TALLOP combines these predictions to select the training-communication allocation that minimizes the post-compression loss under the given round time constraint. Experiments in heterogeneous FL environments show that TALLOP generally achieves competitive or improved time-to-accuracy compared with approaches that optimize only local training or neither, although the benefit over training-only adaptation depends on the setting.


### [5] [Flash RNN-T: Memory-Efficient Joint Network and Transducer Loss Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23409)
*Mahmoud Ashraf*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The recurrent neural network transducer (RNN-T) remains a practical choice for streaming speech recognition, but training requires large intermediate tensors. We present Flash RNN-T, a joint and loss implementation combining specialized kernels with optional tiled recomputation over the full alignment space and vocabulary. Tiled and untiled Flash form the entire measured throughput-memory frontier on medium and long workloads. On H100, untiled Flash achieves 24-39% higher throughput with 26-56% less memory than the fastest baseline on these workloads wherever it fits. Tiling reduces memory further through recomputation. On medium, tiled Flash uses ninefold less memory than that baseline at 16% lower throughput when both use one backward call per batch. On long, it fits where all tested alternatives with that backward schedule run out of memory with 80 GB. These comparisons use each implementation's lowest-memory configuration reaching at least 95% of its peak throughput.


### [6] [Trust but Verify: Designing Region-Inhomogeneous Forward Dynamics for Discrete Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22380)
*Gunmay Jhingran*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Continuous diffusion treats the forward process as a design space; discrete language diffusion often uses absorbing corruption, leaving context fully trusted throughout generation. This makes trust binary: corrupted context is copied as ground truth, propagating errors into generated spans. We reopen this design space by combining absorbing and uniform-mixing kernels with region-dependent rates. Missing positions follow absorbing dynamics, while context undergoes low-rate random substitution, exposing the model to observed but uncertain tokens during training. At inference, a heuristic sampler re-injects noise into suspicious context under an annealed intensity and freezes context updates late in the trajectory. An 8,000-step finetune of a pretrained 170M-parameter masked diffusion model takes under five hours on a 6 GB laptop GPU. The base model assigns roughly 50% probability to observed tokens even when they are uniform-random garbage. For uniform-random corruption, finetuning detects corrupted context with 0.96 recall and 0.91 precision, improves infill token-F1 by 3.8 points at 10% corruption and 6.4 points at 20%, and removes the accuracy dip next to corruptions. Clean conditioning shows no measurable regression, with a false-edit rate below 0.4%. Gains for plausible corruptions are weaker: about 0.5 F1 points at 10% corruption, without statistical significance. Our results suggest that detectability and damage depend jointly on departure from the data conditional: nearby noise is hard to detect and semantically mild, while distant noise is conspicuous and destructive. We interpret absorbing diffusion as the infinite-contrast limit of a trust axis, analogous to the signal-to-noise gap between known and unknown regions in diffusion inpainting. In our setting of a 170M model and a single text domain, intermediate trust is reached by changing forward dynamics rather than architecture.


### [7] [Binding-Specific Relearning Savings for Test-Time Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22381)
*Zhe Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: When a fact becomes harder to elicit from a language model, has the model lost it, or has it merely become less accessible? Parameter-updating test-time continual learning agents are usually evaluated by current output accuracy; we ask whether that measurement captures how prior experience shapes later updates. We test whether learning a specific factual binding produces a later relearning advantage beyond familiarity with its entity, answer vocabulary, format, or query slot. In Qwen2.5-1.5B, we compare three matched, counterbalanced histories: LEARNED receives the correct binding, CONTROL receives matched exposure without it, and WRONG receives a competing relation-valid answer in the same slot. After an identical rehearsal-free continual fine-tuning stream, LEARNED reacquires the target faster than CONTROL at a prespecified checkpoint of partial forgetting. WRONG shows no positive advantage over CONTROL, while its comparison with LEARNED provides slot-matched corroboration, subject to possible competitor interference. The advantage remains positive after adjustment for measured pre-relearning behavior. In a secondary descriptive conversion using an unpaired never-seen pool, prior learning reduces the optimizer updates required for restoration by about 18%. Current behavioral accuracy can therefore underestimate the lasting effect of previously acquired experience. Code is available at this https URL


### [8] [CGDT-CESN: Correlation-Guided and Dynamics-Trained Clustered Echo State Network for Multivariate Time-Series Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22430)
*Hariharan Srinivasan, Masoom Bhargava, Sayan Gupta*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Accurate prediction of multivariate time series requires models that simultaneously capture variable-specific dynamics and cross-variable interactions. In this work, a Correlation-Guided and Dynamics-Trained Clustered Echo State Network (CGDT-CESN) is proposed, which comprises a modular reservoir architecture with each cluster distinctly specializing in one of the variables in the multivariate time series. An innovative reservoir design is proposed in which inter-cluster connections are enabled by empirically observed correlations among variables, providing a data-driven coupling mechanism. Apart from conventional readout training, inter-cluster connection weights informed by correlations are trained, which in turn allows the reservoir to capture time-varying cross-variable dependencies without sacrificing computational efficiency and further improves forecasting accuracy. The prediction performance of CGDT-CESN is found to be superior to that of the conventional ESN when evaluated on two fundamentally different datasets: Twitter information cascade evolution and solar wind forecasting. Also, the improvement in performance is preserved across multiple prediction horizons, even when presented with a low volume of data. By unifying cluster-specific specialization with learned inter-variable coupling, CGDT-CESN offers a principled, data-driven route to multivariate time series prediction.


### [9] [BOLT: Multi-Bottleneck Bound-Guided Learning for Flexible Job-Shop Scheduling with Time Lags and Resource-Coupled Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22433)
*Ziheng Zhang, Wei Zhang, Yunyi Zhao, Zuming Liu, Wei Zhou, Boon Ping Gan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Flexible job-shop scheduling becomes resource-coupled when a scarce vehicle fleet moves jobs between stations, because the schedule sets the transport waits. BOLT (bound-oriented learning for transport-coupled scheduling) builds a lower bound from chain, machine-capacity, and fleet-capacity relaxations. The bound equals the makespan at termination, so its per-step changes form a reward that sums to a constant minus the makespan. Because it never exceeds the attainable makespan, each candidate's bound increase is a proven rise of the makespan floor, which the policy reads as a price. The root bound certifies a completed schedule's suboptimality gap without a solver. On industry-motivated 10-module prefabricated-construction instances, explicit transport coupling outperforms a transport-penalty policy in every tested cell where loaded moves occupy at least 66% of fleet time and in none at or below 56%. Across five zero-shot 50- and 80-module production batches, BOLT outperforms the best dispatching-rule pair on four and the penalty policy on the three with long moves. In the three-vehicle 80-module batch, its mean makespan is 13.0% below the best pair and 4.2% below an hour-budget constraint-programming solver whose proven gap averages 34%. BOLT decides in 2.3-5.9 ms per event on one processor core and re-plans a disruption in seconds.


### [10] [Generative adversarial networks with Monte Carlo dropout uncertainty quantification for structural health monitoring: Fibre Bragg grating data augmentation comparison in Hawk T1A aircraft](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22436)
*Alvaro Peiro, Marios Impraimakis*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The signal generation capabilities of a novel data augmentation framework are examined in aerospace structures. First, the framework learns class-conditional temporal and spectral characteristics of the measured structural responses. It uses conditional generative adversarial network training and Monte Carlo dropout uncertainty quantification as a Bayesian approximation. This step enables stochastic signal synthesis and uncertainty quantification. Second, the generated responses are rigorously compared against experimental measurements across time, frequency, uncertainty, variability, and feature domains to assess physical realism, statistical consistency, and distributional fidelity. Finally, the synthetic data are evaluated to determine whether they should be incorporated into a supervised damage classification workflow. The framework is validated on the full-scale benchmark dataset from a BAE Systems Hawk T1A aircraft tested at the University of Sheffield's Structural Dynamics Laboratory for Verification and Validation. The test involves acceleration, fibre Bragg grating strain, and nonlinear dynamic response measurements. The results demonstrate that the model accurately captures dominant structural dynamics, it guides on signal use for classification improvement based on uncertainty characteristics, and it provides meaningful uncertainty estimates linked to signal complexity and excitation characteristics. Importantly, the framework offers a realistic generative modelling plan as a robust digital twin component for data-driven structural health monitoring, supporting resilient decision-making for condition assessment in safety-critical aerospace systems.


### [11] [CF-NODE: Counterfactual Spatial Node Intervention for Test-Time Adaptation Under Domain Shift, Evaluated Across Five Diabetic Retinopathy Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22437)
*Sabarish G, Deepak Parashar*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Automated fundus-based diabetic retinopathy (DR) grading can substantially expand screening capacity, but source-trained models degrade sharply across different cameras, clinics, and patient populations. Existing test-time adaptation methods depend on entropy minimization or target information accumulated from image batches and streams. These mechanisms can reinforce confidently incorrect predictions and break down when clinical images arrive independently. We introduce CF-NODE, a novel counterfactual test-time adaptation framework for cross- domain DR grading. Instead of trusting prediction confidence or estimating a target distribution, CF-NODE asks how each prediction responds under a controlled intervention on the evidence supporting it. CF-NODE ranks internal spatial nodes using model-derived attention, feature magnitude, and node-set similarity, progressively attenuates the highest-ranked nodes, and uses the stability of the resulting expected-grade response to apply a bounded calibration of the predicted grade distribution. The framework is fully frozen and adapts every image independently using only geometric views and a fixed source-derived prior, requiring no target-derived information or model updates. We evaluate CF-NODE on five external DR datasets under repeated-seed evaluation against TENT, EATA, SAR, and T3A. CF-NODE outperforms all four methods with statistically significant improvements over the second-best method, improves AUC on every target domain, and attains the highest average AUC and macro-F1, delivering stronger grade discrimination together with more balanced recognition across DR severity levels. These results demonstrate that counterfactual intervention sensitivity provides consistent and deployment-ready domain robustness for single-image clinical inference.


### [12] [Rank Measures of Fine-Tuning Updates Need a Noise Baseline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22438)
*Muhammad Usman Altaf*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Studies of what fine-tuning does to a language model often summarise the weight update $\Delta W$ by its rank. We compare the rank measures used for this against the same measures on a random matrix of the same shape, and against two harder references: the pretrained weights, and matched fine-tunes without alignment content. Measures that count directions put our fine-tuning updates only a little below noise; stable rank, computed from the same singular values, puts them far below. Both are right: an update has a few strong directions on top of a long tail. The pattern holds for thirteen released pairs up to 8B parameters, and it is not a mark of alignment: with training held fixed, instruction data does not stand out from Wikipedia or code, DPO gives the same update whether its preference labels are real or half swapped at random (one model, modest preference strength), and scrambled text gives the most concentrated update of all. Applied to a recent claim that post-training is superficial because the effective rank of the weights barely changes, the baselines show that a random update of the same size leaves that rank almost unchanged while wrecking the model, and that an edit built on the effective rank of the update has no measurable effect in our tests. Rank measures of weight updates need a noise baseline, and claims about alignment need a control without alignment content.


### [13] [Sparse Autoencoders Can Induce Local Geometry for Mitigating Catastrophic Forgetting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22439)
*Kamil Faber, Katarzyna Filus, Łukasz Pawela*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Catastrophic forgetting remains a challenge in supervised continual learning, as adapting to new tasks causes representation drift and loss of previously learned information. This raises the question of which representational changes should be constrained while preserving plasticity. We therefore introduce Sparse Autoencoder Geometric Anchoring (SAGA), which converts a Sparse Autoencoder (SAE) trained at each task boundary into local, low-rank constraints on representation drift. Replay examples specify where the constraints apply, while leading right singular directions of the encoder Jacobian specify which changes are penalized. The resulting geometry is input dependent and can be retained without keeping the SAE for subsequent tasks' training. We establish its local relation to latent code matching, define its spectral truncation error, and derive a conditional bound on frozen-readout drift. With replay and snapshot history, the method achieves the highest mean Class-IL accuracy and lowest mean forgetting among the evaluated ER, DER++, ER-ACE, and SGD baselines on three pretrained ViT-B/16 benchmarks, with accuracy gains of 12.15, 4.79, and 3.22 percentage points over the strongest conventional baseline on CIFAR-100, CUB-200, and ImageNet-R, respectively. From scratch results are more mixed, while rank ablations and geometry controls show that SAE-induced low-rank geometry offers useful stability-plasticity trade-offs.


### [14] [Lean4AgenticRL: Advancing Agentic RL with Verifiable Multi-level Process Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22448)
*Ruida Wang, Gerald Friedland, Tong Zhang, Chris Kong*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Enhancing the agentic capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) to tackle complex software engineering (SWE) and general terminal-usage problems is a pressing challenge. Current state-of-the-art LLMs often go through large-scale agentic Reinforcement Learning (RL). However, they mostly rely on high-level outcome rewards, with minimal consideration of low-level toolcall and mid-level workflow-stage rewards, which makes the training less effective. To overcome this limitation, we propose **Lean4AgenticRL**, which, to the best of our knowledge, is the first framework that grounds multi-level process rewards in the unified Lean4 formalization schema for open-ended agentic RL. **Lean4AgenticRL** first builds a *problem-specific workflow* system that balances the agent's own capability and controllability during execution, laying the foundation for further verification. Subsequently, our workflow formalization and multi-level reward annotation methods enable agent behavior verification within a general harness system. It provides multi-level rewards to support more effective agentic RL with the workflow-stage-normalized GRPO algorithm. Using **Lean4AgenticRL**, we train **LAR-9B/27B**, a family of models with enhanced agentic capability. Extensive experiments indicate that our model outperforms the leading model of similar size. **LAR-27B** achieves **77.3%** on TerminalBench-2.1. On average, our models outperform the base model by **9.89%** and surpass the second-best model of comparable size we know by **5.07%**. These results indicate the potential of integrating formal methods in enhancing open-ended agentic RL.


### [15] [Progressive Misalignment Can Emerge from Benign Recursive Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22467)
*Karim Abdel Sadek, Federico Di Gennaro, Fanny Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Training models on their own outputs has attracted widespread attention as a possible route to advance AI capabilities. One prominent approach is self-distillation, where a model is trained to match the token-level predictions it would have produced when given access to additional context, such as a cue, feedback, or instruction. However, the safety properties of this training procedure are poorly understood. In this paper, we present a surprising safety failure of recursive self-distillation, where each self-distilled model becomes the teacher for the next round of training. We observe that when a safe model is recursively self-distilled on its own (safe) generations while receiving an additional non-harmful cue, it becomes increasingly misaligned across a diverse set of harmful queries; we call this Progressive Misalignment. We show this failure mode in both the Qwen3 and Phi-4 model families on SORRY-Bench and StrongREJECT. Progressive misalignment also occurs in a more realistic setup, where the fixed cue is replaced by feedback from a simulated user with benign intent. Using a gradient-based method, we are also able to find benign prompts that lead to progressive misalignment when applying recursive self-distillation, showing that harmful training prompts are not necessary for this failure mode to emerge. We hope our work encourages future investigations of the safety properties of AI systems recursively trained on their own data.


### [16] [Generative Value Classifier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22497)
*Alexander Swerdlow, Perry Dong, Chelsea Finn*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Q-learning is premised on learning a function to predict the value of a state-action pair, with the goal of using such a function to guide policy improvement. Most approaches learn this function from scratch using scalar or distributional return targets. This discriminative approach leaves another source of supervision for learning representations unused: the actions themselves. Rather than directly predict the return of a state-action pair, we ask the reverse question: how likely is the action under each possible return? This perspective provides a critic whose generative component is a return-conditioned policy, which can share the actor's architecture. Training this policy supervises the critic with every action rather than with returns alone, and a squared-error loss anchors the critic's Q-value to the bootstrapped target. We evaluate our approach on both offline and online RL tasks and show gains over leading discriminative methods, including distributional RL methods.


### [17] [Predicting Privileged Information Effectiveness for On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22512)
*Kaijian Zou, Emiliano Penaloza, MohammadHossein Rezaei, Anisha Gunjal, Mohit Raghavendra, Lu Wang, Yunzhong He, Anas Mahmoud*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) enables large language models (LLMs) to learn from privileged information (PI) without a separate teacher model. However, prior work largely treats PI as a fixed design choice, leaving the effects of its content and construction poorly understood. This challenge is particularly important in multi-turn agentic settings, where trajectories contain actions, observations, and reasoning that can be transformed into PI in many ways. We systematically evaluate 16 PI configurations across three multi-turn agentic benchmarks and three student models that vary in scale and reasoning mode. We find that PI effectiveness depends on its content, generation source, and the student model. Information-dense trajectories and PI produced by more capable generators generally yield larger gains, whereas thinking models benefit less consistently. To assess PI before training, we compare PI utility with two KL-based metrics: sampled-token reverse KL ($D$) and error-step KL contrast ($\Delta d^{-}_{\mathrm{err}}$). The KL-based metrics track post-training performance more consistently than PI utility, which can be distorted by information leakage. Empirically, selecting each task's PI by $\Delta d^{-}_{\mathrm{err}}$ also improves post-training performance over random selection, most when the PI candidates differ widely. Finally, effective PI remains beneficial when combined with GRPO, allowing the combined objective to outperform a strong GRPO baseline. Overall, our results provide practical guidance for constructing and evaluating PI in OPSD training.


### [18] [Learning smooth dynamics via Kinematic Interpolants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22519)
*Yiheng, Jiang, Youssef Marzouk, Avishek Joey Bose, Aram-Alexandre Pooladian*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Inferring dynamics from real-world experimental data is inherently ambiguous, as many plausible dynamical systems can explain the same positional observations at measurement times. For higher fidelity modeling of applications ranging from single-cell RNA to vortex dynamics and even flocking of birds, scientific observables increasingly also include velocity estimates that serve to resolve this issue. However, incorporating this rich information in a scalable manner, free of simulation or unnecessary approximations, has remained a difficult challenge. In this paper, we introduce Kinematic Interpolants (KI), a novel framework that extends the seminal methodology of stochastic interpolants to infer nonlinear dynamics across multiple snapshots in phase space, i.e., observables with position and velocity measurements. Empirically, we demonstrate that across several scientific datasets, KI achieves superior reconstruction of the underlying dynamics compared to existing baselines while requiring only a fraction of the training time.


### [19] [Don't Retrain, Remix: Adaptation of Web Agents using Mixture Hypernetworks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22539)
*Md Farhan Ishmam, Minh Pham Dinh, Kenneth Marino*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The web is not static or uniform. Websites that serve the same purpose can look and navigate very differently. Even within a single site, successive redesigns can change how familiar features are presented and accessed. Humans adapt to this by recognizing familiar interaction patterns and reusing what they already know. We give web agents a similar ability with WebMix, a mixture hypernetwork that adapts them to unseen interfaces by composing previously trained low-rank adapters. WebMix begins with a bank of adapters trained on source interfaces. At deployment, a scout agent explores the target interface and summarizes its observations into a compact manual. A lightweight router uses this manual to predict mixing coefficients over the adapter bank, which combine the existing adapters into a new one for inference. Adapting to a new interface takes only a scouting run and a forward pass, without gradient updates on the target. With Qwen and Llama models, we evaluate domain transfer on TimeWarp and WebArena-Lite-v2, and version transfer on TimeWarp. In all three settings, WebMix matches or outperforms in-context learning and LoRA baselines, often matching the average success of specialists trained directly on those interfaces. On TimeWarp domain transfer, it gains about 8 percentage points on average over the best baseline with both models. These gains do not come at the cost of seen interfaces, where WebMix keeps its lead, and extend to version transfer. Adapting to a new interface can therefore rely on exploration and composition instead of retraining.


### [20] [On the SPOT: Enhancing Speculative Decoding for Diffusion Language Models via Commit-Order Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22543)
*Eliezer Shlomi, Eliron Rahimi, Avi Mendelson, Chaim Baskin*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Diffusion language models (DLMs) promise parallel text generation, but in practice face an unresolved speed-quality trade-off. Exact decoding fills in only one masked position per model pass, while faster multi-token decoding schemes are lossy. Speculative decoding is the standard lossless acceleration for autoregressive models. However, it does not transfer directly to DLMs, which must choose both what to generate and where. We show that this order is the missing piece: the model's own predictions from the previous pass already give the right token at 92-100% of positions, but not which position comes next. Based on this observation, we introduce Speculating Positions Order Trajectory (SPOT), which learns to predict this order. A 0.62M-parameter model reads only the target's output distributions and proposes a tree of candidate continuations, and the target verifies all of them in a single pass by checking whether each equals its own next state. On the verification side, our analysis shows that prior block-diffusion verification approaches either assume $T=0$ or a sequential proposal interface. To support tree-based speculation at any temperature, we propose using coupled-noise match verification for diffusion blocks, following shared-Gumbel-noise coupling approaches. Our comprehensive experiments show that SPOT cuts target passes by up to 6.8x and reaches up to 3.2x the throughput of standard decoding, beating the calibrated and training-free drafting baselines in every task and budget at $T=0$ and $T=1$. In addition, we show that the learned drafter transfers across models, demonstrating successful transfer across six different DLMs. Project page: this https URL


### [21] [Deterministic Bounds on Accuracy and Stability of Metric-Space Regression and Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22564)
*Nina Maria Gottschling, Jakob Gawlikowski, Michele Caprio*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Data-driven approaches often face ambiguity in datasets due to sensor or target noise, insufficient information, and noise in the input data. While best-fit machine learning methods can accommodate such noise through uncertainty estimates, they are prone to overfitting and it is challenging to quantify ambiguity of the relation between inputs and targets. We introduce a geometric framework that quantifies this ambiguity through the average kernel size, computed with data-dependent, i.e. model-independent, feasible sets derived from worst-case input and target noise. The framework provides computable, deterministic, non-asymptotic bounds on the best achievable empirical error and accuracy for a finite, constructed dataset, independent of the predictive model class, and applies to regression and classification with pseudo-metric input spaces and metric target spaces. The ambiguity of the input-target relation implies three consequences: an empirical error floor, an accuracy-stability trade-off, and, under standard probabilistic assumptions, a strictly positive distribution-training error gap when training below the accuracy bounds. Under standard Borel assumptions for Euclidean norms, the distributional analogue of the average kernel size is related to Bayes risk as it reduces to the optimal distribution root-mean-squared error times the square root of two. Experiments spanning tabular regression, image regression and classification datasets yield nontrivial bounds on the best achievable empirical error and accuracy on a constructed dataset, alongside descriptive comparisons with model performance on the original datasets. The bounds provide a computable, model-independent reference scale on the constructed dataset and comparisons with model performance on the original dataset are descriptive.


### [22] [Coverage Cliffs in Off-Policy Quantile Certification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22565)
*Pauline Bourigault, Emmanuelle Bourigault*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study when logged contextual-bandit data can certify an upper tail quantile for a policy selected from the same sample. We construct a PAC-Bayes Bernstein lower-confidence envelope for the target cumulative distribution function (CDF), simultaneous over a predeclared threshold grid and all data-dependent posteriors. The resulting procedure, TailCert, uses the envelope's first crossing of level p to return an atom-safe upper confidence bound on the selected posterior's pooled p-quantile (or inconclusive). It can also be used to select a policy under a certified tail constraint. The concentration ingredients are standard; the main theoretical contribution is a matched finite-class certification boundary comparing full logs with their joint weighted CDF indicators at a fixed threshold. On an explicit rare-action class, the required sample size grows linearly with the overlap factor W, logarithmically with policy-search and confidence complexity, and quadratically with the inverse CDF safety margin; the upper and lower bounds agree up to constants in the stated regime. On this class, the CDF reduction hides which actions exceeded the budget; retaining action counts restores the full-log rate. Simulations verify the predicted search-complexity shift, winner's-curse failure of naive reuse, and the power cost of splitting.


### [23] [Structure-Aware Spatial Kernels for Gaussian Process Distributed Port-Hamiltonian Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22575)
*Tianyi Yang, Thomas Beckers*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Gaussian process distributed port-Hamiltonian systems (GP-dPHS) enable data-driven modeling of distributed dynamical systems while preserving their physical structure. However, standard stationary kernels can be inefficient for spatially heterogeneous systems because they neither exploit known input invariances nor reflect structured spatial dependencies. We address this limitation with a structure-aware kernel design that removes irrelevant state coordinates and decouples state and spatial dependencies through a tensor-product covariance. This modular construction allows qualitative knowledge of spatial heterogeneity to be incorporated by selecting an appropriate spatial kernel without requiring an analytical description of the underlying profile. Numerical experiments on a nonlinear string with periodic, smoothly varying, and semi-piecewise stiffness profiles demonstrate that structural decoupling consistently improves reconstruction accuracy, while profile-matched spatial kernels yield substantially larger gains in force-field and trajectory reconstruction.


### [24] [Noise-Robust Forced Alignment for Spontaneous Speech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22583)
*Laurin Wagner, Mario Zusag*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We show that forced aligners degrade on spontaneous, noisy real-world speech, and that fillers, laughter, and disfluencies corrupt word boundaries, impacting many downstream applications. We revisit the classical GMM-HMM aligner with upsampled WavLM features instead of MFCCs, explicit phone units for fillers and non-lexical vocal events trained on verbatim-annotated spontaneous speech, and a discriminative projection fitted on clean and noise-corrupted feature pairs to improve robustness. The pipeline is self-bootstrapped from 149h of curated audio without manual or external boundary targets. On conversational (Buckeye) and disfluent (FluencyBank) speech it reaches word-boundary MAEs of 20.6 and 76.9ms, 19% and 17% lower than the strongest of ten baselines. Catastrophic misalignments (>200ms) are 2.7 times rarer than Montreal Forced Aligners (MFA) best model even after widening MFA's search beam, which we show removes a large pruning artifact from its conversational error; event units halve the error next to laughter, while being meaningfully more robust than existing aligners.


### [25] [Initiating Removal from Operational Symptoms in Federated Vehicular Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22584)
*Zihao Ding, Jun Huang, Qiang Duan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Connected vehicles train a driving model through federated learning (FL) and drive with it, so a faulty contribution often surfaces only as a delayed symptom on the road. Federated unlearning (FU) assumes that a removal request names what to forget. A symptom names no vehicle or round, and aggregation has already spread its cause across the fleet. We propose \underline{A}ggregation-\underline{T}ime \underline{O}perational-evidence \underline{N}eutralization of \underline{E}ffects (\method{}), which makes unlearning a term of the FL aggregation rule. \method{} projects everyday driving errors and reported symptoms onto a subspace spanned by recent global increments. It propagates the surviving effect of every contribution with curvature estimated from these increments and scores all contributions in one backward pass. A two-tier correction suppresses suspected effects in every round and eliminates confirmed ones after a one-round probe. We prove that backward scoring matches forward scoring and that the elimination is exact when training is flat outside the subspace. On three driving datasets, \method{} leaves 43\% to 61\% less residual harm than the strongest baseline in eight of nine settings while staying within 0.36 points of full retraining in utility.


### [26] [Flat Loss, Evolving Features: Grokking in Modular Addition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22593)
*Akash Kumar, Karthik Sankaranarayanan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Can a network keep learning while its training cross-entropy stays nearly flat at a positive level? We show that it can. On modular addition, a standard testbed for grokking, ReLU networks trained with supported-Polar hidden updates, normalized output-layer updates and weight decay can keep 100% training accuracy while their hidden features change and held-out accuracy rises from zero to above 90%, at relatively flat training cross-entropy. Correct classification need not minimize cross-entropy, which still depends on prediction confidence. We prove this coexistence for one-hidden-layer networks in two finite-window theorems from constructed initializations: one gives a constant gain in the Fourier fraction of the hidden features, and the other holds at arbitrarily low positive loss, where a readout refitted on the training data generalizes before the network. Experiments from random initialization show the same coexistence on a window fixed in advance, and in deeper networks held-out accuracy also rises over post-hoc windows with a net increase in training cross-entropy. With a last-layer rate set per training fraction and head decay scaled with the prime, deeper networks at p=47 and 61 meet a criterion on post-hoc windows, fixed in advance, in nearly all fresh runs. A one-block transformer trained with the same updates and a slowly decaying output gain keeps every training pair correct at every logged checkpoint while its training cross-entropy rises and its held-out accuracy approaches one; with two blocks this holds through the held-out rise, after which training pairs begin to flip. A plateau in training loss need not be a plateau in learning. Code: this https URL .


### [27] [Diagnosing GNN Failure Modes with Layer-Wise Probe Entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22595)
*Shanzi Bao, Mar Gonzàlez I Català, Pietro Liò*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Graph neural networks (GNNs) are typically evaluated only at their final layer, obscuring how task-relevant information evolves during message passing. We introduce a post-hoc diagnostic that freezes a trained GNN, fits an independent linear probe to each layer, and tracks probe accuracy alongside normalized predictive entropy. Across real and synthetic graph settings, layer-wise probe entropy distinguishes beneficial, neutral, and harmful smoothing, a capability not demonstrated by existing geometric measures. Moreover, by comparing shallow and deep models, we show that the onset of entropy degradation separates the two main GNN failure modes: structural mismatch under heterophily, which appears from the earliest layers even in shallow models, and excessive-depth oversmoothing, which emerges only as propagation depth increases. These results establish probe-entropy trajectories as a tool for analyzing information flow in GNNs and motivate future work on adaptive depth selection and early-exit mechanisms.


### [28] [Can LLMs predict the outcomes of machine learning experiments?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22599)
*Ayush Bansal, Aldo Pareja, Sean Klein, Serge Didenko, Artem Lukoianov*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Recent advances in AI make Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) increasingly plausible. Large Language Models (LLMs) can already propose, select, and run Machine Learning (ML) experiments. However, the space of possible design choices in ML is vast. An LLM that proposes an experiment needs a reliable intuition of whether it is worth running given the timing constraints of long training runs. In this work, we measure such intuition in current frontier LLMs by evaluating how accurately they predict the impact of self-generated ML experiment ideas before these are put into training runs. To this end, we construct a dataset of 288 LLM-proposed ideas on eight MLE-bench tasks. We then prompt five frontier LLMs (GPT-6 Astra, Claude Opus 5.5, Claude Fable 5.1, Kimi K3, and GLM-5.3) to predict the impact of each idea and compare their predictions with the actual outcomes after training. We report four main findings. First, LLM predictions are substantially better than random chance but far from accurate: even the best model, Claude Opus 5.5, explains only half of the variance in outcomes (R^2 = 0.49). Second, we notice that all tested models systematically underestimate the magnitude of the changes. Third, prediction quality varies widely across tasks. Finally, the choice of the coding harness that actually implements the ideas has little effect on outcomes, and we find no evidence of self-preference. Higher reasoning settings and additional context lead to modest gains. Overall, our results show that current LLMs have useful but limited intuition about which ML ideas will work. We release our dataset to support further study.


### [29] [Rarity of Singletons Matters & Supplementary Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22604)
*Pragatheeswaran Vipulanandan, Kamal Premaratne, Dilip Sarkar*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Estimating missing-mass and unseen-species counts from finite samples relies heavily on rare-event occupancy statistics, particularly singletons. In small-sample regimes, these statistics can be highly variable, making classical estimators sensitive to sampling fluctuations and potentially unreliable. We propose a fragility-aware (FA) framework that explicitly quantifies this sensitivity and adaptively regularizes conventional missing-mass and unseen-species estimators. Using singleton-based fragility measures derived from probability mass and occupancy counts, we formulate an entropy--Kullback--Leibler (KL) optimization that adjusts the reliance on base estimates according to their fragility. The resulting objectives are strictly concave, admit unique closed-form solutions, and require no Poissonization or asymptotic approximations. The framework provides a lightweight correction to classical estimators, including Good--Turing and Good--Toulmin, while reducing reliance on potentially unstable rare-event statistics. Across 360 experimental configurations spanning synthetic distributions and real-world language corpora, our method remains competitive in stable regimes while improving estimation accuracy and stability in fragmented, long-tailed, and small-sample settings. The findings demonstrate that accounting for the fragility of rare observations can improve the reliability of inference about unobserved species and their associated probability mass.


### [30] [Noise-shaped Sampling and Annealing for Scalable On-the-edge Training of Binary and Ternary AI Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22621)
*Zihao Chen, Zhili Xiao, Rui-Jie Zhu, Yipeng Wang, Jason Eshraghian, Shantanu Chakrabartty*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The training loss of binary and ternary neural networks, expanded as a multilinear polynomial over the discrete weights, is structurally equivalent to the energy of a hierarchical high-order Ising model. We exploit this equivalence to apply Ising solvers to discrete-weight network training, while accounting for a fundamental difference in objectives: an Ising solver seeks the ground state of a fixed energy function, whereas training seeks solutions that generalize to unseen data. Our key contribution is $\Sigma\Delta$-Sampler, an optimizer that reconciles these objectives by embedding a stochastic sampling-and-annealing step as a noisy quantizer inside a first-order $\Sigma\Delta$ noise-shaping loop. The loop pushes noise from weight quantization, high-order frustration, and mini-batch sampling out of the low-frequency adaptation band, so that discrete weight updates follow the smooth descent trajectories embedded in the rugged loss landscape. We validate the framework on a planted quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) benchmark with a known minimizer; on CIFAR-10, where a $13$M-parameter ternary model comes within $1\%$ of full-precision accuracy; on ImageNet-1k, where a ternary ResNet-18 comes within $0.4\%$ Top-5 accuracy of a ternary baseline; on class-incremental continual learning, where prior-task accuracy is preserved; and on MatMul-free language models up to $2.7$B parameters, where it outperforms a straight-through-estimator baseline under matched continued pre-training. Compared with standard quantization-aware training, $\Sigma\Delta$-Sampler reduces the training memory footprint by over $50\%$, delivers an order-of-magnitude speed-up, and reduces weight-update memory traffic by four orders of magnitude, making it attractive for on-device training and fine-tuning of large binary and ternary models.


### [31] [Understanding Stability of Outer Nesterov Momentum on Stochastic Pseudo-Gradients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22623)
*Nishant Sharma, Laura Gomezjurado Gonzalez, Ganesh Talluri, Sarath Chandar, Hiroki Naganuma*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Large neural networks are commonly trained across many workers, and communication between them is a major cost. Workers therefore take several local optimization steps before sending their net parameter displacements, or pseudo-gradients, to an outer optimizer, which often applies Nesterov momentum. We study how the outer momentum, the number of local steps and the averaging over workers control the training dynamics, and in particular their stability. For random quadratics, we derive an exact mean-square stability criterion in any dimension, which reveals that the inner batch size determines whether increasing the number of local steps eventually stabilizes or destabilizes the outer iteration. Moreover, while averaging the pseudo-gradients of independent workers can make larger outer momentum values admissible, the number of workers required for stability can grow exponentially with the number of local steps. Finally, we show that the mean-square dynamics governing stability can be probed directly at intermediate neural-network checkpoints using shared-minibatch perturbations. We test our theoretical findings across networks, and measure their consequences for training loss.


### [32] [Network-Wide Freeway Speed Nowcasting and Forecasting from Sparse Fixed Sensors with a Probe-Supervised Graph Convolutional Recurrent Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22630)
*Ran Tu, Zihao Li, Arash Mirzaei*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Traffic agencies need current and near-future speeds on every link of their network, but their fixed sensors cover only part of it. We study joint nowcasting and next-hour forecasting of hourly speeds on all 4{,}127 directional freeway links of the Dallas--Fort Worth region from sensors on 643 links (15.6%). Historical probe-vehicle data supervise every link in training; only sensor readings are used at inference. We call this setting probe-supervised sparse sensing. We propose GCRN+, a graph convolutional recurrent network that predicts deviations from a historical speed profile. It combines a parameter-free, direction-aware kernel interpolation of sensor anomalies, used as input features and as an output baseline, with a diffusion-convolutional GRU on the directed road graph, a low-rank adaptive graph and learnable link embeddings. It scales linearly with network size and trains on a CPU. Sensor readings are emulated from probe speeds, which isolates spatio-temporal inference from detector calibration. On four held-out test days, GCRN+ attains an RMSE of 2.21~mph for the current hour and 2.39~mph for the next hour on unsensored links, against 3.23~mph for the historical average. The best baselines that use the same interpolation features reach 2.53 and 2.75~mph. Adapted recent models without these features (STID, STAEformer, ImputeFormer, MoGERNN) reach at best 2.54 and 2.53~mph; this comparison is between complete pipelines rather than architectures. GCRN+ is best in every distance band except links not connected to any sensor through the road graph. Ablations within one recurrent backbone attribute most of the gain to the link embeddings and the adaptive graph, which can be trained because probe labels exist for every link. For congestion detection on unsensored links, recall rises from 77\% (historical average) and 86% (best competitor) to 89% at aprecision of 0.94.


### [33] [Alert-List Concordance for Evaluating Imputation Strategies in ICU Sepsis Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22635)
*Priyanka Siddappa, Muntaqim Ahmed Raju, Shifat Haider Al Amin, Ruizhe Ma*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Preprocessing is an under-measured reliability risk in clinical prediction: a model can keep a similar AUROC and still change who receives an alert. Imputation quality is commonly evaluated using reconstruction error or aggregate discrimination, neither of which directly measures agreement on which patients are flagged. At a matched alert rate, pipelines with similar aggregate performance can produce different patient lists. This question is especially relevant under the frequent, structured missingness of real ICU records. We therefore introduce alert-list concordance, which asks whether two pipelines identify the same high-risk patients, measured by the Jaccard similarity of their top-k% alert sets with patient-level bootstrap confidence intervals. On PhysioNet/CinC 2019 (40,336 patients, 71.9% missingness), we compare nine imputation strategies and a native-missingness baseline using a fixed XGBoost configuration, fitted and Platt-calibrated separately for each strategy. After Bonferroni correction, 22 of the 28 reconstruction-method pairs showed no statistically significant difference in patient-level AUROC, yet their alert lists overlap at Jaccard 0.518 to 0.750. Similar aggregate discrimination can therefore accompany different patient selections. ForwardFill is a separate outlier, pairing higher discrimination with the lowest overlap in the matrix, 0.40 to 0.45. The broad pattern holds within each hospital system and under six patient-level aggregation rules. At the row level, eight of ten methods fall within an AUROC range of 0.011. In this row-level pipeline, deep imputers did not outperform mean imputation on discrimination, and the sequential implementations cost 5 to 12 times mean imputation in total pipeline wall-clock time on our hardware.


### [34] [The Ephemeral Cost of Switching Optimizers Across LLM Training Stages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22650)
*Xinyu Zhao, Davis Wertheimer, Ashutosh Sharma, Rameswar Panda, Paul Pu Liang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Recent work suggests that switching optimizers between pretraining and subsequent training stages incurs a performance cost, but systematic investigation of this phenomenon is lacking. We study the mismatch between AdamW and Muon optimizers by pretraining 1.2B Llama-3 models from scratch while varying the proportion of updates taken with each optimizer, and similarly interpolating between the two optimizers during continued pretraining and supervised fine-tuning (SFT). Surprisingly, we find little evidence of a meaningful optimizer mismatch penalty: final performance is largely explained by the independent effects of the pretraining and post-training optimizers, with downstream performance generally improving as the proportion of Muon updates during SFT increases. Although matching the pretraining optimizer can reduce forgetting during SFT, this does not translate into improved final benchmark performance. We further evaluate AdamW and Muon for task-general SFT of open-weight models, across the Llama and Qwen families up to 14B parameters, where Muon remains competitive with AdamW, despite these models having been pretrained with AdamW. Together, our results suggest that the choice of optimizer for post-training can be made largely independently of the optimizer used during pretraining.


### [35] [Edit Discovery in Human-AI Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22661)
*Alireza Mirrokni, Shayan Kiyani, George Pappas, Hamed Hassani*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Human-AI interactions are becoming a rich source of data for improving AI systems: while many model responses are already satisfactory, others expose failures that could become valuable supervision if identified and corrected. We study this setting through the notion of an editor: a more capable evaluation process that can inspect a response and either confirm it or modify it, but whose invocation is costly. The editor may be a human expert, a stronger AI system, or the same model equipped with additional computation, context, tools, or information. The key challenge is to decide which interactions to send to the editor before knowing whether an edit will actually be made, since invoking the editor itself consumes limited resources such as expert time, inference cost, latency, or tool-use overhead. We formulate this problem as Edit Discovery Optimization (EDO), which seeks to minimize edit opportunities missed among skipped interactions while controlling unnecessary confirmations and respecting an editor-cost budget. We characterize the population-optimal policy and show that it takes the form of a cost-adjusted threshold on the posterior probability of an edit. Building on this structure, we develop EDIT (Edit Discovery via Iterative Thresholding), an online algorithm for the partial-feedback setting in which edit labels are revealed only for selected interactions. EDIT combines an adaptive edit score, a confirmation-control threshold, and a cost-aware selection rule; it satisfies the editor budget at every round and controls the average confirmation rate up to a vanishing finite-horizon term, without distributional assumptions on the interaction stream. Across four reasoning and factual question-answering benchmarks, EDIT achieves a better trade-off between editor cost, Type-I error, and Type-II error than direct LLM-based batch selection.


### [36] [AlphaBet: Reinforcement Learning for LLM Event Forecasting Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22684)
*Liqin Ye, Rongzhi Zhang, Xin Liu, Zheng Li, Yuan He, Qingyu Yin, Priyanka Nigam, Chao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Forecasting an unresolved event requires finding relevant evidence and deciding how strongly it supports each outcome. Yet outcome-based training of Large Language Model (LLM) forecasters often begins with preassembled evidence at a single prediction date, leaving evidence acquisition outside the learning loop. We introduce AlphaBet, an agentic reinforcement learning (RL) framework that trains a single LLM policy to search, reason, and report probabilities across multiple pre-resolution dates. Each dated forecast is scored by a proper scoring rule against the eventual outcome, allowing one resolved event to supervise decisions under different information states. We decompose forecasting regret into an evidence-acquisition gap and a probability-reporting gap, and show that Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO)'s standard-deviation normalization can distort the reporting target under multi-class proper scores. On GLM-4.5-Air, AlphaBet reduces the Brier score from 0.731 to 0.626 on Forecast-Dojo and from 0.612 to 0.528 on FutureX. Controlled ablations attribute these gains to multi-date training and show that they come from improvements in both information acquisition and probability reporting. Our empirical results validate that GRPO's std normalization degrades proper-scoring-rule performance while leaving top-outcome accuracy largely unchanged.


### [37] [Chance-Constrained Personalized LLM Routing with Online Preference Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22695)
*Ameya Anjarlekar, Rasoul Etesami, R Srikant*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study personalized LLM routing, where a router selects among heterogeneous models with different costs and capabilities. The router must minimize cumulative inference cost while ensuring reliable realized response quality, even though user preferences are unknown and must be learned from feedback during deployment. We formulate this problem as a constrained contextual bandit and develop an algorithm that learns preferences while balancing cost and quality. Under a logistic preference model and a strict-feasibility condition, we establish sublinear expected cost regret relative to an optimal stationary randomized contextual oracle and a high-probability guarantee on realized cumulative satisfaction. The framework extends beyond LLM routing to broader online model-selection problems with reliability requirements.


### [38] [Normalizing Flow Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22711)
*Francesco M. Ruscio, Timothy James Hitge, Fred Zhangzhi Peng, Danyal Rehman, Zander W. Blasingame, Nicholas M. Boffi, Michael M. Bronstein, Alexander Tong, Avishek Joey Bose*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Scalable approaches to learning neural dynamical systems have placed diffusion, flow matching, and few-step generators in flow maps at the forefront of generative modeling. Such state-of-the-art models have limited access to explicit model densities despite evolving from classical generative modeling approaches that leveraged tractable likelihoods, e.g., Normalizing Flows and Autoregressive models. In this paper, we combine these threads of few-step generation and tractable sample likelihoods and introduce Normalizing Flow Maps (NFM), a class of exactly invertible pushforward maps that generalizes normalizing flows and flow maps. More precisely, NFMs learn the integrator of an underlying dynamical system, but crucially, they provide efficient exact likelihood evaluation by construction. To enable scalable training of NFMs, we derive two complementary axes of consistency inherent to NFMs that yield (self) distillation objectives: (1) pointwise matching, which unlocks $\ell_2$-based flow-map style regression losses, and (2) distributional matching, which matches pushforward distributions to the marginals of a prescribed forward process. On conditional image generation on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet-64, NFMs achieve state-of-the-art sample quality among parameter-matched normalizing flows, while supporting likelihood-based RLHF. For equilibrium sampling in Boltzmann Generators, NFMs outperform prior state-of-the-art normalizing flows on both single-system and transferable peptide benchmarks.


### [39] [Spectral Limits of Polynomial Graph Filters on the Long-Range Ising Model Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22713)
*Amin Najafgholizadeh, Mohsen Afsharchi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The Long-Range Ising Model benchmark (LRIM) guarantees long-range dependence by construction: each label is the node's spin times the spin field convolved with a power-law coupling, so any model's distance from the target is measurable. We prove that a readout whose filter is a polynomial in the adjacency, the linear core of isotropic message passing and spectral graph networks, leaves an error floor that no degree lowers, because the target varies along a level set of the spectrum on which every such filter is constant. Computed exactly, the floor and the best filter of each degree predict two spectral networks adapted from published architectures to within a tenth of a decade. A filter escapes the floor only if it weighs separately the offsets the adjacency lumps together, and must still resolve the coupling across the whole lattice. A strided filter of about fifty coefficients does both: fitted to the training labels, its test error is 52 times below the best published network's. A dilated convolutional network beats the best published result at 256 nodes, matches it at 1,024, and gives the first in-domain results at the three larger sizes, while a Fourier neural operator with its standard modes falls further behind as the lattice grows. At its standard budget the dilated network stays two to four orders of magnitude short of a larger strided filter fitted to the labels. The analysis carries over to the benchmark's second coupling.


### [40] [OrderFlowDiT: Transport-Regularized Diffusion Transformer for Probabilistic Limit-Order Supply Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22727)
*Jung Hun Phee, Tucker Balch*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study the conditional distribution of limit-order volume submitted over the next five minutes, given the current order book and recent order flow. The task requires capturing price placement, total volume, and predictive uncertainty jointly. Coordinate-wise diffusion losses do not explicitly account for price displacement, while normalizing fields to unit mass removes information about total volume. Agreement on selected summary statistics also does not establish fidelity to the joint field distribution. We introduce OrderFlowDiT, a conditional Diffusion Transformer for unnormalized bid and ask volume-at-price fields, trained with an unbalanced optimal-transport (UOT) auxiliary loss. We also develop a full-field evaluation framework that combines a transport cost sensitive to price placement and total volume with proper scoring rules and calibration diagnostics. In the primary comparison on 1950 validation conditions across 30 U.S. equities, adding UOT reduces full-field transport cost by 45.8% and the transport energy score by 26.1% relative to training with mean squared error alone. Both measures improve for every stock. These findings support incorporating price geometry and volume into generative modeling, with field accuracy and predictive uncertainty evaluated together.


### [41] [Native Geometry vs. Linear Recoverability: How Alignment Changes Platonic Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22749)
*Angus Hoy, Austin Lutterbach, Jasper Chong, Aman Kumar, Michael J. Smith*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Do larger models learn increasingly similar representations of the same world? The Platonic Representation Hypothesis (PRH) predicts that they should, but similar can mean native cosine geometry---nearest-neighbour structure induced by cosine similarity before any learned alignment---or linear recoverability: correspondence revealed by a supervised linear map. Using matched Legacy Survey and Hyper Suprime-Cam (HSC) embeddings across five model families, we find that Ridge alignment strengthens the relationship between model size and cross-dataset neighbourhood correspondence relative to native cosine geometry. The effect appears in all five families and persists when both representations are reduced to dimension, so it is not simply that wider embeddings give alignment more dimensions to work with. The learned maps remain far from rotations or uniform scaling, and Sparse Autoencoder (SAE) and Block-Sparse Featuriser (BSF) features give no advantage over the same alignment applied to dense embeddings. Model scale thus improves linear recoverability more clearly than native cosine-neighbourhood correspondence, without establishing convergence toward a shared native metric geometry.


### [42] [Refusal Is Not Robustness: Measuring Harmful Reachability in Autoregressive Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22754)
*Parv Kapoor, Teresa Fortes, Claudia Mamede, Rui Abreu, Jonathan Aldrich, Eunsuk Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: LLM safety evaluations via jailbreaking typically index on attack success rate (ASR): whether a generated response violates safeguards. However, ASR only evaluates sampled outputs and provides little information on the underlying generation process. Autoregressive language models define distributions over possible outputs, and a sampled refusal can coexist with substantial probability mass on harmful generation paths. We study this hidden structure through bounded continuation analysis (BCA), which models autoregressive generation as a stochastic process over token prefixes and computes the finite-horizon probability that generation reaches a harmful continuation. BCA constructs a bounded continuation tree, labels terminal continuations with a semantic harm evaluator, and estimates harmful reachability using exact probabilistic model checking. BCA complements ASR by measuring how much harmful behavior remains accessible beneath a sampled safe response. This makes it useful for distinguishing models that look identical under measured ASR values, and allocating red-team effort toward regions of the generation process with greater hidden risk. Across four instruction-tuned LLMs and 100 JailbreakBench behaviors, we show that BCA effectively quantifies hidden harmful behavior and provides a useful signal to guide red teaming.


### [43] [Acquisition Function-Guided Generation for Bayesian Optimization over Discrete Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22763)
*Tommaso Ancilli, Thomas Wedenig, Henry Moss, Robert Peharz*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Bayesian optimization (BO) over discrete spaces is challenging due to missing gradients and the sparsity of valid solutions. Latent-space BO sidesteps both issues by fitting a surrogate in the latent space of a generative model, which however substantially complicates the direct incorporation of domain knowledge that is naturally expressed in the original discrete space. In this work, we instead combine a pretrained generative model with a surrogate defined directly in data space, where inductive biases such as domain-specific kernels can be readily incorporated. Rather than maximizing the acquisition function over the discrete design space, our method samples candidates from the generative model under a distribution tilted by the acquisition function. To recover useful gradients for sampling, we embed this tilted distribution into a diffusion bridge that yields a smooth, posterior-averaged acquisition function. This leads to \emph{acquisition function guided generation} (AFGG), which we instantiate with a categorical diffusion model and an autoregressive normalizing flow. Across a broad suite of molecular benchmarks, the diffusion model variant performs on par with leading BO methods, while the flow model variant outperforms state-of-the-art methods in most cases and remains highly competitive otherwise. Our implementation is available at this https URL .


### [44] [Reusing Calibration Gains across Model Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22771)
*Xu Cao, Wenxuan Xiao*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Scale calibration learns an update for one model representation. We study how much of that update can be reused by another representation of the same pretrained model. Our transfer procedure extracts positive group-wise gains and installs them in the recipient's weights or native quantization scales without recipient-side optimization. On a quantized recipient, it preserves integer codes, zero points and storage layout. For a Qwen3-1.7B floating-point/GPTQ INT3 pair, bidirectional transfer retains 76.9-90.5% of the text-loss improvement from recipient-specific calibration on fresh WikiText and C4 cohorts excluded from project development. Shuffling the same gain values within each projection worsens both recipients, showing that their learned group assignment matters after transfer. A Llama-3.2-3B control keeps the initial native tensors at all four calibrated projections identical and changes nine projections elsewhere. Cross-installation retains 98.6-99.3% of the text-calibration benefit, alongside a small, resolved preference for each recipient's own gain. These results quantify both the adaptation carried by a reusable calibration update and the remaining benefit of recipient-specific training.


### [45] [Next Token or Vector? Learning How Far to Decode](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22788)
*Sai Sreenivas Chintha, Chidera Biringa, Rishab Sharma, Anusha Agrawal, Prachi Badarayani, Gemma Zhang, Nigel Boachie Kumankumah, Marco Ayala, Sulaiman Vesal*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Standard autoregressive decoding advances one token at a time, even when several upcoming tokens can be predicted together. We propose CADENCE (Context-Adaptive DEcoding), a generation method that learns both what to draft and how far to draft. Gaussian prediction heads represent future spans as continuous latent vectors, and a shared decoder converts the selected vector into token predictions in parallel. A learned router selects the prediction length from the model's own hidden states and predicted Gaussian parameters, adapting generation granularity to the current context with single-token generation as a fallback. The backbone verifies each proposed continuation and retains the prefix that matches its greedy predictions. Experiments with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on Alpaca, GSM8K, and HumanEval show that router-selected positions yield higher draft acceptance than applying the corresponding prediction length everywhere. CADENCE achieves up to 1.3x higher generation throughput than standard greedy decoding.


### [46] [Ori: Drift-Aware Streaming $k$-PCA for Very Low-Rank LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22794)
*Xinyu Luo, Jincheng Zhou, Mengbo Wang, Zilin Shen, Nadia Atallah Lanman, Petros Drineas, Bruno Ribeiro, Brian Bullins*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: A prominent family of low-rank LLM optimizers, pioneered by GaLore, performs gradient-subspace projection: at each step, they project the gradient into a low-dimensional subspace and map the update back to the full model. Most existing work either recomputes the gradient subspace at certain intervals, or performs post-hoc adaptation to catch up to the moving subspace. In this work, we explicitly identify in practice the slow, stable manner in which LLM gradient subspaces drift across time. To this end, we propose Ori, a novel low-rank optimizer for training LLMs that captures the shifting principal gradient directions over time to achieve state-of-the-art accuracy under stringent low-rank constraints. Ori employs a streaming $k$-PCA algorithm inspired by Oja++, whose low per-step cost enables high-frequency, on-the-fly subspace tracking. In practice, Ori reduces the performance gap of the state-of-the-art low-rank optimizers to full-rank optimizers by up to 69.3\% on a 1B LLaMA pretraining task, and achieves the best performance on 10 out of 16 task scenarios in Roberta-Base fine-tuning. Together, these contributions allow Ori to further push the performance and efficiency frontiers of gradient-projecting optimizers, narrowing the gap to full-rank training for LLMs. Code can be found at this https URL .


### [47] [Partial-Revelation Panel Forecasting: Predictive Information Gain, Allocation, and Coarsening-Consistent Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22831)
*Bin Wen, Dai-Qiao Zhang, Tien-Ping Tan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study partial-revelation panel forecasting: given $k$ of $n$ annotator votes on the previous dialogue turn, predict the full count distribution of the next turn's panel. This cross-turn predictive information differs from same-item annotation value. Under any nested revelation mechanism, the population decrement satisfies $V(k)-V(k+1)=I(Y;R_{k+1}\mid X_k)$; a fitted model also contributes adjacent excess-risk differences. Coarsened-completely-at-random (CCAR) masking supplies the exchangeable random-rater interpretation used in our operators. We define coarsening-consistent panel operators with a closed-form Dirichlet-multinomial realization, learned completion, and rater-count transfer. Against per-level lookup, cold-start count negative log-likelihood differences are -0.422/-0.166/-0.014 nats on IEMOCAP/M3ED/EmoWOZ. A held-out rater experiment measures same-item value: first and second additional votes reduce NLL by 0.583/0.221, 0.578/0.184, and 0.180/0.069 nats, respectively. Cross-turn and same-item gains are not interchangeable, and CCAR masks are controlled interventions rather than observed arrival processes. Consistency is auditable but does not guarantee predictive improvement; per-level marginalization can outperform a shared table. Target-free allocation beats uniform only where row-level signal is predictable: at budget one it recovers 83--93% of retrospective feature class headroom on IEMOCAP/M3ED but does not improve EmoWOZ. Gains are modest and corpus-dependent, with several small or null partial-revelation differences. Code and aggregate result artifacts accompany the preprint.


### [48] [Benchmarking and Enhancing Agentic Causal Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22846)
*Pingchuan Ma, Rui Ding, Shuai Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: LLM agents can now run causal discovery algorithms on a dataset and report what comes out. Whether they do more than that, such as noticing where an algorithm went wrong and fixing it with further tests, is hard to tell from current benchmarks. Most of them give the variables meaningful names, and an LLM shown only the names of the Tübingen cause-effect pairs already gets 99% of the directions right. With the names removed, the data are usually clean enough that picking a better algorithm than PC closes most of the gap. We introduce Heuresis, a benchmark of anonymous simulated datasets whose mechanisms range from linear Gaussian to heavy-tailed. For each family of datasets it separates what a better existing algorithm can gain from what only a closer look at the data can gain, and it traces every edge an agent changes.
