Every family leaves a gap that none of the algorithms, the router and the two causal-discovery foundation models we ran can close, so a score above the best chosen algorithm has to come from using the data. We instantiate the benchmark with an agent harness and three LLMs, GPT-6.1 Sol, Claude Opus 5.5 and Qwen 3.8 Flash. GPT-6.1 Sol surpasses the best chosen algorithm on all six families, by 14% adjacency F1 on the most difficult one, and Claude Opus 5.5 does so by 7.4% there. A third of the true edges the two agents restore on the two hardest families appear in no algorithm's output. The code and data will be publicly released to facilitate future work.


### [49] [Learning to Be Guided: Shaping Latent Geometry for Classifier-Free Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22885)
*Haoming Liu, Junhao Zhu, Shaoyi Zheng, Yuanhe Guo, Jinnuo Liu, Shenji Wan, Hongyi Wen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Classifier-free guidance (CFG) is a standard component of conditional diffusion models, and guided generation performance has become the \textit{de facto} measure of state-of-the-art models. Recent work on latent diffusion models seeks to learn latent spaces that are easier to model with diffusion. However, under CFG, generation additionally depends on how conditions are arranged in the latent space. We study this arrangement under discrete class conditions, termed \emph{class geometry}, and investigate what class geometry allows a latent space to work well with CFG. Under a Gaussian class model, we show that shifting variance from intra- to inter-class at fixed total variance makes classes more distinguishable under noise, and derive a noisy posterior objective whose per-sample descent direction is proportional to the oracle CFG correction. Based on the analysis, we propose Prototype Regularization (PR), a lightweight surrogate for the intractable noisy posterior objective, which applies a cosine prototype classifier to spatially pooled latents. With a controlled training budget (20 epochs for VAE, 80 epochs for DiT), adding PR to an LDM baseline reduces guided gFID on ImageNet from 3.31 to 2.28, surpassing a DINOv3-aligned VA-VAE (2.44) with negligible overhead. We further demonstrate that PR generalizes to pseudo-labels derived from clustering, continuous text-embedding conditions, and video latents. Together, our findings highlight class geometry as a critical factor in guided generation and take a first step toward CFG-compatible representation learning. Our project page is available at: this https URL .


### [50] [Batched Contextual Reinforcement II: Does RLVR Need Long Rollout Windows?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22887)
*Bangji Yang, Hongbo Ma, Ruihan Guo, Jiajun Fan, Xi Zhu, Ge Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Long rollout windows make reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) expensive, yet simply shortening them can remove the correctness signal: when every single-problem rollout truncates before an answer, group-relative accuracy advantages vanish. As a technical continuation of Batched Contextual Reinforcement (BCR), we study how concurrent rewards relax this dependence on long windows. Concurrent Constraint Reinforcement (CCR) places three independently verifiable problems in one capped training completion. Correct answers completed before truncation retain credit, while a shared budget encourages shorter useful reasoning without an explicit length penalty. We connect this mechanism to conditional reward- and update-variance analyses and study 4B and 8B mathematical reasoning models. Against single-problem GRPO with a 32k window, CCR with an 8,000-token window reduces tokens per training step from 975,464 to 174,957 on 4B and from 793,832 to 165,788 on 8B, saving 82.1% and 79.1%, respectively. An 8B training trace shows increasing accuracy reward alongside shorter completions. Single-problem evaluations provide supporting evidence of retained reasoning quality. These results identify reward-preserving training structure as a practical route to lower per-step cost in short-window RLVR.


### [51] [What Accuracy Hides In Learned Transformer Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22891)
*Jonathan Lee, Narmeen Oozeer, Philip Quirke*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Transformer Programs (TP) (Friedman et al., 2023) constrain a Transformer's architecture so that its learned computation can be extracted as readable Python code. TP expose learned computations, but token accuracy can hide incorrect complete answers. In this work, we examine how program inspection provides insights beyond accuracy scores and helps guide training. We first evaluated TP on nine algorithmic tasks. We compared token accuracy with accuracy on complete output sequences and checked for failures missed by sampled tests. Further, we assessed training data improvements informed by program inspection and failure analysis, finding gains in sequence accuracy on several tasks. We examined a counting task where a program achieved 100% accuracy on sampled test sequences but failed on another input using the symbols and lengths allowed during training. The program counted correctly, but the required answer was missing from its output labels. After adding training examples with larger counts, the retrained models passed exhaustive evaluation over the bounded domain. In the frequency ranking task, inspection showed how correct counts contributed to incorrect answers in programs trained before and after changes to the training data. Finally, we studied constraints imposed by TP training and operations. The TP training process can lead to accuracy losses late in training. Our reference constructions also show how composing the restricted TP operations can use many layers or large lookup tables.


### [52] [A Feature Selection Layer Approach for Multi-Class Classification on Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22898)
*João Pedro Silveira e Silva, Márcio Dorn, Bruno Iochins Grisci*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In the age of data-driven decision-making, deep learning models have become powerful tools for uncovering patterns and making predictions that influence many aspects of society. However, their broader adoption in scientific and business applications is often limited by a lack of interpretability. Feature weighting methods, which assess the relevance of individual features, are among the most widely studied approaches for improving interpretability. While post hoc techniques have advanced significantly, especially in modeling nuanced relationships between features and labels in multiclass problems, embedded methods typically fall short in this regard. Embedded techniques integrate feature relevance learning directly into the model training process, but they are often unable to capture label-specific relationships. To address the limitation, we propose multiple extensions to the Feature Selection Layer, an embedded feature weighting mechanism for neural networks. Through extensive experimentation, we identify the most effective extension and benchmark it against established feature weighting methods. Our results show that the proposed method matches existing techniques in identifying globally relevant features and outperforms them in capturing label-specific relevance. By leveraging the internal dynamics of the trained neural network, it reveals how individual features contribute to the prediction of specific labels, requiring no post-processing beyond the training process.


### [53] [Large Diffusion Models Need More Than EMA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22945)
*Chuyu Hu, Huanran Chen, Shitong Shao, Yinpeng Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Large-scale diffusion models are commonly trained with a constant learning rate (LR) and exponential moving average (EMA), despite the strong performance of LR decay and Schedule-Free optimization in training large language models. We study whether EMA is uniquely suited to diffusion training, or whether stronger alternatives have been underexplored. Our theory shows that averaging a constant-step trajectory has an inherent optimization limitation, whereas LR decay and Schedule-Free optimization admit vanishing stationarity guarantees under standard nonconvex assumptions. Empirically, LR decay and EMA provide complementary benefits: combining decay with EMA further improves the standard baseline at most tested starting points, although the gain remains sensitive to when decay begins. Schedule-Free avoids this timing requirement and consistently outperforms EMA with a constant LR across training stages, achieving both lower fixed-evaluation objectives and better generation quality. Our analysis further shows that all three strategies substantially reduce parameter variability, yet stronger stabilization is not always better: EMA with a constant LR retains comparatively higher parameter variability, while aggressive decay combined with EMA achieves the lowest checkpoint variability but also weakens continued optimization. Schedule-Free substantially reduces parameter variability while preserving more late-stage progress, providing a better balance between parameter stability and continued optimization. Finally, the benefits of Schedule-Free extend beyond pretraining to ReFL and generator-side DMD, demonstrating its transferability across distinct diffusion optimization settings. Overall, our results suggest that large diffusion models need more than parameter averaging alone: effective optimization requires balancing variance reduction with continued optimization.


### [54] [On the tradeoff between robustness and geometric accuracy in adversarial training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22955)
*Rachel Morris, Ryan Murray, Kerrek Stinson*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: For non-parametric binary classification in the presence of an adversary, we prove an optimal quantitative estimate for adversarial classifiers controlling geometric accuracy, as measured by the Hausdorff distance to the Bayes classifier, in terms or their robustness, as encoded by the adversarial budget $\varepsilon$. This rigorously shows that robust classifiers sacrifice at most $O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})$ or $O(\varepsilon)$ in accuracy, depending on the regularity of the underlying Bayes classifier. In contrast to prior work on the incompatibility of robustness and accuracy over low-complexity hypothesis classes, our work shows that high-complexity hypothesis classes allow for robust classifiers with precisely quantified accuracy. Our results apply for adversaries determined by any norm on Euclidean space and in both the infinite and finite data settings.


### [55] [Towards Hyperparameter Transfer for Differentially Private Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22976)
*Tianze Wang, Zhiqi Bu, Linjun Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Scaling laws and hyperparameter tuning remain central challenges in training large-scale models, particularly under differential privacy (DP), where optimization is further complicated by noise injection and per-sample gradient clipping. While Maximal Update Parametrization ($\mu$P) allows for zero-shot hyperparameter transfer in standard training, we show that it fails under DP constraints due to the distinct spectral properties of high-dimensional noise versus low-rank gradients. In this work, we introduce DP-$\mu$P, a theoretically grounded framework that extends $\mu$P to the privacy-preserving setting. By analyzing the interaction between gradient clipping, noise injection, and feature learning, we derive a Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR) condition that governs layer-wise learning rate scaling. Combining this with our proposed spectral scaling formulation, we provide specific scaling rules for DP-SGD, DP-Adam, and DP-Muon optimizers. Empirically, we demonstrate that DP-$\mu$P enables effective zero-shot transfer of learning rates across model widths on diverse architectures (MLP, ViT, GPT-2), matching or exceeding the performance of individually tuned baselines while significantly reducing computational costs.


### [56] [A Label Noise Robust Algorithm for Graph Data using Neighborhood Label Distribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22998)
*Pintu Kumar, Nandyala Hemachandra*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Graph Convolutional Networks (GCNs) have demonstrated exceptional performance on graph-structured data. However, their success heavily relies on the availability of clean labels, an assumption that often fails in real-world scenarios. The learnability of GCNs is intrinsically linked to the neighborhood structure of nodes, making it critical to understand how this structure changes in the presence of label noise. In this work, we investigate how the neighborhood label distribution of a node changes under label noise. We mathematically derive that label noise smooths this distribution, which inherently increases its entropy and severely decreases interclass distinguishability. To address this challenge, we propose Leave-one-out Entropy Node Screening (LENS), an end-to-end framework that identifies noisy nodes by measuring the reduction in neighborhood entropy when a specific node is removed. LENS further incorporates a relabeling mechanism to recover and preserve valuable feature information instead of entirely discarding the identified noisy nodes. Crucially, the end-to-end computational overhead of LENS is remarkably low. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that our algorithm achieves state-of-the-art node classification accuracy while executing an order of magnitude faster than existing noise-robust approaches.


### [57] [A Minimal ReLU Classifier on Non-Linearly Separable Data: Convergence and Implicit Bias at and beyond the Edge of Stability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23007)
*Quan Nguyen, Robin Yadav, Param Mody, Mark Schmidt*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Gradient descent (GD) with large learning rates can successfully optimize neural networks even though classical smoothness-based learning rate conditions are violated. Yet theory for this regime largely covers linear models or linearly separable data. We analyze a minimal setting outside both regimes. Specifically, a two-neuron ReLU network trained with logistic loss on 1D non-linearly separable data. The objective is nonconvex and nonsmooth, and GD exhibits edge-of-stability (EoS) behavior. We identify an active cone in parameter space that contains every perfect classifier, where the problem becomes piecewise logistic regression with a varying effective dataset. Within this cone, we prove a finite-time loss bound for constant learning rates and show that GD converges in direction to an explicit max-margin vector, while the orthogonal component converges to the unique minimizer of a non-strongly convex potential. We further establish catapult results where exponentially small loss is reached after a single loss spike. Furthermore, we propose an adaptive schedule that lets learning rates grow to infinity without leaving the active cone. We prove that this schedule achieves a loss rate of $O(1/(T\log{T})$, which is faster than the standard $O(1/T)$ rate. Our theoretical results extend to local convergence on more general network and are validated on MNIST dataset. Overall, we show that this setup provides a tractable neural-network model for studying large and adaptive step sizes beyond linear predictors and separable data.


### [58] ["Deep lifts": Neural networks can generalize even with the dumbest optimizer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23009)
*Sunil Madhow, Yu-Xiang Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Given a labeled dataset $\{(x_i, y_i)\}_{i = 1}^n$, consider drawing a parameter vector $\hat{\theta}$ uniformly at random from those that interpolate the data within a width-$W$ depth-$D$ ReLU architecture with continuous weights bounded within $[-M, M]$. We study the generalization properties of $\hat{\theta}$ in the case where the dataset can, in fact, be explained by a smaller $w$-by-$d$ ReLU network. Despite the obliviousness of the selection rule for $\hat{\theta}$, it adaptively generalizes at the rate $\tilde O\left(\frac{wdWD}{n}\right)$, rather than being governed by the VC dimension of the full $W$-by-$D$ hypothesis class. The reason is due to a particular inductive bias in ReLU networks: the $w$-by-$d$ parameter *witnesses* an exponential volume of $W$-by-$D$ interpolating parameters, which dwarfs the volume of poorly generalizing parameters (conditioned on interpolation). We explicitly construct this set of near-equivalent $W$-by-$D$ parameters by a proof technique called *deep lifting*, and then tie the achievable volume to stability properties of the $w$-by-$d$ witness. Examples show that these techniques allow us to convert approximation-theoretic results into generalization guarantees for nonparametric classes. We further show that generalization of uniform-random interpolators can survive label corruptions.


### [59] [Towards Looped Models Done Right, Part I: Topology, Input Injection, Recurrent-State Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23036)
*Benhao Huang, Chufan Shi, Junlin Chen, Shicheng Wen, Zhengzhong Liu, Eric Xing, Xuezhe Ma*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Looped language models reuse physical Transformer blocks across logical depth, but established architectures entangle recurrence placement, input injection, and recurrent state organization. We isolate these axes in models trained from scratch at matched parameter scale, logical depth, and token budgets, and then test whether the resulting design principles transfer to resident- and active-parameter-matched Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models. The prelude-recurrent block-coda sandwich architecture consistently improves performance on context extraction and reasoning-intensive tasks. Input injection primarily enhances knowledge retrieval and context utilization, whereas random initialization and separate H/L recurrent states provide mixed or negative returns. Taken together, these results favor the Huginn-style design: at the 730M-parameter and 336B-token scale, the complete Huginn architecture outperforms Ouro across all ten benchmarks. The advantages of Huginn over Ouro transfer to MoE models. At the 8BA0.8B (32BA3B when unrolled) scale trained on 500B tokens, Huginn outperforms Ouro on nearly all benchmarks spanning knowledge, reasoning, and coding, while also exhibiting more balanced expert routing. Under matched training and inference FLOPs, the Huginn-style model uses 75% fewer resident parameters than a 112-layer feedforward MoE baseline. Despite its smaller physical parameters, it outperforms the feedforward baseline on DROP and GSM8K, matches it on MATH500, and remains competitive on the other benchmarks.


### [60] [GR-OPD: Group-Relative Sequence Credit for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23042)
*Yushu Wang, Renkai Yang, Jingcao Xu, Zili Geng, Haihong Tang, Yaohui Jin, Hao He*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) provides token-level teacher feedback, yet common sampled-token updates assign credit locally and omit the effects of earlier actions on subsequent feedback. We introduce \method{} (Group-Relative On-Policy Distillation), which decouples feedback computation from credit assignment by aggregating token feedback into calibrated sequence-level credit. We establish a theoretical basis for this approach by showing that, under ideal on-policy assumptions, broadcasting raw summed feedback to every response token yields an unbiased estimate of the negative gradient of the sequence-level reverse-KL objective. To support effective learning in practice, \method{} discounts later feedback that may become less reliable under distribution shift and uses same-prompt rollout groups to calibrate teacher guidance. It preserves relative response preferences while retaining a controlled fraction of the group mean, which we term collective teacher pressure. Across five Qwen3 teacher--student settings and six mathematical reasoning benchmarks, \method{} outperforms vanilla OPD in macro Avg@K in every setting, including gains of 10.9 and 11.5 percentage points in two settings with matched thinking patterns. It also maintains stable training under mismatched thinking patterns, where vanilla OPD collapses. Ablations show that retaining an appropriate amount of collective teacher pressure can improve learning, while excessive collective teacher pressure or an unrestricted feedback horizon can destabilize training.


### [61] [Path to Prior-Data Fitted Relational Models via Learned Latent Multi-table Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23073)
*Mohammad Sadeq Abolhasani, Viswanath Ganapathy*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Prior-data fitted networks (PFNs) show that pretraining on large populations of synthetic tasks can enable generalization to unseen datasets with little or no downstream fine-tuning. This paradigm is effective for transformer-based tabular and relational foundation models such as RDB-PFN, but remains largely unexplored for graph-based relational models built on message passing. We pretrain the graph-based relational foundation model Griffin on large collections of synthetic relational databases and ask whether curriculum learning, which benefits transformer-based relational learners, also improves message-passing representations. Rather than fine-tune on downstream tasks, we use the pretrained encoder as a general-purpose relational representation learner and pass its latent representations to a frozen in-context predictor (\ticl{}); no component is trained on the target task. On 16 real-world relational classification tasks, evaluated over five seeds against raw-feature and untrained-encoder baselines, a purely synthetic curriculum that begins with schema-guided data and continues with schema-agnostic data gives the strongest frozen in-context performance\rev{, outperforming real-data-only pretraining and every unordered single-stage mixture of the corpora; when appended after real-data pretraining, it yields the best encoders overall}. These results provide initial evidence that PFN-style synthetic pretraining transfers to graph-based relational foundation models.


### [62] [Where Latents Belong and How They Flow: Joint Identification of Structural Roles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23074)
*Xintong Li, Kun Zhang, Yujia Zheng*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Latent factors in dynamical systems can play different roles across observations and time. For example, an idea in a group meeting may be expressed by only some participants, inherit information from earlier discussions, and shape what is said next. We formalize this notion as the structural role of a latent factor, describing where it is expressed, whether it receives influence from the past, and whether it transmits influence to the future. The main challenge is correspondence, since the three structures may be identified under different permutations, while a structural role is defined only when they are aligned to the same latent coordinate. We show that, under suitable structural and variability conditions, all three roles are jointly identifiable under a common permutation at each time point. Guided by this theory, we develop RoleTrace, a framework that traces structural roles in a shared latent coordinate system. Synthetic experiments validate structural recovery, while experiments on multiparty meetings examine the identified roles in real sequential interactions.


### [63] [Towards a GraphNARX model for structural dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23103)
*George Tsialiamanis, Keith Worden*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The current paper introduces the GraphNARX model, a graph-based nonlinear autoregressive exogenous model for structural dynamics. The model is built to account for the geometry of structures and to model dynamic behaviour as the interaction between elements of the structure. Similar to other types of NARX models, GraphNARX is used for system identification. Nodal attributes of the graph encode past displacements and nodal forces, and the output of the model is the next-timestep displacement. The graph formulation of the model allows it to generalise to structures of different dimensions and degrees of freedom. Moreover, because graph-learning is performed locally between the nodes, the GraphNARX model can also learn to infer displacements of nodes for which data are not available, i.e.\ to perform virtual sensing. The proposed model's performance is evaluated in a system-identification framework and a virtual-sensing framework, and achieves good accuracy and exhibits generalisation capabilities to structures of different sizes.


### [64] [Domain-specific language alignment of multimodal embeddings towards explainable predictions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23110)
*Gabrielė Tijūnaitytė, Thijs L. van der Plas, Mohamed-Bachir Belaid, Rob Knapen, Maciej J. Soja, Ioannis N. Athanasiadis*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Multimodal data are commonly fused into latent representations that improve downstream task performance but are not interpretable, limiting their application in domains that require explainable model decisions. Vision-language alignment has made image embeddings interpretable through language, but requires vast image-caption training data. However, captioned data are typically not available for multimodal embeddings, as relevant features cannot directly be observed and annotated from embeddings alone. Here, we propose a framework to language-align any embedding space that can be linked to interpretable auxiliary data. Captioning functions convert auxiliary data into partial captions; a soft contrastive loss weights pairs by the similarity of their properties that each caption describes; and a rank-based concept retrieval task evaluates alignment against the auxiliary data rather than the captions. We apply this framework to multimodal Earth embeddings to facilitate explainable predictions for two species distribution modelling tasks: S2BMS and SatBird. AlphaEarth embeddings beat previous state-of-the-art models on 6/7 metrics, while our framework language-aligns domain-specific concepts of the embedding space, outperforming previous Earth observation vision-language models on average concept retrieval for both datasets. Finally, aligned concepts recover species-habitat associations ($\rho$=0.44) and relate habitat descriptions to species predictions, showing that abstract embeddings can be interrogated in the language of domain experts.


### [65] [LED: Improving Masked Diffusion Language Models by Lookahead Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23122)
*Yuyan Zhou, Zeyuan Yang, Yuxuan Song, Zhicheng Cai, Hongli Yu, Weiying Ma, Hao Zhou, James Kwok*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) have emerged as a promising alternative to autoregressive language models, enabling non-autoregressive text generation through parallel decoding. Existing dLLMs can be broadly categorized into two families: masked diffusion models (MDMs) and uniform diffusion models (UDMs) In this work, we theoretically show that MDMs are easier to optimize due to their mask-based transition schedule but have lower computational expressivity, whereas UDMs enable iterative state revision and greater expressivity but are substantially harder to train. This trade-off raises a natural question: can we obtain the expressivity of iterative revision without giving up the simple, easy-to-optimize transition schedule of MDMs? Motivated by this analysis, we propose Lookahead Editing (\ours{}), which augments masked diffusion decoding with a rewritable proposal state. At each decoding step, the model performs several lookahead updates to iteratively refine future predictions in this proposal state before committing tokens to the masked sequence. This design introduces a rewritable proposal that uses additional compute to improve expressivity while keeping the simple mask-based transition schedule of MDMs. We fine-tune Qwen2.5-7B and Qwen3-8B to obtain LED-\texttt{7B} and LED-\texttt{8B}, respectively. Across eight benchmarks, LED SFT yields average gains of $+11.4$ and $+7.7$ points over standard MDM SFT. Moreover, using SFT alone, LED-\texttt{8B} outperforms LLaDA2.1-16B Q-mode, which is trained with continued pre-training, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning.


### [66] [MetaCurate: Evolving Curation Filters for High-Quality Agentic Coding Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23134)
*Donghoon Kim, Seunghyuk Oh, Kevin Galim, Minseo Kim, Minjae Lee, Younghyun Kim, Wooseok Seo, Hyung Il Koo, Wonjun Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning on agent trajectories is an effective way to improve coding agents, but selecting useful training examples requires more than identifying successful runs. A resolved patch may follow inefficient reasoning or unnecessary tool use, while a failed run may still contain useful intermediate steps. Training pipelines therefore rely on rules and qualitative assessments to filter trajectories, yet designing these filters typically requires substantial human expertise, and validating improvements through repeated training and evaluation is expensive. We introduce MetaCurate, a training-free framework that evolves executable trajectory curation filters using pairwise LLM judgments. Disagreements between the filter's rankings and the judge's preferences provide targeted feedback for jointly revising its rules, rubric, and aggregation policy. The resulting filter scores trajectories independently, so pairwise comparisons are required only during search. Starting from a blank filter, MetaCurate autonomously discovers a curation strategy that achieves competitive downstream performance without task-specific handwritten criteria. We validate downstream utility through controlled SFT experiments on our uncurated trajectory pool and the Nemotron v3 SWE dataset, with random selection and hand-authored filtering as baselines. In our experiments, MetaCurate achieves the highest mean SWE-Verified score among the compared methods, including hand-authored rule-based filtering and a training-loop baseline whose search costs 3.5x as much.


### [67] [Local proxies for memory-efficient training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23145)
*Wu S. Zihan, Brian A. Yuan, Guillaume Bellec*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Local learning algorithms can reshape resource requirements of artificial intelligence. The memory bottleneck of standard end-to-end backpropagation lies in the need to store all network activations until the loss gradient has been propagated back through. Local learning avoids this, but typically at a cost in accuracy. We study how to achieve accurate and memory efficient local learning in transformers. For each transformer block, we train a local proxy network to predict the global network output via distillation. Gradients can be independently propagated through this local proxy while estimating well the backpropagation gradients. In theory, this approach is exact for deep linear networks; in non-linear residual models, the gradient error can be bounded when using the appropriate residual proxy. We verify this numerically and demonstrate that optimizing the distillation loss improves gradient estimation. Evaluating the performance of proxy propagation on CIFAR-100 in vision transformers, we find that it achieves a better accuracy/memory trade-off compared to other local algorithms. Finally, we show that the method is further relevant for fine-tuning large language models on a small memory budget. Our method trains faster and achieves higher performance than memory-efficient zeroth-order learning algorithms with comparable memory cost.


### [68] [Grounded Credit Assignment in Reinforcement Learning for Multi-Turn Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23148)
*Qirui Liu, Hao Chen, Yi Wen, Weijie Shi, Yao Zhao, Xianhe Chen, Hao Zhao, Xing Tang, Weihong Luo, Yuyu Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Outcome-reward reinforcement learning such as GRPO gives every token of a search-agent rollout the same advantage, although the rollout exercises two capabilities: finding evidence and using it. We show that accuracy improves without grounding: Search-R1 raises accuracy on all seven QA benchmarks, yet on Bamboogle the evidence-supported share of correct answers falls from 84.0% to 42.9%. We attribute this to GRPO's credit assignment: rollouts that retrieve answer-bearing evidence are favoured in aggregate only in proportion to their accuracy gain over the rest, a grounding gap that answering from memory or misusing evidence shrinks or reverses. To address this, we propose Grounded Credit Assignment (GCA), which uses verifiable sub-outcomes (evidence retrieval and answer correctness) to correct search and answer credit separately: a wrong answer is blamed more than the search that found the evidence, and a correct answer that bypasses retrievable evidence is credited less. Never negative on evidence-finding search, the correction does not shrink with the grounding gap; capped at 10% of each token's advantage, it never overrides the outcome signal. Without extra models, rollouts, or training stages, GCA improves average EM over Search-R1 by 5.7 (Qwen2.5-3B) and 6.3 points (Qwen3-30B-A3B) and raises this share from 76.9% to 93.4%.


### [69] [Nearly Linear Private Learning of Littlestone Classes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23160)
*Shay Moran, Chao Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Differentially private learning aims to learn from data while protecting individual training examples. For binary classification, finite Littlestone dimension characterizes approximate private PAC learnability (Alon et al., JACM 2022). Understanding how this dimension controls private sample complexity remains a central question. For classes of Littlestone dimension $d$, the first general upper bound was doubly exponential in $d$ (Bun, Livni, and Moran, FOCS 2020). Subsequent work improved this to $\widetilde O(d^6)$ (Ghazi et al., STOC 2021) and then to $\widetilde O(d^5)$ (Lyu, STOC 2026). We prove a nearly linear upper bound of $O(d\log d)$. Our main algorithmic contribution is a structural exponential mechanism for data-dependent candidate sets equipped with a rank satisfying suitable stability conditions. For natural Littlestone classes such as subspace indicators, this rank has a simple geometric interpretation: it is the codimension. This yields a private PAC learner for subspaces of dimension at most $d$ over any field, with tight linear dependence on $d$ and no dependence on the ambient dimension, improving the quadratic dependence of Kaplan et al. (STOC 2025). Our second contribution is combinatorial: drawing on model-theoretic ideas, we construct a suitable rank for general Littlestone classes and establish the counting bounds required by the mechanism.


### [70] [Structural Affinity: An Algorithm for Coherent Cage-Based Shape Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23173)
*Aviraj Newatia, Shengan Yang, Hantao Zhong, Rika Antonova*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Cage-based deformation (CBD) parametrises 3D geometry through a small set of control handles, effectively enabling a variety of tasks from animation and interactive modelling to tool-based co-design, neural deformation transfer, and aerodynamic shape optimization. Many optimization approaches relying on CBD employ regularisation strategies to reduce the distortion of the deformed mesh by encouraging smooth, regular cage deformations. These techniques offer soft guidance to an optimization algorithm towards desirable deformations, but compete with the task objective rather than changing the deformation parameterization itself. In this work we introduce Structural Affinity for CBD, an explicit regularisation method which enables smooth deformations through control cages by exploiting a two-cage structure to couple cage-handle motion directly. Structural Affinity precomputes an affinity matrix using mean-value coordinates, binding the inner control-cage to a coarser outer-cage. This matrix defines how much a cage handle displacement propagates to neighbouring handles which substantially decreases and often eliminates cage self-intersections, reduces mesh distortion, and adds linear overhead at deformation time. Across robotic control--morphology co-design, source-to-target neural deformation transfer, and aerodynamic wing shape optimization, Structural Affinity produces more regular deformations and substantially reduces reliance on hand-designed structural loss terms. We report consistent gains on three complementary diagnostics: the cotangent Laplacian, a proxy for surface smoothness; cage distortion, capturing whether control handles move coherently; and surface roughness, which measures undesirable oscillation in wing geometry.


### [71] [Tree-Aware Posterior Distillation for Long-Horizon Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23181)
*Heming Zou, Qi Wang, Yixiu Mao, Yun Qu, Yuyi Guo, Zeyuan Liu, Chenglin Li, Weijie Liu, Saiyong Yang, Xiangyang Ji*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards has become a scalable post-training paradigm for large language models. However, training agents over long-horizon interactions presents two challenges: using only terminal rewards makes turn-level credit assignment sparse and high-variance, while sequential mini-batch updates on a fixed rollout batch introduce policy staleness as the current policy drifts from the behavior policy. Existing approaches obtain denser credit via learned critics, process reward models, or on-policy distillation, but they rely on a value network, step-level annotations, or a stronger external teacher that increase training complexity. Meanwhile, importance sampling and clipping mitigate policy drift, but they do not specify a finite target that stops sequential updates from overshooting. In this work, we introduce TreeOPSD, a critic-free, tree-aware posterior self-distillation framework for long-horizon agentic reinforcement learning. Treating each interaction turn as a tree node, TreeOPSD branches alternative continuations from shared prefixes and recursively backs up terminal rewards into implicit turn-level credit. It then maps each credit to a closed-form, bounded teacher distribution parameterized by the likelihood ratio relative to the frozen behavior policy. Minimizing the divergence between this teacher and the student policy recovers the policy-gradient direction as the tangent at the behavior policy while bounding updates toward a finite target across sequential mini-batches. Empirically, TreeOPSD improves agentic capabilities across several competitive benchmarks and model scales, especially for long-horizon tasks, e.g., raising the average accuracy of Qwen3-30B-A3B-Thinking-2507 by 4.97 points over GRPO on the Deep Research setting.


### [72] [A Stronger Teacher Within: Strengthening MoE Models via On-Policy Bidirectional Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23185)
*Haoyu Chen, Jun Bai, Yang Liu, Jianfei Zhang, Wenge Rong*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has substantially improved Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs, but its outcome-level rewards provide only sparse supervision. On-Policy Distillation offers dense token-level feedback, yet requires a stronger compatible teacher, while on-policy self-distillation relies on hand-designed privileged information. We observe that the MoE architecture itself offers a way to resolve this dilemma, as increasing the activation budget per token exposes latent expert capacity and yields a potentially stronger teacher within the model. However, naive expansion degrades performance because newly co-activated experts are not optimized to work together. We therefore propose MoEvolve, a bidirectional distillation framework following a recovery-then-improvement strategy: we first restore the activation-expanded high-$k$ model and further strengthen it by exploiting its additional active capacity, then reverse the direction of distillation to transfer the enhanced capabilities back into the original low-$k$ configuration, retaining its inference-time activation budget. Across diverse MoE models and benchmarks, MoEvolve improves the initial models by up to $22.2$ domain-average points and outperforms same-budget RLVR baselines by up to $6.5$ points. Notably, the final low-$k$ models often surpass their activation-expanded teachers.


### [73] [LangNCO: Compiling Natural-Language Constraints into Inference-Time Interventions for Neural Combinatorial Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23190)
*Hironori Ohigashi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Neural combinatorial optimization (NCO) rapidly produces high-quality solutions for problem structures encountered during training, but operational requirements may change after deployment and be communicated in natural language. We propose LangNCO, a framework that compiles instance-specific requirements into structured inference-time interventions for a frozen NCO solver, without changing the pretrained policy's architecture, learned input representation, or parameters. A pretrained LLM serves as a replaceable semantic front end, translating each requirement into a schema-validated domain-specific language (DSL) rule set. A deterministic Rule Executor applies the rules as nonexpansive hard action masks. Because local masking prevents immediate violations but does not ensure that a complete feasible continuation remains reachable, Critical-Offset Decoding extracts rule-referenced critical nodes and reallocates part of a fixed rollout budget to multi-strength biased rollouts. Using controlled task-specific language to isolate solver adaptation from open-ended language robustness, we evaluate a CVRP policy on road closures, time windows, vehicle assignments, visit-order requirements, all pairwise combinations, and twelve additional constraint types. LangNCO achieves 100% feasibility on the four primary datasets with mean reference gaps of 1.63--3.26%, 97.33--100% feasibility when separately generated rules are composed for pairwise constraints, and 98.67% feasibility across the additional constraints. These results demonstrate modular post-training adaptation of a frozen NCO solver through inspectable language-derived rules, without retraining.


### [74] [Non-Parametric Optimization for Scalable Learning in Stochastic Decision Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23204)
*Mohsen Amidzadeh, Lauri Viitasaari, Mario Di Francesco*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Stochastic optimization (SO) plays a central role in decision-making under uncertainty. Among SO problems, time-varying stochastic optimization (TV-SO) is particularly important due to its applications in adaptive control and machine learning. Non-parametric approaches have been proposed for time-varying deterministic optimization; however, they have not been developed for their stochastic counterparts. This work addresses that gap by developing a stochastic variational framework based on Malliavin calculus. This framework yields non-parametric optimality conditions for SO problems with stochastic decisions and supports the design of a scalable deep-learning algorithm that is insensitive to the parameterization dimension. This algorithm, called the Stochastic Path Follower (SPF), is applied to two important problems under distribution drift, namely least-squares recovery and logistic regression. Experimental results show that the proposed approach outperforms state-of-the-art learning-based and gradient-based methods in both performance and scalability.


### [75] [Process Reward Modeling with Reliability- and Conservatism-Aware Bayesian Belief](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23223)
*Zhongjian Qiao, Zhixiang Zhou, Jiafei Lyu, Peisong Wang, Qiaosheng Zhang, Siyang Gao, Shuang Qiu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Process Reward Models (PRMs) play a central role in test-time scaling for large language model (LLM) reasoning. However, PRMs may provide inaccurate process rewards for test-time scaling, which we identify as arising from two sources of uncertainty: (1) \textit{PRM uncertainty} that arises from insufficient supervision data and imperfect PRM training; and (2) \textit{policy uncertainty} that results from the mismatch between the policy to collect supervision data and the policy for test-time scaling, leading to systematic reward overestimation. Previous studies fail to jointly consider both uncertainties. To bridge this gap, we propose BayesianPRM, which treats the process reward as a random variable within a reward hypothesis space and maintains a Bayesian belief over it. Since exact Bayesian inference over the continuous hypothesis space is generally intractable, we discretize the reward hypothesis space with a reward ensemble and introduce two complementary belief updates, each targeting one source of uncertainty: (1) to address PRM uncertainty, we introduce \textit{reliability-aware belief updating} that measures how well each ensemble member explains the supervision data and updates the belief with an amortized belief network optimized by the evidence lower bound (ELBO); and (2) to tackle policy uncertainty, we propose \textit{conservatism-aware belief calibration} which further reweights the belief toward lower rewards to suppress overestimation. These two updates yield a reliability- and conservatism-aware Bayesian belief, whose expectation determines the process reward. Experiments on multimodal reasoning benchmarks indicate that BayesianPRM consistently yields more accurate process reward estimates while enhancing test-time scaling performance.


### [76] [Response-Informed Value Estimation for Cost-Aware Multi-Turn LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23239)
*Haotian Yan, Jiaqi Liu, Bingyi Jing, Tianjun Yao*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Multi-turn LLM routing selects a model at every turn before its response is available, yet task success is revealed only when the interaction ends, leaving each intermediate choice without a label of its own. Logged trajectories, however, retain the response each call produced, which exposes what each turn required and how well the chosen model served it, evidence that outcomes alone hide. We introduce RIVET (Response-Informed Value Estimation for Turn-Level Routing), which exploits this post-call evidence during training while keeping routing strictly pre-call at deployment. RIVET jointly trains two value estimators over a shared state-model representation: a deployable Routing Value Estimator that scores candidate models from pre-call information, and a training-only Response-Informed Value Estimator that additionally observes the realized response. Conditioning on the response turns response-induced return variation from residual noise into an observed covariate, and we show that this reduces the leading asymptotic estimation error of the shared representation. On the MINT benchmark, RIVET solves more held-out tasks than the strongest multi-turn baseline while using 74.7% less total call cost.


### [77] [Anisotropic Representation Dynamics in Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23242)
*Nguyen Do, My T. Thai*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Small differences between internal representations can shrink or grow as they pass through a Transformer layer, affecting how information is preserved and how sensitive the model is to perturbations. Yet it remains unclear when changes in attention weights can turn a representation difference that would otherwise shrink into one that grows. We develop a \textbf{Mixture-Gated Theory of Anisotropic Representation Dynamics} that separates self-attention into value transport, query gating, and key gating. Our analysis reveals how the geometry of attended representations constrains gating and how interactions between these mechanisms can reverse contraction induced by value transport. We formalize this phenomenon as gating-induced regime switching, where a representation difference contracts under fixed attention routing but expands under full attention. We derive a necessary and sufficient spectral criterion that certifies the existence or absence of switching and identifies switching directions without exhaustive search. Beyond existence, our theory characterizes maximum relative switching strength, certifies the severity of contraction that gating can overcome, and determines the largest dimension of a subspace in which every nonzero direction switches. Experiments across four Transformer architectures validate these predictions through analytical and finite-forward comparisons, revealing distinct and joint query--key gating mechanisms and nontrivial switching subspaces. Our results provide a mechanistic and spectral understanding of how attention routing can alter local representation dynamics.


### [78] [FLARE: Physics-Grounded Evaluation of Language-Model Agents for Feeder Restoration with Degraded Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23251)
*BHVSP Subrahmanyam, Neeraj Choudhary*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Restoring service after distribution-grid faults requires decisions from incomplete and conflicting information while respecting electrical constraints. Addressing this need, we introduce FLARE (Feeder-Level Agent Restoration Evaluation), a physics-grounded benchmark for post-isolation feeder restoration. FLARE con- tains 800 AC-feasible cases across five observation settings and allows agents to gather evidence, evaluate switches, and perform restoration actions under fixed operational limits. We evaluate five instruction-tuned models over 78,750 episodes, including topology, distributed-generation, and loading shifts. Mean normalized regret ranges from 0.082 to 0.148. Although every model has zero median regret, P95 regret ranges from 0.814 to 1.000, revealing an important failure tail. Shadow replay shows that 96.4% of 15,706 guard-rejected actions would have caused a physical violation. FLARE enables reproducible evaluation of language-model based restoration support while emphasizing the need for external safety checks.


### [79] [Sample, Mix, or Verify? Allocating Test-Time Compute under Imperfect Verifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23255)
*Srinivasan Iyengar*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: A common way to make language models and agents more accurate is to spend more compute at inference time: generate many candidate solutions and submit the one a verifier prefers. Success then requires that some candidate is correct and that the verifier picks it. The first grows predictably with the number of samples; the second does not, because real verifiers (unit tests, majority votes, LLM judges, reward models) accept wrong answers, reject right ones, and make correlated mistakes. With a fixed budget, a practitioner must decide how many samples to draw, which models to draw them from, and how much to spend on verification, and today these choices are made by rules of thumb. We introduce SPLIT, which makes them from a small labelled pilot. It rests on an exact formula for the probability of submitting a correct answer under accept/reject and top-score selection that accounts for varying task difficulty, blind spots shared across models, and correlated repeated verifier calls. SPLIT fits the formula by empirical Bayes and uses it to plan budgets on unseen tasks. Across seven benchmarks spanning math, science QA, code and software-engineering agents, 26 models, 175 agent systems and six kinds of real verifiers, all from public releases, SPLIT lands within 0.02-0.8 percentage points of the best allocation in hindsight, while every fixed rule we test loses at least 2.5 points in some setting. Regret depends only on how well SPLIT ranks configurations, and it stays near-optimal under compute, price and latency costs and online. The formula also explains when asking more LLM judges lowers precision. As a forecaster far beyond the pilot, SPLIT beats power-law fitting when the verifier caps success, and we report where it loses.


### [80] [ADT: All-Atom Diffusion Tokenizer for 3D Molecular Structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23265)
*Xuantuo Huang, Encheng Su, Aoran Wang, Pan Tan, Shengyong Xu, Yizhou Wang, Chen Tang, Shixiang Tang, Mingxiao Li*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Discrete structural tokenization provides a promising route to autoregressive 3D molecular generation. Yet, existing tokenizers face a trade-off between compact representation and all-atom reconstruction: atom-level tokens preserve atomic details but produce long sequences, while compact residue-level protein tokens typically omit side-chain geometry. To overcome this limitation, we propose All-atom Diffusion Tokenizer (ADT), which decouples token granularity from reconstruction resolution. ADT uses one structural token per atom for small molecules and one token per protein residue that jointly represents backbone and side-chain geometry. To mitigate geometric distortion introduced by quantization, we leverage the generative capacity of diffusion models to recover fine-grained atomic geometry beyond deterministic decoding from discrete tokens. Extensive experiments across small molecules and proteins demonstrate that ADT achieves strong reconstruction from compact discrete representations while enabling high-quality autoregressive generation of both small molecules and all-atom protein structures. These results establish compact structural tokens as an effective discrete interface for all-atom molecular reconstruction and generation.


### [81] [Beyond Marginal Influence: Interaction-Aware Memory Selection for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23266)
*BHVSP Subrahmanyam, Neeraj Choudhary*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: LLM-agent memory selection often uses retrieval rankings based on record relevance or individual influence. Such rankings cannot identify sufficient subsets under redundant or jointly necessary evidence. We study per-decision memory sufficiency: selecting a small subset that preserves full-memory behavior. To separate search error from model error, we introduce JointCoreBench, a benchmark with nine interaction families, six workflow domains, natural-language decisions, and exact sufficient-set oracles, and JointCore, a relevance cost-ordered group-deletion baseline that tests blocks before individual records. On 6,912 exact conditions, cost-ordered JointCore recovers a minimum-cost sufficient set in 100.0% of cases, reduces subset evaluations from 30.00 to 11.13 (62.9%), and achieves 87.8% active-memory savings. Across four 12B-70B model families and 432 tasks per model, its unweighted mean is 10.28 versus 16.00 model queries and 50.6% versus 43.1% minimum-cost recovery relative to cost aware singleton deletion. Both methods preserve full-memory behavior under the deterministic primary interface. Tests with physical deletion, UNKNOWN masks, PENDING sentinels, counterfactual values, and alternative labels identify interface dependent behavior. JointCore is an efficient benchmark baseline; the study does not establish deployment-ready memory compression.


### [82] [DeDiCa: A Scalable Deconfounding Discrete Causal Generative Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23272)
*Kunchangtai Liang, Adrián Javaloy, Antonio Vergari, Isabel Valera*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We introduce DeDiCa, a practical framework for efficient causal inference with discrete variables and hidden confounding that revisits classical probabilistic modeling through a tensorized parameterization of causal Bayesian networks, enabling tractable inference. Given a causal graph, DeDiCa is trained once on observational data by maximum likelihood, exactly marginalizing over hidden confounders, and can then estimate any (proxy-)identifiable interventional query. Despite this deliberately simple parameterization, DeDiCa matches state-of-the-art neural causal generative models in ATE estimation, while supporting memory-efficient CPU training and exact interventional inference in milliseconds. Compared with causal foundation models, DeDiCa scales more effectively with additional observational samples and achieves competitive accuracy at substantially lower inference time and memory. DeDiCa provides a tractable, accurate, and scalable approach to causal inference in discrete settings with hidden confounding.


### [83] [Data-Efficient Classifier Pretraining Through Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23289)
*Qinyu Li, Yee Whye Teh, Chris Holmes, Joel Jaskari*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Machine learning with limited data is susceptible to overfitting, especially with modern deep neural networks. Prior to the introduction of AlexNet, reconstruction-based pretraining with auto-encoder and denoising auto-encoder were found to be beneficial, partially due to the limited size of the training sets at the time. Motivated by these traditional approaches, we propose DiffPT, a simple diffusion modeling approach to pretrain a given classifier backbone for limited domain-specific training data. We experiment on time-series data, standard natural-image benchmarks, and medical imaging datasets, and contrast the diffusion approach to a multitude of modern and traditional un/self-supervised methods. While contrastive pretraining performs better on CIFAR-10 and Tiny ImageNet in low-data regimes, DiffPT is particularly effective on medical imaging and time-series tasks, where it outperforms self-supervised baselines across a range of low-data regimes. Our results suggest that DiffPT is a simple and effective approach for data-efficient classifier pretraining on different modalities.


### [84] [Untrained CNN and coefficient-space regularization for joint reconstruction in photoacoustic tomography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23315)
*Alfio Bozi, Anwesa Dey, Souvik Roy*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The joint reconstruction of the initial pressure and spatially varying sound speed in photoacoustic tomography from boundary measurements is considered. A regularized data-driven framework is proposed in which the unknown fields are represented in finite-dimensional wavelet spaces and fixed nonlinear features generated by an untrained convolutional neural network (CNN) are used to reconstruct the initial-pressure coefficients through Tikhonov-regularized least squares. A learned coefficient-space prior is subsequently used to infer the squared sound-speed coefficients. Numerical experiments demonstrate accurate reconstruction on held-out data and strong generalization to previously unseen phantom geometries. The frozen reconstruction map also remains stable under measurement noise not represented during training, with only minor changes in reconstruction accuracy for noise levels up to $10\%$. In comparison with the supervised trained-CNN approach considered in this work, the untrained representation yields substantially smaller reconstruction errors while requiring approximately one-sixth of the computational time. These results demonstrate that fixed random convolutional features combined with regularized coefficient-space learning provide an efficient and robust approach to joint reconstruction in photoacoustic tomography.


### [85] [Peak-Memory Schedules You Can Trust: Exact Realization, Certified Optimality, and the Limits of Recomputation for Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23328)
*Ivan Fomin, Ivan Koryabkin, Boris Sheludko*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Real-time inference on small devices needs low activation memory without extra latency, which makes operator order the most valuable memory lever: it is free in time. We show that the usual way of deploying a better order, handing it back to the compiler and regenerating code, is unreliable. In PyTorch Inductor the regenerated program misses its predicted peak in 22 of 54 CPU configurations. It is worse than the stock program in 9 of them and in 19 of 50 on a GPU, and on the GPU it also changes numerical results. We instead profile the generated program, model its memory exactly from its own statements and their read/write effects, and permute those statements. We prove that this realizes the modeled peak whenever allocation does not depend on history. It does so to the byte, with bitwise-identical results, in all our CPU runs. On the GPU we characterize why the caching allocator breaks the condition. To know when to stop, we give forward and reverse "trap" lower bounds, each computable with $O(n^2)$ minimum cuts. They certify the found order optimal for 94% of held-out wrapper graphs and all 16 microcontroller-class models we study, in under a second. So most inference graphs are already scheduled optimally. Branching networks are the exception, where reordering is free and pays, e.g. 10.0% on DeepLabV3. In compiled training we save up to 7.6%. Beyond the certified optimum we study selective recomputation with per-operator caps. It is strongly NP-hard for every fixed cap even at treewidth two, encodes knapsack exactly on series-parallel graphs, and is solvable exactly on bounded treewidth. It is useless on public tiny models whose peak is a single layer, but on architectures that hold a cheap value for a late reuse it pays: on the public ReDimNet speaker networks, whose order is already optimal, it lowers the peak by 5.2-6.1% at under 0.4% extra MACs, with bitwise-identical outputs.


### [86] [HiPredict: Predicting the missing HiRISE RED4 spectral channel with machine learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23335)
*Ardan Suphi, Ben Moseley, Gareth S Collins, Valentin T Bickel, Vidhya G Rangarajan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The High Resolution Imaging Science Experiment (HiRISE) onboard the Mars Reconnaissance Orbiter (MRO) acquires imagery of Mars at 25--30 cm/pixel, supporting studies of martian geology, climate history, and landing-site characterization. Its 14-detector focal plane assembly includes several overlapping detectors that enable the acquisition of three-channel colour-infrared images, which are important for identifying compositional variations and studying active surface processes. In 2023, a detector in the center of the focal plane assembly (RED4) began failing intermittently, creating a gap in acquired imagery and reducing the instrument's colour-imaging capability by half. The HiRISE processing pipeline provides a synthetic red (SYN4) channel obtained by linearly scaling the co-located infrared (IR10) channel. However, this approach cannot capture observed non-linear relationships between the colour channels, limiting its accuracy and applicability. Here, we present HiPredict, a machine-learning framework that reconstructs RED4 from co-located IR10 and blue-green (BG12) imagery, neighbouring red-channel observations, and acquisition metadata. The model employs a U-Net architecture to learn non-linear spatiotemporal-spectral relationships between these inputs and is trained on 81,717 training samples, representing 13,625 observations. Evaluated on 23,348 test samples, representing 3,892 pre-failure observations, HiPredict reconstructs RED4 with a Normalised Mean Absolute Error of 1.1%, substantially outperforming SYN4 (3.1%). The reconstructed products improve colour image recovery and more closely represent features of interest such as icy craters, stratigraphy exposed along ridge crests and chloride deposits. We release HiPredict as an open software tool. This work demonstrates the potential of machine learning to mitigate data loss as planetary missions age and naturally degrade.


### [87] [Minimax-optimal Scaling Laws in Ridge Regression via Transfer Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23337)
*Yalda Shabanzadeh, Diyuan Wu, Marco Mondelli*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Pretrained models are often reused across downstream tasks, motivating the question of whether this transfer learning procedure can provide a substantial performance improvement, namely, in terms of the scaling law. We address this question in the prototypical setting of high-dimensional regression, where (i) a pretrained \emph{source} model is trained via ridge regression, and (ii) a fine-tuned \emph{target} model is regularized via the $L_2$ distance from the source estimator. First, we establish a deterministic equivalent for the risk of this two-step procedure under both model and covariance shift. Next, by specializing the deterministic equivalent to a shared regression function and commuting power-law covariance spectra, we identify a regime where the resulting scaling law achieves the minimax-optimal exponent. Notably, in this regime, the optimal source regularization depends on source data only, indicating its universality across all targets. Furthermore, we show that the same optimal rate is achieved by performing the fine-tuning step via one-pass stochastic gradient descent. We support our theoretical analysis via experiments on both synthetic settings and language models.


### [88] [SETAREH Transform: A Sorting-Assisted Haar-Wavelet Sparsification Method for Approximate Inner-Product Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23338)
*Seyed Kamaledin Setarehdan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Modern deep learning systems increasingly rely on high-dimensional vector operations, in which large input and parameter vectors are repeatedly stored, transferred, and processed. Although substantial research has focused on reducing neural-network model size through pruning, quantization, knowledge distillation, and efficient architectures, the storage, memory-traffic, and communication costs associated with repeatedly presented high-dimensional input vectors remain important practical considerations, particularly in distributed and edge-computing environments. This paper proposes the SETAREH (Sorting-assisted Energy-concentrating Transform for Approximate Representation using Haar wavelets) transform, a Haar-wavelet-based sparsification approach for approximating the inner product between a fixed high-dimensional coefficient vector and successive input vectors. By applying the same sequence of permutations and Haar transforms to both vectors, SETAREH preserves the exact inner product before truncation. It then retains only a subset of the transformed coefficient-input pairs, reducing their representation cost while introducing a controllable approximation error. To evaluate SETAREH, we consider two complementary experimental settings: a synthetic Gaussian-vector baseline and a real apparent-age estimation task using a convolutional neural network on the APPA-REAL dataset. The results show that SETAREH can approximate the evaluated inner products using substantially fewer transformed coefficient-input pairs than the original vector dimension. The findings support its use as a potential structured data-representation method for repeated inner products with fixed weights, while the practical benefit depends on approximation accuracy and the cost of storing or transmitting permutation metadata.


### [89] [How Finite-Rank Bottlenecks Shape the Low-Rank Adaptation Landscape](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23343)
*Long Nguyen-Chi, Quynh Nguyen, Thanh Nguyen-Cung, Binh T. Nguyen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) has become the standard method for parameter-efficient fine-tuning of large pretrained models, yet theoretical explanations for why low-rank updates suffice remain incomplete. Most existing analyses rely on the Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK) regime, which linearizes the network around the pretrained weights. In this work, we take a different approach that requires no linearization: our key observation is that, once the backbone is frozen, the loss sees each adapted weight matrix only through a finite-rank linear map induced by the frozen activations. Trace-norm regularization then forces the optimizer to pick a low-rank matrix among all matrices that produce the same observed output. This yields global minimizers whose ranks are controlled by the rank of this map, which we call the \emph{bottleneck rank}, rather than the sample size. For multi-head attention, we obtain tighter rank upper bounds by exploiting two additional invariances: row-wise softmax is insensitive to additive row constants in the score matrix, and value updates are observable only after attention weighting and frozen output projection. For the factorized LoRA objective with weight decay, we prove a complementary result on the nonconvex side: after an arbitrarily small generic positive semidefinite perturbation, every first-order stationary point is rank-deficient once the LoRA rank exceeds a threshold determined by the bottleneck rank. Finally, we show that global minimizers of the perturbed objective are near-optimal for the original trace-norm problem, and we provide uniform generalization bounds governed by the spectral structure of the frozen features.


### [90] [RTSM: Adapting Recursive Models to Multivariate Time-Series Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23353)
*Georg Tenzing, Anna Tegon, Luca Benini, Thorir Mar Ingolfsson*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Accurate multivariate time-series classification requires extracting temporal features and integrating information across a sequence. Weight-tied refinement offers a way to repeatedly process these features without allocating new parameters at each stage. Applying this principle to time series requires choosing both a temporal representation and a latent-state organization suited to classification. We introduce the Recursive Time-Series Model (RTSM), which combines a learned convolutional temporal tokenizer, single-state weight-tied refinement, and a pooled classification readout. Across all 30 UEA datasets, RTSM achieves 72.81% mean accuracy under validation-based configuration and checkpoint selection, the highest observed mean among the 17 evaluated deep-learning models and 1.60 percentage points above MambaSL. Ablations on eight datasets favor the proposed tokenizer over a simple convolutional embedding and the single-state model over the tested two-state variants. The complete classifier reaches 86.10% mean accuracy versus 84.79% for a separately trained tokenizer-only classifier. These findings identify temporal encoding and state organization as key design choices for adapting recursive architectures to time series. The resulting classifier combines competitive accuracy with a shared refinement module whose parameter count is independent of computational depth.


### [91] [Forecasting Voltage Time Series under Anomalies Using a WGAN Model With Lipschitz Control and Progressive Kernel Critic Strategy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23394)
*Najmieh Sadat Safarabadi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In time-series forecasting tasks, the use of recursive forecasting on high-dimensional data substantially increases the complexity of the prediction process. The main challenge is that long-term dynamics becomes harder to model, since the sequences show greater temporal variability. To counteract this limitation and improve the modeling of long-term dynamics, and more importantly, address high-dimensional recursive forecasting, this paper uses a Wasserstein GAN equipped with a progressive Lipschitz penalty and an adaptive kernel. This model is capable of taking a window of the data stream and forecasting the next values, subsequently reconstructing the time-series stream and dynamically adapting its kernel to each new window. The proopsed model is a deep learning framework based on a Wasserstein GAN (WGAN-CLP) with an adaptive kernel to model alterations in streaming voltage time series. To establish the methodological foundation, we review the WGAN-LP formulation another variant model in this class and extend its framework through an adaptive kernel critic model. We then evaluate this extended model using power ratios, correlation coefficient (CC), and relative root mean squared error (RRMSE). The experimental results demonstrate that the proposed model outperforms benchmark methods in both accuracy and robustness. The evaluation shows promising performance of the WGAN-CLP with an adaptive kernel framework in forecasting sequence alterations across the voltage and energy time-series datasets.


### [92] [Contracting with an Agent under Model Mismatch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23402)
*Elif Yılmaz, Jakub Tłuczek, Victor Villin, Christos Dimitrakakis*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In the classical principal-agent setting, the principal delegates some task to the agent, who receives a payment conditioned on the outcome of the task, which in turn benefits the principal. To align their objectives, the principal designs a contract that incentivizes the agent to take actions beneficial to the principal. In practice, however, the agent may act under misspecified beliefs about the underlying environment, leading to systematically suboptimal decisions. In this work, we study this problem through a principal-agent Markov Decision Process (PAMDP), in which the principal repeatedly interacts with an agent whose transition model may differ from the true environment dynamics. The principal has access to the true transition model and designs contracts that account for the agent's belief and incentives. We characterize contract design under model mismatch and develop a framework that enables the principal to induce desirable long-term benefits even though the agent has a potentially inaccurate understanding of the environment.


### [93] [Information Estimation with Discrete Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23404)
*Alberto Foresti, Giulio Franzese, Pietro Michiardi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Information-theoretic measures, such as Mutual Information (MI), play a crucial role in understanding non-linear relationships between random variables and are widely used across scientific disciplines. Yet, their use on real-world discrete data remains challenging. Existing methods typically rely on embedding discrete data into a continuous space and apply neural estimators originally designed for continuous distributions. This process requires careful engineering for both the embedding model and estimator architecture, but suffers from issues related to high data dimensionality. In this work, we introduce InfoSEDD, a discrete diffusion-based approach that bridges information-theoretic estimation and generative modeling such that they can be used to compute Kullback-Leibler divergences. Backed by Continuous Time Markov Chains theory principles, the design of InfoSEDD is lightweight and scalable and allows seamless integration with pretrained models. We showcase the versatility of our approach through applications on motif discovery in genetic promoter data, semantic-aware model selection in text summarization, and entropy estimation in Ising models. Finally, we construct consistency tests on real-world textual and genomics data. Our experiments demonstrate that InfoSEDD outperforms alternatives that rely on the "embedding trick". Our results position InfoSEDD as a robust and scalable tool for information-theoretic analysis of discrete data.


### [94] [Residual Stochastic Concept Bottleneck Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23421)
*Stephen Marcou, Mateo Espinosa Zarlenga, Sonia Laguna, Moritz Vandenhirtz, Julia E. Vogt, Mateja Jamnik*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Concept-based models (CMs) learn human-interpretable intermediate representations that enable users to understand the model's reasoning and steer its predictions through test-time concept interventions. However, in realistic settings, their performance can be limited by concept incompleteness, where the training concepts fail to capture all task-relevant information. Adding an unconstrained residual pathway to the model's predictive process can overcome the consequences of incompleteness. However, this introduces an inscrutable, leak-prone prediction pathway that may weaken concept interventions, as these often leave residual variables unchanged. To address this limitation, we introduce Residual Stochastic Concept Bottleneck Models (Res-SCBMs), a family of concept-based models that jointly model concept and residual variables through a multivariate Gaussian distribution. Res-SCBMs learn a covariance structure that explicitly captures dependencies between the known concepts and learnt residual variables, allowing interventions to propagate to statistically dependent residual variables through Gaussian conditioning. This extends the intervention capabilities of previous CMs without requiring concept completeness during training. Our results show that, across multiple concept-incomplete benchmarks, Res-SCBMs improve predictive performance over competing baselines while retaining effective test-time concept interventions.


### [95] [Bridging Compressed Communication and Local Steps via Spectral Method in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23434)
*Dmitriy Bystrov, George Chirkov, Daniil Medyakov, Dmitry Bylinkin, Aleksandr Beznosikov*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) enables collaborative training of complex models across large-scale networks of edge devices while keeping data localized. However, data heterogeneity and high communication overhead remain fundamental bottlenecks. Recently, the spectral optimizer Muon has demonstrated remarkable advantages over Adam-like methods in centralized training. While recent efforts have attempted to transition Muon to FL, naive integrations into traditional architectures fail short. Crucially, existing approaches do not support the the communication compression and local steps --- the two foundational techniques of efficient FL. To address this limitation, we introduce a novel spectral FL method that utilizes both local updates and compression. We establish rigorous convergence guarantees under mild assumptions, notably eliminating the restrictive bounded heterogeneity condition. Empirical evaluation on standard image classification benchmarks validates the efficiency and robustness of our approach.


### [96] [RECREG: Recovery-based Shielding for Regular Safety Properties in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23445)
*Alexander W. Goodall, Francesco Belardinelli*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In many real-world scenarios we need to consider formal requirements, such as safety and constraint compliance, to deploy Reinforcement Learning (RL) agents. In this paper we introduce RecReg, a safe RL framework capable of enforcing non-Markovian regular safety properties via recovery-based shielding. RecReg is a unified approach that supports different levels of a priori knowledge and safety guarantees. The paper primarily focuses on building the theoretical foundations for RecReg in the tabular setting, providing rigorous safety and convergence guarantees that are not present in related work. We provide an extensive set of empirical results using the MASA-Safe-RL library, and we outline an extension of RecReg to deep RL domains using SOTA model-based RL. Our empirical results demonstrate that RecReg achieves efficient learning and matches the theory in this paper.


### [97] [Regularized free deconvolution for spectral repair of resampling distortion in high-dimensional particle filters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23448)
*Deming Song, Chi-Kin Lam, Kefeng Li, Hui Yu, Xilin Li, Huagang Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Resampling is the standard remedy for weight degeneracy in particle filters, but in high dimensions it distorts the spectrum of the ensemble covariance in a way that is computable from the importance weights.
