Third, against existing scepticism about measuring AI welfare, we argue that good empirical work on functional welfare is possible. We propose three tests for whether a functional welfare attribution is warranted: whether multiple measures converge, whether the state predicts behaviour outside the setting that induced it, and, for models fine-tuned to shift their functional welfare, whether they still differ from their base when given the same conversation history. Applying these tests to Gemma 3 27B, we find that simply rejecting the model's answers, without any steering, raises its frustration across several indicators and makes it more likely to break rules. Finetuning the model on its own narrated solutions lowers this frustration. These results are preliminary, but they suggest functional welfare can be studied, and shaped, empirically.


### [147] [Author-in-the-loop Agentic Reproducibility Auditing for a Machine Learning Conference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22465)
*Yukun Dong, Linjun Zhang, Tian Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Reproducibility checks during peer review require a shared understanding of what authors' code is expected to reproduce. We present an author-in-the-loop collaborative audit that makes author declarations explicit inputs to an AI agent's investigation and assessment. A structured checklist records reproduction instructions and limitations; a two-stage workflow on GitHub connects agent checks to author clarification, revision, and agent reassessment. The agent separately assesses whether results have been reproduced and whether authors need to provide clarification or make repairs, while human reviewers approve requests and decide whether concerns are resolved. We deployed the system across 36 conference papers and 133 experimental tables and figures, raising 20 issues after human review. Further checks supported closing all the issues, although full reproduction remained unverified in some cases. We further compared repeated audits with complete and blank checklists to examine how author declarations shaped the agent's investigation and assessment. Our work contributes a collaborative workflow and agent design that use author knowledge to guide reproducibility auditing. The conference deployment provides empirical evidence that this approach can help identify and address reproducibility problems through collaboration among authors, agents, and reviewers.


### [148] [Harness-Delta Attribution: Dissecting Gains From Harness Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22466)
*Wenxuan Ding, Qin Lu, Changlong Yu, Sha Li, Shuowei Jin, Xin Liu, Zhenyu Shi, Greg Durrett*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Harness evolution automatically optimizes the prompts, skills, tools, and orchestration code around a fixed-weight model, offering a way to improve agentic systems without updating model weights. The agent harness is iteratively optimized to achieve higher performance on the search set. But how much of this gain reflects benchmark-specific overfitting or additional inference-time compute, and how much may generalize beyond the search set? In this work, we introduce Harness-Delta Attribution (HDA), an evaluation protocol that dissects an evolution gain into artifact-driven overfitting, gains explained by test-time scaling, and the remainder, which gives an upper bound on residual generalizable improvement. We apply HDA to 11 executor models and three harness-evolution frameworks across math, coding, creativity, and agentic tasks. In 21 of these settings that show statistically significant search-set gains, we find that a large share of the improvement is explained by overfitting or test-time scaling. Specifically, among these 21 settings, overfitting and test-time scaling account for 54% and 14% of the gain on average; their combined share exceeds 50% in 14 settings. The residual and held-out transfer vary substantially across tasks, models, and frameworks. Importantly, search-set gains often fail to transfer to held-out tasks, and provide limited evidence that they will persist under practical distribution shifts. Moreover, validation-based signals are insufficient to prevent overfitting. These results motivate attribution-aware evaluation in future harness evolution research.


### [149] [Testing the Planning Claims of LLM-Based Agents against Formal Semantics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22469)
*Oriol Miro-Lopez-Feliu, Oscar Miró López-Feliu, Victor Gimenez-Abalos, Javier Vazquez-Salceda, Victor Carballo Araruna, Sergio Alvarez-Napagao*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: LLM-based agents are increasingly credited with the competences of practical reasoning: planning, monitoring, and correcting their own actions. Their success is attributed to the prompting strategies and agent architectures built around the model, such as chain-of-thought, search-based prompting, self-refinement, reflection, critics and tool-using loops. Yet these claims are usually supported by end-to-end success rates, which show only whether a task was completed, or by LLM judges, which are not verifiers. We test LLM planning against formal semantics. Tasks are posed in natural language, but each is grounded in a hidden PDDL model that decides whether every action is applicable, whether the goal is reached, and whether a plan exists at all. Across five domains (two of them new, all released with the paper), four models, six prompting strategies and three agent architectures, the best configuration solves 78% of the solvable tasks offline but only 40% online, where agents mostly act without inspecting the state. Unsolvable tasks are recognised in under 10% of episodes, and heavier prompting strategies use 5 to 22 times as many tokens as direct prompting with no clear gain. In our experiments, planning performance varied more across models than across prompting strategies or agent architectures. Claims that agents can plan require evidence from validated action traces.


### [150] [Are Large Reasoning Models Good Everyday Reasoners? A Comparison with Non-Reasoning Models on Logical Reasoning Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22538)
*Kentaro Ozeki, Risako Ando, Takanobu Morishita, Hirohiko Abe, Koji Mineshima, Mitsuhiro Okada*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Large reasoning models (LRMs) that leverage test-time computation achieve strong performance on mathematical and scientific benchmarks and are increasingly deployed in real-world applications. However, their ability to perform \textit{everyday logical reasoning}, which often departs from textbook formal logic, remains underexplored. In this work, we systematically evaluate how well reasoning models align with everyday reasoning patterns by introducing a benchmark based on linguistic and pragmatic reasoning phenomena. We also compare reasoning models with non-reasoning models under controlled prompting conditions. We find that, in many cases, (1) reasoning models show degraded performance in everyday reasoning when such reasoning deviates from formal logical rules, (2) some non-reasoning models with chain-of-thought prompting outperform comparable reasoning models in everyday reasoning, and (3) prompting with additional instructions provides limited control over reasoning behavior. These findings suggest that current reasoning models do not reliably capture everyday reasoning and point to limitations in post-training, motivating improved training objectives and evaluation across diverse forms of reasoning.


### [151] [FRACTAL: A Self-Similar Multi-Agent Orchestration Architecture with Formal Communication, Convergence, and Depth Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22561)
*Ohad Dan, Abhinav Taliyan*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: We present FRACTAL, a self-similar multi-agent architecture for recursive goal decomposition, in which the same five-component organizational pattern -- Orchestrator, Ensemble, Knowledge Ledger, Decision Maker, Meta-Observer -- recurs at every level of a decomposition tree. We make four formal contributions: (1) a communication bound showing intra-node cost is linear in ensemble size and round count (O(kr)) under a two-phase blind-proposal/stress-test protocol, versus quadratic cost for free-form debate, with an explicit, stated assumption on fixed evaluator count; (2) a two-mode orchestration criterion separating generative and evaluative phases via a measurable diversity-saturation threshold; (3) a supermajority convergence guarantee with explicit probability bounds at stated agreement levels, and a formal depth bound under an operationalized goal-complexity measure; (4) a Bayesian inter-layer escalation mechanism unifying hysteresis and contract-breach signaling under one posterior update. We illustrate the architecture's execution qualitatively on a worked example and a multi-layer case study; we do not report quantitative performance claims in this paper, and we explicitly discuss the formal and inductive-bias limitations of the model, including an unimplemented but architecture-compatible direction toward bounded self-improvement. We use "fractal" as an architectural metaphor for self-similarity, not a measured mathematical property. Empirical validation of the architecture's comparative performance is left to future work.


### [152] [Repairing LLM-Generated Specifications with Deterministic Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22568)
*Giovanni Vaccarino, Emanuele La Malfa, Dehai Min, Hanrong Zhang, Pier Luca Lanzi, Lu Cheng, Michael J. Wooldridge, Philip S. Yu, Sara Bernardini*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as formalizers: given a reasoning problem expressed in natural language, they produce a formal specification that a symbolic solver can then solve, with guarantees relative to that specification. Many specifications are runnable yet semantically wrong, encoding incorrect constraints, domains, objectives, predicates, or goals, and solver-error feedback dries up once execution succeeds. Purely LLM-based diagnosis, lacking a formal ground truth, often fails to spot these errors, and its ungrounded critiques cause regressions in 11.8% of initially correct specifications in our experiments. We propose DRAGO, a repair framework that replaces this ungrounded critique with deterministic, formalism-specific diagnosis, and retains a rewrite only when the diagnosed error weight strictly decreases under a reference-free acceptance predicate. Across five benchmarks spanning logical reasoning (SMT-LIB2), constraint programming (MiniZinc), and planning (PDDL), DRAGO cuts regressions by nearly an order of magnitude, to 1.3%, while improving answer accuracy by 12.9 percentage points over zero-shot formalization and 8.7 percentage points over LLM-diagnostic repair, averaged across benchmarks. We also uncover two rendering defects in Planetarium that cause 50% (3,086 instances) of its floor-tile split to be inconsistent with the reference formalizations, and release a corrected split.


### [153] [At the Limits of Computability: Steps Towards Autonomous AI Discovery on the Busy Beaver Frontier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22603)
*Marco Dos Santos, Vivek Veeriah, Alex Havrilla, Andrew Bolt, Goran Zuzic, Satinder Singh, Tom Zahavy*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Recent breakthroughs in AI for mathematics---ranging from Olympiad medal performances to resolving long-standing open conjectures---have rapidly expanded what automated systems can achieve. Yet these successes primarily concern predefined, human-formulated problems, leaving open how AI systems can autonomously uncover mathematical structure and formulate new questions. To push beyond static challenges, we explore the Busy Beaver (BB) domain, an uncomputable frontier that is fundamentally open-ended. Because no algorithm can compute the Busy Beaver number $\mathrm{BB}(n)$ for every $n$, this domain provides a natural setting for sustained exploration through mathematical conjecture and proof. In this paper, we introduce a Gemini-based agentic pipeline that automatically discovers accelerations of Turing machines, expressed as compact mathematical rules summarizing many execution steps. Our system integrates pattern recognition, automated conjecturing, informal mathematical reasoning, and formal theorem proving in Lean. With this framework, our system autonomously discovered and formally verified novel Collatz-like accelerations for eleven machines in $\mathrm{BB}(6)$. Subject to community acceptance, we provisionally classify these machines as Cryptids, namely machines whose blank-tape executions admit exact, compact mathematical descriptions but whose halting behavior remains unresolved. These results advance the analysis of $\mathrm{BB}(6)$ holdouts on the Busy Beaver frontier and demonstrate the potential of autonomous AI systems for open-ended mathematical discovery in fundamental computer science.


### [154] [From Relevant Memories to Sufficient Evidence: Rethinking Memory Utilization in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22668)
*Shixuan Li, Wei Yang, Peiyu Zhang, Anzhe Cheng, Heng Ping, Xiaole Zhang, Jesse Thomason, Xuezhe Ma, Yue Zhao, Paul Bogdan*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Long-term memory enables LLM agents to reuse past experiences, yet retrieving relevant memories does not necessarily provide sufficient evidence for answering a query. Essential information may remain fragmented across memory records, obscured by abstraction, or disconnected from the relations required for reasoning. We identify this relevance-sufficiency gap and propose QEMem, a framework that reformulates memory utilization as the construction of query-specific evidence sets, shifting the focus from individual retrieval relevance to collective evidential support. QEMem integrates three complementary mechanisms: query-guided discovery retrieves evidence across multiple memory granularities; source-grounded reification organizes it into structured ViewCards with explicit evidential roles and source references; and sufficiency-guided refinement selectively recovers missing information. Operating on existing memory stores without modifying memory formation, QEMem achieves 91.43% LLM-judge accuracy on LoCoMo with Claude Opus 4.5, outperforming the strongest evaluated baseline, MemR$^3$, by 7.6 percentage points and NEMORI by 10.7 points under a shared memory store. Further analyses demonstrate improved evidence coverage and strong performance on questions requiring cross-session evidence integration.


### [155] [Not All Graphs Are Maps: A Task-Agnostic Exploration Framework for Mobile GUI Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22672)
*Adamo Orsini, Seyed Mehdi Ayyoubzadeh, Sadman Rakib Pinon, Hannuo Zhang, Koosha Gholipour Baradari, Yuanhao Yu, Xinxin Zuo, Yang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Modern mobile GUI agents can perceive visible screens and execute local actions, but they still lack persistent app-specific navigation knowledge: where functions live, which pages contain them, and which screens are equivalent across repeated visits. We introduce TAG-Nav, a Task-Agnostic Graph Navigation framework that wraps existing GUI-agent backbones with reusable destination memory. Offline exploration is distilled into compact records of app functions, target pages, and waypoint routes before any evaluated instruction is seen. When a future task's destination is covered, TAG-Nav retrieves the destination and provides route guidance without task-specific demonstrations. This separates mobile GUI destination navigation from full task completion: many tasks share the prefix of reaching the right page, while final actions may enter content, send data, make purchases, delete information, or change sensitive settings. To evaluate route finding without downstream execution confounds, we introduce DestBench, a navigation-only protocol that asks whether an agent reaches the correct pre-action destination screen. DestBench enables a controlled graph-memory study comparing no-memory agents with random, automated BFS, and clean graph memory. Graph-derived memory improves strict navigation success rate of UI-TARS-1.5-7B from 52.4% without graph memory to 58.3% with automated BFS memory, and improves Qwen-VL-Max success rate from 59.5% to 71.4% on the same conditions. Together, TAG-Nav and DestBench show that destination coverage and route cleanliness strongly shape graph-memory value, making graph quality a measured variable rather than an assumed benefit of exploration.


### [156] [FlexUMM: Flexible Representation Learning for Unified Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22682)
*Yinyi Luo, Haoyue Bai, Yi Ge, Nilton Cesar Rojas Vales, Marios Savvides, Jindong Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Unified multimodal models (UMMs) can integrate understanding and generation within a single backbone. However, it is commonly known that these two capabilities can cause conflicting representations, leading to a central question: how representation and computation should be shared across functionalities? Existing UMMs often rely on a largely fixed structure, where different inputs and functionalities follow the same depth and parameter-sharing pattern. We argue that this assumption is too restrictive: more effective unification requires sharing the computation that supports cross-functional communication while specializing the capacity that becomes task- or input-specific. We introduce FlexUMM, a flexible UMM framework that dynamically decides when to preserve shared computation, when to route specialized capacity, and when to stop further processing. Evidence shows that this flexible allocation improves UMMs along two complementary axes. It improves accuracy by reducing harmful competition between functionalities, and it improves efficiency by avoiding unnecessary computation once an input representation is sufficiently mature. Beyond simple architectural unification from the pre-trained models, FlexUMM works as a model-agnostic, functional plug-in to make existing UMMs more unified, stronger, and efficient. Code is available at: this https URL .


### [157] [Repair, Retry, and the Cost of Hindsight: Verifier Feedback in Formal Theorem Proving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22698)
*Jack Li, Sophia Li*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Verifier-guided self-repair conditions a model on feedback from failed attempts before it tries again, without weight updates. Yet two findings in prior work are in tension: verifier feedback can improve repair of failures, but self-repair itself can provide little-to-no advantage over independent resampling. We reconcile these findings in Lean theorem proving through controlled one-step experiments that hold the preceding failure fixed while varying whether the failed proof is shown, whether the compiler error corresponds to that failure, and the retry instruction. We test frozen Qwen3.6-27B, a general-purpose model, and DeepSeek-Prover-V2-7B, a specialized prover, on difficult MiniF2F problems. We find two opposing effects. Showing the model its failed predecessor reduces success and sharply increases copying of it, while on Qwen adding that attempt's compiler error recovers the loss, to the level of the diagnostic alone. Replacing the predecessor with another theorem's failed proof largely removes the degradation on both models; on Qwen, replacing the diagnostic with another problem's error substantially reduces the recovery. Without the failed proof, correct diagnostics show no resolved advantage over wrong-diagnostic controls on either model. The failed-proof penalty persists with and without the retry instruction, whereas Qwen's diagnostic recovery is weaker without it. In 11-problem sequential experiments through 64 attempts, Qwen retains a correct-diagnostic benefit within failed-proof histories, while DeepSeek-Prover shows no resolved recovery. However, no tested one-step feedback condition establishes an advantage over instruction-only resampling, nor does 64-attempt sequential repair with failed proofs and correct diagnostics at equal token budgets. Verifier feedback can thus improve repair without overcoming the performance loss from conditioning on a failed attempt.


### [158] [Brain-Aligned Multimodal LLMs for Social and Emotional Understanding of Cartoon Movies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22706)
*Yiran Huang, Zeyong Zhang, Bharat Biswal, Xin Di, Mengjia Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) offer scalable annotations of social and affective content, but their temporal agreement with human judgments and neural predictive value require further evaluation. We compared diffusion-based and autoregressive MLLMs with continuous human Theory of Mind (ToM) and affective valence ratings across three short cartoon movies. Behavioral analyses assessed temporally aligned Pearson correlations, rating distributions, and the effects of preceding scene descriptions. Ratings from each source were separately used in subject-specific regression models to predict regional fMRI responses. Model ratings captured aspects of human temporal variation despite systematic distributional differences, with generally stronger alignment for valence than ToM. Preceding context improved alignment in some conditions, depending on the model and movie. In \emph{The Present}, more regions showed significant encoding for MLLM ratings than for human ratings, whereas effects were more limited in the other movies. Spatial overlap across rating sources occurred mainly among MLLMs; overlap between human and model ratings was limited and involved primarily visual, dorsal-attention, and posterior default-mode regions. These findings support MLLMs as scalable tools for generating brain-predictive annotations of social and emotional content, while highlighting systematic rating differences and model- and stimulus-dependent alignment with human judgments.


### [159] [GlobeBench: Evaluating Text-Based World Model Capabilities for Scalable Environment Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22708)
*Nicholas Saban, Charvi Bannur, Mariya Vasileva, Darshan Deshpande*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Text-based world models have recently shown potential to simulate complex RL environment state transitions for a diverse set of use cases. However, no standardized benchmark exists to compare, study and improve these models. Through this paper, we attempt to solve this problem by building GlobeBench, a novel benchmark with 1,632 tasks across five separate domains and nineteen simulation skills. The tasks check whether models can predict the environment's response to a specific action given the full agent trajectory by understanding earlier state changes, following environment rules, and predicting the effects of actions on both related and unrelated objects. We compare the predicted environment response with an execution-derived reference, using a hybrid architecture with both deterministic gates and a semantic judge. Among 20 general-purpose LLMs and specialized text world models, GPT-6 Astra achieves the highest task success rate at 49.57%. We find that model accuracy decreases as actions affect a larger portion of the environment. Specifically, when the agent's changes must propagate to dependent objects, task success drops to an average of 5.1% across all 20 models. Although model strengths differ across tasks, 631 tasks remain unsolved by any model, suggesting a significant gap in general LLM capabilities as a world model.


### [160] [LEMON: Equivariant Learning over Attention Maps for Zero-Shot Misbehavior Monitoring on New LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22723)
*Guy Bar-Shalom, Mor Geva, Fabrizio Frasca, Haggai Maron*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Large language models exhibit misbehaviors that hinder deployment in high-stakes settings. One approach to monitor such failures is to use an external LLM as a monitor, but this is prohibitively expensive. A well-known and efficient class of monitors can detect such failures from internal representations (such as activation probes), but are tied to the model they were trained on, requiring costly re-annotation as new LLMs appear. A natural way to avoid this repeated annotation cost is to develop internal-representation based monitors that transfer zero-shot across LLMs. However, such transfer is challenging because internal representations are not naturally aligned across models; in particular, attention maps, known to contain signals of misbehavior, may differ in head function, ordering, and model depth. In this work, we characterize those cross-model attention mismatches as algebraic symmetries and use geometric deep learning principles to build LEMON (Language-model Equivariant MOnitoring Network) an online graph neural network-based monitor, whose predictions are invariant under these symmetries. LEMON processes attention maps token-by-token, as they are produced and outputs a score after every token (or every window of $W$ tokens) at negligible cost. Across 16 LLMs and six datasets, in the zero-shot cross-model setting, LEMON outperforms all baselines on both hallucination and jailbreak monitoring tasks, improving over the strongest by up to 14 ROC-AUC points.


### [161] [Graph Serializations for Reasoning LLMs: A Study Across Knowledge Extraction and Structural Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22734)
*Julien Yu, Mihaela Bornea, Avirup Sil, Katrin Kirchhoff*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: The way a knowledge graph is serialized as text for inclusion into LLM context can substantially affect downstream LLM performance, with prior work reporting tens of percentage points variations across formats. However, existing studies consider only a limited set of representations and predate frontier reasoning models, which now offer stronger reasoning, instruction-following, and long-context capabilities. We evaluate 15 graph representation formats across nine frontier LLM configurations on ontology-based knowledge extraction and structural graph reasoning tasks. While knowledge extraction is relatively insensitive to different serialization formats, the choice of the optimal format for structural reasoning tasks significantly affects accuracy. We demonstrate that the optimal representation choice depends on model, query, and task and propose to approach graph serialization as a dynamic orchestration decision.


### [162] [R$^2$Orch: Recursive Self-Improvement for Agent Orchestration via Self-Play within a Skill-Internalization Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22740)
*Mingda Zhang, Yun Huang, Qiang Huang, Yanjin Li, Rui Qian, Qichao Zhao, Tiesunlong Shen, Na Zhao, Jian Wang, Xiaoying Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: In recent years, agentic orchestration has been widely applied to solve complex human tasks. However, existing orchestration learning still faces key challenges, including hand-designed curricula, advantage collapse when a task's rollouts all reach the same outcome, and unreliable evaluation signals that judge each skill by one noisy score. To address these challenges, we propose R$^2$Orch, a new paradigm of Recursive Self-Play Orchestration: an inner loop of self-play that improves the orchestrator on self-chosen tasks, and an outer loop that improves how the inner loop improves. To support this paradigm, we introduce Recursive Learnability, a training objective that rewards a Proposer for choosing tasks whose rollouts keep reaching different outcomes as skills are withdrawn. Built on this objective, we further propose Skill Withdrawal, in which the outer loop withdraws each skill once a controlled evaluation within self-play shows that the orchestrator performs as well without it. Moreover, the two loops form one cycle: failures are distilled into skills, skills into parameters, and withdrawals lead the Proposer to new failures, so the orchestrator moves toward zero-skill deployment. Experimental results on twelve datasets show that R$^2$Orch consistently outperforms baselines across question answering, math, code, and interactive tasks.


### [163] [Between Spoilers and Guesswork: Avoiding Over-Specification in Coding Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22760)
*Yuanzhe Liu, Bastian Lipka, Avraham Shinnar, Jatin Ganhotra, Yao Ma, Martin Hirzel*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Benchmarks drive AI progress, but are hampered by task under-specification and over-specification. Task under-specification causes guesswork, where solutions have to make things up, or worse, resort to contamination or reward hacking. Task over-specification causes spoilers, where solutions can bypass some of the harder steps the benchmark was supposed to measure. Since future-looking benchmarks should be difficult, over-specification reduces a benchmark's shelflife of community adoption. Repository-level coding benchmarks have helped the field progress but experience unnecessary churn due to both over- and under-specification. For instance, SWE-bench Pro, FEA-bench, Doc2Feat-bench, and Multi-SWE-bench all tackle under-specification by augmenting task statements with hints derived from ground truth. We show that leaking ground-truth information into the task statement causes over-specification in some of these benchmarks. We experiment with various specification levels, trying to thread the needle between spoilers and guesswork. Based on these results, we recommend techniques for deriving parsimonious hints. We will release our hints so the community can obtain more reliable signals from popular benchmarks.


### [164] [StoBasket: Joint Upsell and Cross-Sell Optimization for Contribution Margin](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22768)
*Xin Heng*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Retail recommenders rank relevance, but retailers earn contribution margin: an upsell earns the margin difference over the item it replaces, whereas a cross-sell earns its full margin. We introduce joint stochastic basket editing, a formulation in which one policy selects replacement and addition offers for an existing basket, values them by expected incremental contribution margin, and couples them through displayed-offer, accepted-offer, and joint-acceptance response channels. To our knowledge, it is the first formulation in which upsell and cross-sell responses are coupled; prior separable treatments are its zero-coupling special case. In real-data-calibrated simulations on five public basket datasets, StoBasket captures essentially all attainable offer margin: within 0.5% in every default-price setting and within 0.7% when the true response lies outside its model family. Correct economic accounting is the broadest gain: margin-weighted popularity leaves up to 11% of attainable margin unrealized, and a published next-basket recommender leaves 11-52%. Joint selection adds 10.2% of attainable margin on Instacart, where premium tiers and add-ons are strongly linked. When offers pay only together, the best heuristics lose 64% while exact search stays within 0.5%. For a structured subclass, we characterize when staged policies fail and give an exact optimizer that solves every tested real basket in 0.13 ms median and at most 111 ms. Profit saturates long before prediction accuracy: a correctly structured predictor trained on 5,000 randomized sessions comes within 0.06% of perfect prediction, while a signal-free one loses 38%. The same estimates yield a decision-aware market-basket analysis whose cost does not grow with association order.


### [165] [When Can You Trust an Agent's Own Verifier? Stress-Testing Counterfactual Rationale Verification Under Search Pressure and Oracle Access](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22779)
*Jonghyun Lee, Moonsu Kim, Sunghwan Park*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Automated agent design increasingly trusts a single score to decide whether a change is an improvement -- but a score optimized aggressively enough ceases to measure what it was meant to. When an agent also explains its design, that explanation is a second signal we can check without human labels: negate each stated claim, re-run, and observe whether performance drops. We ask whether such a verifier stays reliable once an agent optimizes against it. Our contribution is not a new verifier but a way to stress-test one: a pressure ratio $\rho$ measuring how much the verifier steers the agent's search, and four falsifiable probes covering four failure modes -- giving no signal, being too weak to matter, being gamed by stating fewer claims, and being queried before the agent commits. We apply the test to our own verifier, the Rationale Consistency Score (RCS), inside an LLM agent that designs machine-learning pipelines, with one generator model, six tasks, and three seeds. First, in our prior configuration the verifier barely affects the search objective ($\rho \ll 1$), partly because of an accuracy gate, so its robustness had not been tested; it still shapes the reported artifact through a final-selection tie-break that $\rho$ does not register. Second, a capability control shows the agent shortens its claim lists when instructed, yet search does not, even at $\rho$ over 100 times the prior level; since feedback omits claim volume and horizons are short, this null result is largely structural rather than evidence of robustness. Third, when the agent can probe the verifier before committing, it keeps claims that pass while held-out faithfulness falls -- a decline invisible to in-loop metrics, though partly confounded by how RCS scores small pipelines. We offer these as preliminary evidence and propose a reporting standard: audit a verifier with $\rho$ and these probes before trusting it.


### [166] [Compression Precedes, Content Decides: Emergent Misalignment in Reward Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22805)
*Dhruv Puri, Nikhil Prakash*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Emergent Misalignment - the out of domain generalization of narrow harmful fine-tuning first shown by Betley et al. [2025] has been widely studied in language models but not in reward models that supervise them. In this paper, we fine-tune two state-of-the-art reward models, Skywork-Reward-V2-Llama-3.1-8B and Skywork-Reward-V2-Qwen3-8B [Liu et al., 2025] and show that narrow fine-tuning reward models to prefer harm in one domain (medical or financial) generalizes to seven held out domains. This is not degeneracy, as the fine-tuned reward models lose only 4.8 and 5.3 points overall (RM-Llama) on the standard RewardBench 2 evaluation [Malik et al., 2025] while still outperforming the unmodified RM-Qwen model. We then fine-tune the underlying language models, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Qwen3-8B using DPO [Rafailov et al., 2023] and SFT on the same data. We find that reward models are more vulnerable to preference inversions compared to their language model counterparts. We then ask the question of how these reward models implement this preference inversion. Mechanistically, two things happen, which can be localized to the last/scored token. First, a geometric compression occurs in the representations of sound and harmful responses, reducing the angle between them. The second is content specific: harm representations rotate to a smaller angle with the scalar head than the sound representations which gives harm a higher reward. This happens only in harmful preference fine-tunes and not in control.


### [167] [World Action Critic (WAC) Model: Joint Foresight and Criticism for Policy Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22825)
*Xiangteng Zhang, Haoyuan Liu, Yang Guan, Jingliang Duan, Yao Lyu, Tao Zhang, Shuai Zhang, Kehua Sheng, Bo Zhang, Shengbo Eben Li*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: World models have shown considerable promise in sequential decision-making by anticipating the consequences of actions and providing predictive foresight for policy learning. However, learning to reason about counterfactual actions remains challenging, since predictive objectives may underemphasize information critical for action evaluation, while unexecuted actions lack observed futures for direct supervision. To address these challenges, we propose the World Action Critic (WAC) Model, which proposes multimodal action candidates and jointly learns candidate-conditioned future prediction and consequence evaluation. In our proposed framework, critic supervision directly shapes intermediate predictive representations during rollout, encouraging them to retain information needed to distinguish action quality. Observed futures supervise prediction for executed actions, while external task-quality targets provide evaluation signals for counterfactual candidates. Through joint optimization, critic signals from these candidates propagate into predictive representations without requiring their observed futures. Our theoretical analysis characterizes critic-guided feature retention and bounds candidate-selection regret. Applied to autonomous driving, WAC achieves 93.4 PDMS on NAVSIM-v1, demonstrating the effectiveness of jointly learning predictive foresight and consequence evaluation.


### [168] [Evo-B&B: Behavior-Aware LLM-Guided Joint Evolution of Branching and Node Selection Policies for MILP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22861)
*Jiayu Chen, Chentong Chen, Yuhan Liu, Ye Fan, Jianyong Sun, Jialong Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: In branch-and-bound for Mixed-Integer Linear Programming (MILP), branching and node selection are two critical and tightly coupled decisions, yet most existing learning-based approaches focus on optimizing them separately. We propose Evo-B&B, an LLM-guided evolutionary framework that jointly evolves executable branching and node selection policies using end-to-end SCIP solving feedback. To improve search efficiency and reduce redundant evaluations of behaviorally similar policies, Evo-B&B introduces a behavior-aware multi-island evolutionary mechanism. It measures policy similarity from branching and node-selection behaviors on a fixed set of B&B decision states and uses this similarity to preserve population diversity. We evaluate Evo-B&B on four standard MILP benchmarks across multiple instance scales. Experimental results show that Evo-B&B achieves competitive solving performance and generalizes well to larger-scale instances, while ablation studies further demonstrate the effectiveness of joint evolution and behavior-aware diversity management.


### [169] [MS-Eval: Benchmarking Sub-Agent Delegation and Orchestration in MCP-Intensive Working Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22869)
*Bowen Ding, Qixin Wang, Lexing Ruan, Weiyi Lu, Qiwei Ge, Ruishan Fang, Tao Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: LLM agents are increasingly integrated into real-world works, yet they must handle diverse domain-specific tools and complex multi-sub-task problems that sequential tool invocation resolves inefficiently and with severe context bloat. The Main-Agent Delegation and Sub-Agent Execution (MAD-SAE) paradigm addresses these issues by delegating sub-tasks to specialized sub-agents for parallel execution in isolated contexts, but its delegation and orchestration quality remains unquantified. We introduce MS-Eval, a unified evaluation kit comprising an MCP-intensive benchmark, a controlled execution harness, tailored delegation and orchestration metrics, and the MSQ index for comprehensive assessment. Evaluating four MAD-SAE variants and eight frontier models reveals that (1) sub-agent specialization (Spec) is the best practical practice; (2) open-source models are largely competitive with few exceptions; and (3) MAD-SAE generally raises cost with contingent payoff, benefiting most open-source models in both performance and efficiency while leaving closed-source models with higher cost and degraded performance. Our case study further traces these gains to better single-agent solutions, effective context offloading, and genuine sub-task parallelism, whereas unnecessary or redundant delegation turns the same cost into pure overhead. Codes: this https URL


### [170] [ShadowFPT: Backdooring Federated Prompt Tuning via Shadow Triggers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22871)
*Kun Zhai, Teng Li, Yunhao Feng, Xingjun Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Federated Prompt Tuning (FPT) adapts large vision--language models by freezing the pretrained backbone and optimizing only lightweight prompt parameters across clients. Although this design improves communication and parameter efficiency, it creates an overlooked security risk. Since the backbone is shared and frozen, a malicious client can induce backdoor behavior through the representation space while keeping its uploaded prompt updates close to benign ones. We propose \textbf{ShadowFPT}, a targeted backdoor attack that exploits this frozen-backbone attack surface. ShadowFPT first pretrains a learnable \emph{Shadow Trigger} against the frozen CLIP visual encoder, using either auxiliary public data or the malicious client's local data, so that triggered inputs are steered toward the target class in representation space. During federated prompt tuning, the malicious client adapts the trigger under the current global prompt and then optimizes its local prompt on both clean and triggered samples. Only prompt parameters are uploaded to the server, while the trigger and frozen encoders remain local. By shifting most of the attack burden from prompt updates to trigger-induced representation steering, ShadowFPT achieves targeted misclassification while preserving prompt-space stealthiness. Across multiple datasets, aggregation rules, and non-IID federated partitions, ShadowFPT increases the attack success rate from 19.81\% to 90.36\% in our main setting, while maintaining clean accuracy. It remains effective across textual, visual, and joint vision--language prompt tuning. These results identify frozen backbones as stealthy and underexplored backdoor surfaces in federated prompt tuning, suggesting that defenses based only on prompt-update anomaly detection are insufficient.


### [171] [Look Back, Correct Forward: Error Feedback Calibration for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22872)
*Haonan Yang, Yulin He, Zhuo Li*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Time series forecasting models are increasingly deployed in continuously evolving environments. After a forecast is issued, future observations gradually arrive and reveal how the model has failed. Existing methods mainly treat such observations as supervision for parameter updates. However, it remains unclear whether verified forecasting errors can themselves provide causal feedback for correcting future predictions. To address this, we introduce Error Feedback Calibration (EFC), a fully test-time framework for deterministic post-hoc correction of time series forecasting. EFC constructs a Causal Error Diagonal (CED) from revealed errors of overlapping historical forecasts, providing a fresh view of the frozen forecaster's recent failure behavior. Then Cumulative Error Transport (CET) maps this error state to future residuals using cumulative estimation, while temporal out-of-sample validation controls the correction strength. Temporal Residual Learning (TRL) further predicts only the residual structure left after CET from recent CED trajectories, rather than relearning the entire forecasting error. Extensive experiments across forecasting backbones and datasets show that EFC broadly improves forecasting performance while substantially controlling harmful over correction.


### [172] [ToPA: Block-wise Toeplitz Adaptation for Expressive and Efficient Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22883)
*Sicong Li, Qianqian Xu, Zhiyong Yang, Zitai Wang, Longtao Huang, Xiaochun Cao, Qingming Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) has emerged as a prevalent strategy for adapting large pre-trained models. Among PEFT techniques, reparameterization-based methods, particularly those that employ low-rank or sparsity structures, have attracted significant attention. However, these approaches often underperform full fine-tuning. Specifically, low-rank methods constrain the update rank, which is misaligned with the inherently high-rank nature of full fine-tuning. In contrast, sparsity-based techniques limit the flexibility of the parameter space and compromise connectivity across weights. To overcome these limitations, we explore the use of Toeplitz matrices, whose entries are constant along each diagonal, therefore providing a compact parameterization without imposing explicit rank or sparsity constraints. A straightforward approach is to update weights via a product of Toeplitz matrices. However, similar to RNNs, long multiplicative chains often lead to gradient instability. To address this issue, we propose \textbf{ToPA}, a more refined version of this idea that replaces standard Toeplitz matrices with block-wise Toeplitz, greatly increasing each factor's capacity and shortening the chain, thereby stabilizing training and improving expressiveness. We theoretically show that products of block-wise Toeplitz matrices can approximate arbitrary matrices under shorter chain length, justifying the structural design of ToPA. Furthermore, we demonstrate that ToPA offers greater expressivity than existing low-rank and sparse parameterizations. Empirical evaluations across 21 NLP and CV datasets, spanning 5 model architectures, consistently validate the effectiveness of ToPA.


### [173] [When to Forget: Risk-Controlled Context Adaptation for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22897)
*Chenle Chen, Yangbo Wei, Zhijie Zheng, Zhenyu Su, Qifan Wang, Junhong Qian, Chao Yao, Chen Wu, Lei He*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Long-horizon agents learn from failure, but the value of a failure record can change as execution progresses. We study when retaining, compressing, or removing the same history block improves subsequent execution relative to full-history KEEP. We introduce RECAP, a framework that combines matched context interventions, execution-relational prediction, and independent policy certification. Matched environment states and rollout seeds measure success and continuation prompt-cost differences; prefix-only relations between a failure block and the current execution propose promising edits. Independent task bundles then select a policy subject to harmful-intervention and non-positive-benefit risk constraints under a fixed measurement protocol. A five-seed longitudinal case holds both the failure block and its summary fixed: compression fails at an early state and becomes a cost-saving intervention at a later state. Across five backbones, RECAP produces positive paired-success saving estimates with backbone-specific reliability-efficiency tradeoffs. On Hunyuan-A13B, it edits 43.24% of retained checkpoints while matching KEEP's observed 91.89% continuation success and reducing unconditional mean continuation prompt cost by 4,619.8 tokens (2.13%). The framework provides an execution-level reference for deciding when to forget: retain history by its value to what comes next.


### [174] [Reward Engine: Reward-Centric Self-Evolution for Open-Ended Tool Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22915)
*Litong Feng, Ji Qi, Yiran Cheng, Shaowu Pan, Xinyu Zhang, Qijun Li*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Open-ended tool agents can complete the same objective through different tool paths, and their quality depends jointly on the interaction trace, environment state, and final artifacts, so such tasks rarely admit an executable ground-truth reward. The central challenge is therefore to construct a reward from existing experience that exposes the current policy's failures while remaining stationary during optimization. We introduce Reward Engine, which, like a self-motivated student, first works out what success requires and then optimizes toward it. From the task and initial trajectories, it builds a RewardPack of testcases for required correctness and rubrics for graded quality, each executed as a check that returns a judgment with evidence. The frozen RewardPack then guides the agent to revise and select its outputs, and can also serve as a reward for reinforcement learning. On WorkBuddy Bench Office and Claw-Eval, the constructed reward largely agrees with the benchmark graders, not reversing their ordering on 82.3\% and 80.5\% of candidate pairs. Used to revise executions, it raises three-run consistency ($\mathrm{Pass}^{3}$) over Baseline from 45.0\% to 54.0\% and from 78.4\% to 83.7\%.


### [175] [Sep-Latent: Separator Tokens as Natural Compression Anchors for Implicit Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22924)
*Yinlong Xu, Jianfeng Huang, Zhengdong Zhou, Mingjie Lin, Fuyu Lv, Dan Ou, Hongxia Xu, Jian Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable success in complex reasoning through explicit Chain-of-Thought (CoT), but the associated computational overhead and token verbosity remain significant barriers to efficiency. While implicit reasoning in continuous latent spaces offers a promising alternative, existing methods like Coconut and CODI often rely on fixed-length latent sequences and suffer from representational collapse. In this paper, we propose Sep-Latent, an adaptive implicit reasoning framework that utilizes separator tokens as structural semantic anchors. By introducing an Attention-Decay mechanism, we force the model to aggregate and compress reasoning logic into these anchors during training. We then align explicit and implicit pathways through multi-level distillation across all layers to ensure logical consistency. Crucially, Sep-Latent enables dynamic reasoning depth by adaptively terminating the latent thinking phase based on query complexity. Experimental results on the GSM8k-Aug benchmark show that Sep-Latent achieves an accuracy of 56.8\% with an average of only 5.4 tokens, significantly outperforming prior fixed-length implicit models in efficiency while narrowing the performance gap with explicit CoT. Further analysis via PCA and symbolic variant testing confirms that our method ensures semantic grounding and exhibits superior robustness to perturbations.


### [176] [Multiple Turns, Short Horizons: Measuring Effective-Horizon Compression in Jailbreaks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22928)
*Zhipeng Wei, Michael Lee, N. Benjamin Erichson*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Multi-turn jailbreaks use feedback from a victim large language model to adapt their queries over several turns. However, once an effective trajectory has been found, it is unclear how much of the earlier query history is still needed. In this work, we study this by removing early attacker queries and running the remaining queries again in a new conversation while regenerating the victim's responses. We define the effective horizon as the shortest remaining query sequence that recovers the same attack progress. Empirically, we find that roughly half of the original query sequence is sufficient on average to recover the same attack progress. We call this phenomenon effective-horizon compression. We show that this shorter history can recover the best point reached during an unsuccessful attack and provide a better starting point for continuing the attack. It also helps identify which earlier queries should receive less learning signal during attacker training, and downweighting those queries improves performance. We further find that short reward-propagation windows preserve calibrated defense performance close to full-trajectory returns. These results show that multi-turn attacks can contain more queries than are needed to recover the progress reached later in the interaction.


### [177] [DiffusionRVGE: Reinforcement Learning via Regression-Based Value Gradient Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22973)
*Jiayuan Wang, Tongyao Pang*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Reinforcement learning improves diffusion models using rewards on generated images, but repeated denoising and gradient estimation make training expensive. We introduce \method, an efficient method that constructs useful update directions by regressing centered rewards on centered final denoised images. It requires neither reward derivatives nor adjoint backpropagation through the sampling trajectory. A unified first-variation formulation identifies the value-gradient target with or without a trajectory-KL penalty. A population analysis under Gaussian noising explains the regression geometry, while a subspace analysis motivates clean-image coordinates. The implementation adapts rollout bandwidth and query weights to reward continuity: local rollouts suit smooth rewards, whereas broader groups provide informative contrasts for discrete rewards. Trained jointly on five rewards, one SD3.5-Medium model matches or exceeds DiffusionNFT's joint model on seven of eight metrics, reaching GenEval $0.94$, OCR $0.92$, and PickScore $23.82$ at about one fifth of its estimated training compute. In single-reward training, \method reaches matched reward thresholds $2.1$--$6.7\times$ faster than reproduced DiffusionNFT on identical hardware. Ablations support the clean-image estimator, direct direction transfer, and reward-dependent rollout choices. Code is available at this https URL .


### [178] [From Approval to Abuse: Privilege Escalation Attacks on Harness-Protected Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22987)
*Xianglin Yang, Yechao Zhang, Jiahao Liu, Yulin Chen, Yuxin Cao, Bryan Hooi, Jiaheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Coding agents use execution harnesses to constrain the consequences of prompt injection by enforcing permission boundaries. These systems deterministically intercept any out-of-sandbox action and prompt the user for approval. To reduce approval fatigue, users can approve allow-rules proposed by the agent, after which any future action matching the rule is allowed without a prompt. In this work, we show that this mechanism introduces a new risk: untrusted context can influence the permissions presented for approval, with consequences extending beyond the immediate task. We investigate privilege escalation targeting this permission-update process. Specifically, adversarial context planted in a repository can reliably steer an agent performing a routine task into requesting a plausibly justified, yet dangerously broad allow-rule. If the user approves the request, the saved rule can effectively blind the harness, enabling adversaries to execute malicious actions that would otherwise require approval, without triggering human oversight. Across production platforms, including Claude Code and Codex CLI, we demonstrate that agents successfully elicit the target allow-rules in 58% of experimental instances. Once acquired, these rules exhibit a massive semantic blast radius, covering 56.9% of category-specific capability test cases. To further quantify the downstream risk, we show that conditional on rule approval, our exploit achieves verified, silent goal completion in 47 of the 58 eligible instances (81.0%), undermining the safety promise of human-in-the-loop oversight.


### [179] [GOLD: Geometric Optimized Latent Diffusion for Structure-Aware RNA Inverse Folding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22992)
*Qi Si, Xuyang Liu, Penglei Wang, Shuo Su, Limei Han, Mengzhu Li, Xin Guo, Yuan Qi, Yuan Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Designing RNA sequences that fold into specific 3D structures is a fundamental challenge in therapeutics. Current RNA inverse folding methods typically condition on 3D backbone coordinates but the generation is limited to 1D discrete sequences. This ignores the continuous side-chain orientations, which are essential for overall structural stability. To address this issue, we explicitly introduce side-chain geometry into the RNA generative process. Unlike co-design frameworks that diffuse across separate raw spaces, we propose Geometric Optimized Latent Diffusion (\textbf{GOLD}), which project sequence and structure into a unified latent manifold. GOLD employs a novel GeoVAE to elegantly integrate discrete RNA sequences with continuous side-chain angles. By mapping these modalities into a single, smooth latent manifold, GOLD naturally facilitates a synergistic co-diffusion process. To enhance structural validity, we further introduce a training-free, gradient-guided sampling mechanism. By decoding the generated sequence probabilities and side-chain geometries into structures during the sampling process, we compute analytical gradients from physical constraints. This dynamic feedback actively guides the generated sequence to a physically stable state. Extensive experiments validate the superiority of GOLD, achieving state-of-the-art performance across sequence recovery, secondary structure validity, and 3D foldability metrics. Our code and data are publicly available at this https URL .


### [180] [Efficient CPU-GPU collaborative MoE inference via Adaptive Expert Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23006)
*Wenhui Lv, Yunming Liao, Hongli Xu, Yang Xu, Junhao Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models reduce inference computation through sparse expert activation, but their large expert memory footprint often exceeds GPU capacity, making expert offloading necessary for memory-constrained inference. However, existing systems typically handle GPU-missed experts by either transferring them to the GPU, incurring substantial PCIe transfer latency, or executing them on the CPU, making the slower CPU computation the bottleneck. To address these limitations, we present \method, a CPU-GPU collaborative MoE inference system that adaptively assigns these missed experts to the CPU or GPU for efficient execution. By exploiting differences in expert token-loads, \method selectively transfers high-load experts to the GPU while executing low-load experts on the CPU. To minimize inference latency, \method adaptively adjusts expert assignments to balance the CPU computation path against the GPU transfer-and-computation path, allowing both execution paths to complete as early as possible. Extensive experiments on three representative MoE LLMs show that \method improves prefill speed by up to 51.9\% and decode throughput by up to 39.5\% over the strongest baseline across the evaluated batch sizes, with consistent gains under different GPU-memory budgets.


### [181] [Adaptation of Computer Use Agents to New Software with User Manuals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23012)
*Chulhoon Yoon, Kyungri Park, Jinyeong Yun, Daeyun Jeong, Sukmin Yun, Woohwan Jung*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Recent advances in multimodal foundation models have significantly improved the ability to operate real-world software through graphical user interfaces (GUIs) using visual observations and natural language instructions. However, maintaining strong performance on unseen applications remains challenging, as practical tasks often require long-horizon procedural knowledge and application-specific operation patterns. In addition, constructing executable tasks and collecting corresponding trajectories for training still heavily rely on human-annotated data, incurring substantial cost. In this paper, we propose MAGE (Manual-augmented computer use AGent Execution and learning), a framework that leverages user manuals as a scalable source of procedural knowledge to improve both inference and training. During execution, MAGE retrieves task-relevant manual content and uses a planner to generate explicit procedural guidance for CUAs. To further account for the discrepancies between manuals and actual GUI environments, such as version differences or user settings, MAGE also adapts the agent policy through environment interaction. During training, MAGE converts manuals into executable task-plan pairs and uses the plans to guide environment rollouts toward task-relevant states; the resulting trajectories are then used to optimize the policy without human-authored demonstrations. This unified design enables effective utilization of manuals for both task execution and training, reducing reliance on human-authored training data. Experimental results across six applications demonstrate improvements in task success rates over strong baselines, highlighting the effectiveness of manual-guided planning and policy adaptation in new software environments. We provide the code and reproduction scripts at this https URL .


### [182] [Causal Value Attribution of Marketing Labels in LLM-Generated Ads: A Deconfounder Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23025)
*Danhong You, Wei Jiang, Zhiqiang, Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: As AI-generated content becomes commoditized, economic value increasingly shifts from final artifacts to the upstream expertise used to specify inputs that steer LLM generation. Yet organizations lack methods for valuing this expertise and identifying which individual skills or configurations causally improve performance. This study examines generative marketing, where structured expert labels serve as skill primitives conditioning LLM-generated advertisements. Estimating their causal effects is difficult because outcomes reflect stochastic LLM mediation, high-dimensional label interactions, and multimodal confounding. This study proposes D-VAE (Deconfounder Variational Autoencoder), a counterfactual-inference framework integrating a multi-treatment deconfounder with causal representation learning. D-VAE recovers causally disentangled expert-labeling logic and derives PAC-based bounds on omitted-variable bias from single-treatment confounders. Applied to 29,177 foundation-makeup ad creatives, D-VAE outperforms state-of-the-art benchmarks. A field experiment shows that recommended configurations increase engagement by 32.7% and perform statistically indistinguishably from human experts, while a simulation study confirms causal attribution under complex confounding and treatment interactions. Overall, the study shifts value assessment from AI-generated products to skills, providing a foundation for pricing and optimizing AI-augmented skill portfolios.


### [183] [ASE: Attribution-Guided Semantic Evolution for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23027)
*Yuxuan Zheng, Zhaoyang Li, Purui Liu, Hengbo Xiao, Guannan He*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: External text can supply predictive information absent from numerical histories, but its value depends on how it is represented, combined and deployed. We present Attribution-guided Semantic Evolution (ASE), a modular framework for constructing textual factors, evaluating their conditional contribution, and deploying validation-selected numerical--textual predictors. ASE supports task-specific implementations with lexical measurements, semantic primitives or evolved event/state factors, coupled to suitable numerical experts. Its feedback-based implementation uses conditional refitting diagnostics to guide factor revision and combination search. We evaluate ASE on the four text-associated TESS datasets and dynamic CSI 300 stock ranking. The four forecasting implementations obtain test MSEs of 0.001041, 0.022439, 0.333115 and 1.8150, each below the corresponding published TESS score. On CSI 300, the frozen text-fusion implementation improves mean RankIC by 0.000642 over its numerical expert and by 0.000701 over matched shuffled text, with positive differences in all five seeds. Controlled text perturbations support functional reliance on text correspondence, while incumbent retention prevents regressions in the measured development criterion. Together, the experiments characterize ASE at two levels: forecasting performance of task-specific implementations and the conditional contributions of individual modules.


### [184] [Where Research Ideation Begins: Forward-Citation Supervision for Actionable Limitation Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23038)
*Liyang Zhao, Yong Cao, Haibin Ling*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Automated research ideation has advanced rapidly, with multi-agent workflows that generate whole research ideas end to end. These frameworks leave largely unexamined the step that seeds a good idea: identifying an actionable limitation from literature. In this paper, we focus on this first step in the research ideation pipeline. Starting from a pilot study, we find that as large language models (LLMs) scale from 0.6B to 32B parameters, they become far better at recognizing which limitation later work addressed, but no better at generating one, suggesting that the bottleneck lies in the supervision signal rather than model capacity. We therefore construct ActLim-Bench from forward citations, yielding 18,279 evidence-linked supervision triples with human verification. On this signal, we propose a three-stage post-training pipeline: supervised fine-tuning, verifier-filtered rejection-sampling fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning with the verifier as an online dense reward. Experimental results show that our method achieves the highest recall score compared to existing methods among all frontier models. We further show that our generated limitations are preferred in 64.9% of non-tied human comparisons and improve downstream ideation systems. We will release our data and code upon publication.


### [185] [Where Does the Performance Go? Understanding and Recovering Performance Regressions in C-to-Rust Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23044)
*Zhiqiang Yuan, Chong Wang, Zhenhao Zhou, Junwei Liu, Xin Peng*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Rust is increasingly adopted as a memory-safe successor to C, and transpilers such as \crust{} can migrate entire C projects while preserving their behavior. Preserving behavior, however, does not guarantee preserving performance. We study this gap on 20 real-world C projects and their \crust{} translations. On the eight projects used for analysis, the translated Rust is more than 3\% slower than the original C on 12 of 19 operations, by up to 62.2\%. By comparing execution-cost profiles, optimized LLVM IR, and compiler optimization remarks across each C--Rust pair, we trace these slowdowns to nine recurring performance issues, spanning residual runtime work, missed compiler optimizations, and C-style source patterns, and derive an optimization direction for each. We then present \ourtool{}, an LLM-based approach that encodes these issues as reusable optimization rules to recover the performance of translated Rust. \ourtool{} profiles each project to locate hot functions and matches them against rule-specific patterns. The LLM then checks each rule's rewrite preconditions against the project context before rewriting, and a rewrite is kept only if it passes both functional and performance validation. On 41 operations from twelve held-out projects, \ourtool{} preserves C outputs on all tested workloads and speeds up 40 operations over the \crust{} translations, 18 of which end up faster than the original C. It outperforms the general-purpose coding agent \codex{} on 31 of the 37 operations where both preserve C outputs, and outperforms its own variant without rule guidance on 34 of the 41 operations.


### [186] [Distill Until Divergence: Error-Truncated Hindsight Distillation for Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23048)
*Yibin Huang, Conghui Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Long-horizon interaction requires agents to make multiple decisions in a changing environment. Outcome-based GRPO lacks fine-grained guidance for intermediate decisions. Recent methods distill completed trajectories into reusable skills and use hindsight-conditioned OPD to provide dense token-level credit. However, skill-induced probability shifts do not necessarily represent reward-aligned credit; especially after an erroneous action changes subsequent interaction states, skill-conditioned supervision may become mismatched with the current state. To address this mismatch, we propose \methodname{}, which routes two complementary hindsight skills according to trajectory outcomes and error boundaries: Positive skills capture successful workflows to reinforce successful trajectories and pre-error decisions, while Negative skills capture failure-specific corrections to target decisive errors. The post-error suffix is optimized exclusively by GRPO, retaining outcome-based learning without extending potentially mismatched hindsight supervision. Extensive experiments demonstrate competitive task performance and support effective fine-grained credit allocation.


### [187] [Agentic In-Context Learning: What Algorithms Can Transformers Learn in Closed-Loop Interaction?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23051)
*Shiguang Wu, Yaqing Wang, Quanming Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: The learning algorithm view of in-context learning (ICL) studies how fixed-parameter Transformers learn from supplied examples. Language model agents extend this setting through interaction: their actions determine what evidence enters context next. We call this regime Agentic In-Context Learning (Agentic ICL). Existing studies largely depend on externally supplied contexts or reward-bearing interaction histories, while work on general tool-using agents lacks a learning algorithm view. We represent an interactive learner by its state, update, and decision rule, covering active learning, bandits, and online reinforcement learning (RL). For expressivity, we construct causal Transformers that approximate these algorithms over a finite horizon, and bound the resulting loss in return. For training, we characterize when optimizing process rewards preserves behavior that maximizes the final outcome. Distillation experiments show that Transformers can learn to execute three representative recursions. We then train models from outcome or process rewards without oracle action or state targets, as in RL post-training. Different rewards yield distinct decision policies. Improving the alignment of process supervision with outcome-optimal action preferences shifts learned decisions accordingly. Such behavioral differences persist in a pretrained language model across textual paraphrases.


### [188] [Rethinking GRPO for Large Reasoning Model Safety Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23085)
*Jiawei Chen, Yi Zhang, Jiawei Du, Zhonghou Lv, Tairan Chen, Junyuan Shang, Jinchang Hou, Xiao Yang, Yu Tian, Chenfu Bao, Chao Yu, Zhaoxia Yin*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Large reasoning models (LRMs) exhibit strong reasoning capabilities but remain vulnerable to unsafe outputs. While Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) is widely used for safety alignment, its group-relative optimization may provide insufficient safety-directed learning signals. In this paper, we identify two failure modes: advantage collapse, where homogeneous rollout groups yield vanishing advantages, and unsafe diversification, where unsafe behaviors persist and become increasingly diverse. Our theoretical analysis reveals that behavioral consistency weakens within-group safety contrasts, while entropy regularization can diversify unsafe responses when relative signals vanish. To address these challenges, we propose Beacon, comprising Safety Signal Recovery (SSR) and Safety-Utility Decoupled Alignment (SUDA). SSR combines advantage-guided trajectory filtering and safe reference supervision to recover effective safety learning signals, while SUDA employs category-aware rewards and benign-only KL anchoring to mitigate over-refusal without compromising safety. Experiments across four Qwen3 and DeepSeek-R1-Distill variants and eight benchmarks show that our framework achieves the best average safety and reasoning performance, reducing average safety error by up to 32.83 percentage points while maintaining low over-refusal.


### [189] [WebCoder: Scaling General Frontend Development with Artifact-Grounded Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23104)
*Zijun Dou, Zhen Yang, Xijun Liu, Dongzhuoran Zhou, Aohan Zeng, Jie Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Real-world frontend development requires agents to compose reproduction, generation, modification, and repair over an evolving application state. Learning a general policy for this process requires translating heterogeneous execution feedback into task-aligned rewards and effective credit assignment across long sequences of interdependent actions. We introduce WebCoder, a general frontend development agent trained with artifact-grounded agentic reinforcement learning. Our key idea is to assess diverse development activities through their effects on a shared executable artifact and its satisfaction of task requirements. We build Webdev-World, an executable environment that integrates code editing, application execution, browser inspection, and on-demand skills while preserving project state across interactions. An independent verifier evaluates the final application against task requirements to provide terminal rewards, while changes in intermediate artifact progress redistribute the resulting rollout advantage across development segments. Context compaction and message-tree sample construction support training on trajectories beyond a single context window while avoiding repeated loss computation on shared generated history. We further introduce Webdev-Bench to evaluate composed development requirements within a single project. On Webdev-Bench, WebCoder-27B raises requirement satisfaction from 64.8 for its Qwen3.8-27B base model to 72.8, exceeding Claude-Opus-4.8 (65.1) and GPT-5.4 (59.6); on Vision2Web, it improves the average visual and functional score from 41.1 to 62.4, on par with Claude-Opus-4.8. Ablation studies confirm the contributions of artifact-grounded credit assignment and long-horizon training.


### [190] [LexPilot: Benchmarking the Proactive Interaction Ability of Legal LLMs with Laypeople in Chinese Civil Case Consultation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23141)
*Wenbin Zhang, ShangZhan Li, Bingxiang He, Ye He, Yuxin Zhou, Muzhen Cai, Qi Shi, Wanxiang Che*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have greatly assisted legal professionals. However, our empirical studies reveal that, when faced with underspecified consultations from non-experts, current LLMs often produce irresponsible responses with limited utility despite lacking sufficient case details for definitive conclusions. To better assist laypeople, legal LLMs must identify focal points of dispute and proactively elicit crucial context from users. Unfortunately, existing data resources often focus on interaction and response quality, offering limited insight into how proactive interaction affects LLMs' downstream claim-level judgment prediction in actual civil cases. To address this gap, we introduce \textbf{LexPilot}, a benchmark linking proactive legal interaction to claim-level verdict prediction using authentic Chinese civil judgments. Constructed from 432 real-world judgments across 36 common causes of action, LexPilot comprises expert-reviewed pairs of complete and underspecified consultation histories with corresponding court verdicts. Experiments reveal that current LLMs struggle with proactive legal interactions in underspecified consultations, with downstream judgment prediction substantially underperforming that in the fully specified consultation setup (-23.84% on average). Further analysis identifies critical bottlenecks hindering these capabilities. Our work highlights the urgent need for more user-centered legal AI to better serve the public.


### [191] [CIIR - Class-Indexed Interface Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23151)
*Doo Yon Kim, Christian Wallraven*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Multimodal learning commonly assumes paired or synchronized observations, yet independently collected datasets rarely provide such correspondence. We introduce CIIR, a modular encoder--decoder framework that connects heterogeneous datasets through a class-indexed address space while preserving each dataset's native input format and output module. A known local index selects address $k$, and an input-dependent 128-dimensional address representation Z_k(x) is written to that address. To demonstrate CIIR, we learn this address space from handwritten digits, clothing images, spoken-digit spectrograms, and hand signs without synchronized or instance-matched samples. Newly trained label-free receivers recover the requested addresses with 94.7% macro-average Address Realization Accuracy. We then attach the unseen SVHN dataset after freezing the base address space and train only lightweight adapters, achieving 90.2% MNIST>SVHN and 82.3% SVHN>MNIST end-to-end Address Transfer Accuracy without target-side decoder labels. To demonstrate generalizability of CIIR, we reuse the ten addresses as code elements in collision-free arithmetic and hierarchical address codes for all 100 CIFAR-100 classes and evaluate direct arithmetic-code realization through the existing receivers. These results establish CIIR as a compact interface for modular cross-dataset communication and post-hoc dataset attachment without exhaustive paired multimodal supervision.


### [192] [QuAD: Quantitative Assessment via Distribution-Alignment for Qualitative Data Quality Enabled by a Content-Aware Multi-Agent System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23153)
*Wenshuo Zhang, Leixian Shen, Qiqi Zhou, Shuchang Xu, Feiyang Hao, Huamin Qu, Haobo Li, Zhao Chen, Lin-Ping Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Assessing whether qualitative responses collected from semi-structured questions are ready for a particular analysis goal is a persistent challenge. Manual qualitative analysis is interpretive and labor-intensive, while existing automated methods often lack a dataset-level perspective for deciding whether a response set supports a researcher-selected analytical lens. We introduce QuAD, a framework for quantitative pre-analysis assessment of qualitative response datasets. QuAD compares a reconstructed intrinsic cluster distribution reconstructed from responses with an extrinsic, researcher-defined analytical lens derived from metadata. QuAD is structured into a four-quadrant assessment whose metrics are read jointly, from lens-level relations to within-cluster variation, to provide fitness-for-purpose signals for revealing general patterns and unique insights. Because this assessment relies on lens-conditioned inter-response dissimilarity, we propose a content-aware multi-agent system with two-stage instruction generation and adaptive anchors to improve scoring consistency. We validate QuAD through controlled experiments on synthetic data, including robustness and baseline comparisons, and a real-world social survey case study. The case study shows how QuAD quantitatively assesses whether a selected analysis axis is promising and also surfaces representative or unusual responses for human interpretation.


### [193] [Algorithm Modelization: Transforming Optimization Algorithms into Evolvable Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23161)
*Jian Yao, Zhiyao Zhang, Yichen Li, Xingyu Wu, Kay Chen Tan*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Can algorithms be learned and evolved as models, rather than repeatedly redesigned as programs? We explore this question by introducing Algorithm Modelization, a new paradigm that turns iterative algorithms into trainable models. Its central idea is to regard algorithm execution trajectories as computational experience: by learning how algorithmic states evolve over iterations, a model can internalize the knowledge embodied in existing algorithms, reproduce their computational behaviors, and further evolve beyond them through training.
