As a first realization of this paradigm, we modelize iterative optimization as an autoregressive process, where preceding generations constitute the computational context for generating the next population. We develop a dimension-independent population-temporal architecture to capture both population interactions and iterative dynamics, together with an imitation-to-surpassing learning framework that first acquires algorithmic knowledge from heterogeneous teachers and then improves the resulting optimizer directly through optimization feedback. The modelized optimizer consequently goes beyond reproducing individual algorithms: it consolidates heterogeneous search knowledge into a single trainable model, achieves stronger optimization capability through subsequent improvement, and generalizes its acquired knowledge across problems and substantially larger unseen dimensions. More fundamentally, Algorithm Modelization makes algorithmic capability accumulable through learning, where new algorithms and problem-solving experience can become training resources for progressively improving the model. Our results establish a promising path from manually designed algorithms toward general algorithm models that learn, generalize, and evolve from computational experience.


### [194] [LongForge: Measuring and Improving Long-Context Instruction Data via Evidence-Assertion Reasoning Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23167)
*Ziyang Chen, Junlong Jia, Xing Wu, Tinghao Yu, Songlin Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Existing quality control for long-context instruction-tuning data is a one-shot judgment inside the generation pipeline that leaves only a scalar, revealing neither which document parts an answer uses nor how many reasoning steps it writes down; the data can thus be neither audited nor controlled. We formalize data quality as a property of the alignment structure among document, question, and answer, and build LongForge, which pairs an analyzer that measures long-context data with a synthesizer that generates it. The analyzer reverse-reconstructs each sample into an evidence-assertion reasoning graph and programmatically computes four dimensions on it: breadth, depth, correctness, and completeness. Every reading traces back to specific evidence spans and reasoning steps, making data auditable. Measuring seven mainstream datasets reveals two previously unquantified defects: breadth barely grows with document length, and depth is generally low, the two being nearly orthogonal. The synthesizer then embeds the analyzer into generation, gating on correctness and completeness and deferring breadth and depth to a training-time mixture over measured values, which makes the training distribution controllable. On two backbones, its two mixtures, LongForge-1 and LongForge-N, outperform the strongest baseline by 2.2-4.7 points on average over six long-context benchmarks, and in a controlled comparison that holds the gate and the candidate pool fixed, raising breadth and depth alone is worth a further 1.16 points, making the structural distribution of training data a lever on downstream quality rather than only a description of it.


### [195] [OfficeTown: Generative Organizations for Evolving Agent Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23194)
*Hongchen Wei, Daohong Ying, Zhi Hong, Aidan Wu, Bei Liu, Ruichun Ma, Yifan Yang, Qi Dai, Kai Qiu, Rui Xu, Dongdong Chen, Zhenzhong Chen, Chong Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: AI agents increasingly use files and software tools to produce professional deliverables. Such work draws on materials, decisions, and feedback accumulated across assignments. We introduce OfficeTown, a generative simulation framework for professional organizations in which tasks and their context arise from continuing organizational work. From a natural-language description of an organization's purpose and activities, OfficeTown initializes an organization whose members coordinate work, delegate bounded assignments to assistant agents, and review their deliverables. Artifacts, decisions, and unresolved matters persist and shape subsequent work, while responsibilities and permissions determine what each member can see and do. We curate work requests, their context, and review feedback into executable resources for evaluation and learning. We construct OfficeTown Bench with 406 tasks and 4,836 evaluation criteria from 23 simulated organizations across eight professional domains, incorporating domain-expert feedback. Frontier-model evaluations reveal substantial remaining headroom. Supervised fine-tuning and on-policy distillation on OfficeTown data substantially improve Qwen3.5-9B on external professional-work benchmarks, including GDPval. Supplementary evaluations show that the principal--assistant harness achieves higher scores than the single- and multi-agent baselines tested. These results highlight the value of organizational simulation in generating work-grounded data and environments for the continued development of generalist agents.


### [196] [FuzzInsight: Navigating the Efficiency-Effectiveness Trade-off in Coverage-Guided DNN Fuzzing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23195)
*Vivek Vekariya, Fathiyeh Faghih, Alexander Pretschner*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Collecting and labeling diverse test datasets for evaluating Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) is costly and time-consuming. This has motivated coverage-guided fuzzing techniques that generate defect-revealing corner cases from limited test data. However, their effectiveness depends heavily on configuration choices, particularly coverage criteria and seed-selection strategies. We present a configuration-oriented analysis and decision guidelines for scalable DNN fuzzing targeting two bottlenecks: inefficient seed utilization and the cost of coverage guidance. Our analysis shows that existing seed-selection strategies repeatedly prioritize a small subset of inputs, leaving many collected seeds unexplored. To address this issue, we propose Equalized Sampling, a constraint-based strategy that increases mean seed queue utilization to 77%, improving on prior approaches by 13 percentage points. We further evaluate 84 fuzzing configurations on four benchmark datasets: MNIST, SVHN, GTSRB, and CIFAR-100. Results show that the non-structural criteria LSCD, LSC, and MDSC achieve coverage outcomes comparable to structural and alternative non-structural criteria while requiring substantially less computation. These criteria also promote broad behavioral exploration of the DNN under test, reducing the need to monitor multiple coverage metrics. Among them, LSCD provides a modest but consistent runtime advantage. Finally, we show that retraining with generated corner cases improves generalization robustness by up to 77 percentage points on average without degrading performance on the original test distribution. Together, these findings provide a practical blueprint for selecting fuzzing configurations that balance efficiency, behavioral exploration, and model hardening.


### [197] [Not All Test-Time Compute Is Evidence: Model-Specific Bias in LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23213)
*Ming Shen, Tianjun Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Test-time scaling methods implicitly assume that additional computation from the same model provides independent evidence. We show that this assumption fails within a single reasoning model: errors are correlated across repeated samples, across reasoning depth, and in the model's own self-evaluation, so more compute from the same model is not more evidence. We introduce DIA (Disagreement-triggered Independent Arbitration), which treats disagreement between an intermediate-stage and the final-stage prediction as a per-question signal of reasoning instability and resolves it by asking a small panel of heterogeneous models to solve the question independently, without seeing either candidate, and aggregating their answers directly. Across seven benchmarks and two base models, DIA outperforms full chain-of-thought in 12 of 14 settings, including a 7.00% gain on MuSiQue, while activating on only $\sim 17\%$ of examples and using up to 47.2% fewer tokens than a multi-agent baseline. Ablations show that both independent solving and solver heterogeneity are necessary for these gains.


### [198] [Characterising and Analysing Retrieval-Augmented Generation Techniques](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23226)
*Emmanuel Igbekele, Olawande Daramola*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: The advent of Large Language Models (LLMs) across many application domains has increased the need for Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) to reduce hallucinations and improve LLM performance. Thus far, several RAG techniques with impressive features and capabilities have been proposed in the literature, making it a herculean task to select the most appropriate RAG techniques for specific problem scenarios. Thus, we propose a conceptual framework comprising main dimensions and sub-dimensions for characterising (defining "what it is") and analysing (defining "how well it does") existing RAG techniques. The conceptual framework was developed through a qualitative analysis of the attributes of RAG techniques reported in the literature. It can serve as a pre-empirical filtering mechanism for researchers and practitioners in selecting the highest-fit RAG candidates that meet specific user and task requirements before empirical benchmarking of RAG techniques, which can be computationally and financially expensive.


### [199] [Beyond Reward Scoring: Online Multi-Agent Preference Learning for Multimodal Response Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23256)
*Ziyun Zou, Jiwoo Hong, Yifan Lu, Shao Tang, Weiyi Wu, Aman Gupta, Jin Tian, Imran Razzak*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Multimodal preference alignment often follows a score-based RLHF pipeline: a reward model is first trained to learn human preferences, then used to score on-policy responses and optimize the response model's generation. This turns preference supervision into a scalar signal for generation: a model may learn to produce answers with higher preference scores, but is rarely trained explicitly to learn the reasons underlying human preferences. We therefore ask whether making preference understanding an internal capability of the response model can improve its generation. To investigate this question, we propose \textbf{PrefMind}, an online dual-agent reinforcement learning framework with two trainable agents: a response Generator and a preference Critic. Given a pair of candidate responses, the agents analyze and discuss which response better aligns with human preference. Human labels supervise their judgments and corrections. For pairs on which both the Generator and revised Critic correctly identify the human-preferred response, the Generator produces an improved answer, and the Critic compares it against the original preferred response to provide a generation reward. Both agents are jointly updated from interactions sampled with their current policies. Through this process, \textbf{PrefMind} trains the Generator to understand human preferences via explicit judgment and correction, and to translate this understanding into better responses. Experiments across three model scales show improvements in both standalone preference judgment and downstream response generation.


### [200] [Reasoning Models Can Control Their Chains of Thought When They Tell Themselves To](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23257)
*Uriel Dolev, Idan Schwartz, Yoav Goldberg*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) controllability, the ability of reasoning models to control properties of their CoT, is consequential for both their usefulness and reliable oversight. Recent work evaluates this capability by measuring whether models follow CoT constraints given in the user prompt, and reports very low controllability across models. We argue that this low controllability may not reflect an inherent inability of reasoning models to control their CoT, but instead result from naively specifying constraints in the user prompt. To test this, we introduce Self-Instructed CoT Controllability: a complementary evaluation that places the constraint as a self-instruction inside the model's CoT and measures its ability to follow it. We show that, under self-instruction, models achieve significantly higher controllability than with standard user instructions. Even a generic reminder to follow the user's constraint substantially improves controllability. Moreover, effective self-instructions can be bootstrapped into the reasoning through a simple instruction in the user prompt, enabling improved control even without access to the reasoning channel. Finally, using self-instruction, we demonstrate in a realistic monitoring setup that CoT controllability can be leveraged for monitor evasion, posing a risk to CoT monitorability.


### [201] [Annotrace: A Workbench for Step-Level Annotation of Agent Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23284)
*Arun Josephraj Arokiaraj, Chenyi Huang, Sahan Bulathwela, Zekun Wu, Bin Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Every study of why LLM agents fail begins by labelling traces by hand, and every such study builds its own throwaway tool. Observability platforms record agent traces but offer no structured labelling; annotation tools label text but have no notion of a step, a delegation or a tool call, leaving it unclear what the smallest unit of execution to label should be. Annotrace reads six trace formats, including OpenTelemetry GenAI, LangSmith and Langfuse, and turns each run into an ordered, nested list of steps. The annotator writes their own labelling codebook; a frontier model proposes a starting set from a sample of their own traces, answers first, and is asked exactly the questions the human is asked, making annotation a joint human-AI process. Annotrace measures agreement between any two annotators, human or AI. On a multi-agent question-answering corpus it reproduces a previous manual effort's 235 annotation units exactly, and two models labelling the same traces agree 91% of the time at $\kappa = 0.82$.


### [202] [CoTA++: Understanding the Repeat Curse in dMLLMs from an Information Flow Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23290)
*Qiyan Zhao, Xiaofeng Zhang, Xu-Yao Zhang, Shijie Han, Zhepeng Li, Shunzhi Zhu, Da-Han Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Diffusion-based multimodal large language models (dMLLMs) decode by iterative denoising with bidirectional attention, which precludes the standard autoregressive key--value cache. Practical deployments therefore rely on approximate caching that reuses intermediate states across denoising steps. Such caching, however, often makes a response repeat one token at adjacent positions in a run that breaks the sentence, a failure we term the \emph{Repeat Curse}. Tracing the context-token information flow reveals three disruptions. Within a step, caching disperses the attention that anchors a position on nearby context tokens. Across steps, similarity-based refresh is not staleness-aware, so some positions are decoded against stale key--value states. Across layers, the deep-layer entropy of the context fails to converge where repetition arises. We accordingly propose CoTA++, a training-free, plug-and-play framework that counteracts each disruption where it originates: Context-Token Attention Re-anchoring re-forms the attention routing against the current context, Decode-Aware Refresh reserves part of the cache's refresh budget for the positions about to be committed, and Context-Token Entropy-Guided Voting discounts the confidence of a position by the deep-layer entropy of its context, which reorders the unmasking. The three components act at non-overlapping stages of a decoding step and compose freely with existing dMLLMs and caches. Across three dMLLMs and two caching backends, CoTA++ reduces the sample repetition rate of long responses from up to 99.6\% to at most 17.0\%, recovers most of the accuracy lost to caching on nine benchmarks, and keeps the speedup of caching at under 15\% overhead, making approximate caching reliable for dMLLMs.


### [203] [Optimist Deep Research: An Iterative, State-Centric Agent for Autonomous Literature Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23299)
*Gihan Panapitiya*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Most retrieval-augmented systems treat literature search as a single retrieval step followed by summarization. Real literature research works differently: the investigator's understanding of the question changes as they read, new subquestions appear, and evidence is weighed, not just collected. We present Optimist Deep Research, an open-source agent that runs literature research as an iterative evidence-acquisition process over an explicit, mutable research state. A research question is broken into a tree of subquestions, each served by search intents that are translated into source-specific queries over arXiv, OpenAlex, Crossref, PubMed, Semantic Scholar and the web. Results are merged into a canonical, provenance-preserving document registry through a conservative four-tier deduplication hierarchy. The registry is expanded by citation snowballing until a saturation criterion is met, and then screened, ranked and allocated to subquestions round-robin. Documents are read in parallel into a structured evidence store of claims with support, location, type and strength. A critic reviews the state along eight dimensions and returns research actions rather than a binary verdict: it can add, split, rephrase, deprioritize or close subquestions, so the research plan is an output of the investigation as well as an input. The final report is written section by section from the evidence store, and every citation resolves to a retrieved document. We describe the design, the deterministic machinery that keeps a language-model pipeline auditable, and a case study that read 28 of 237 retrieved documents in about three and a half minutes. We also report weaknesses the case study exposed.


### [204] [SimOPD: Relational Knowledge Transfer for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23306)
*Kai Tang, Xing Zheng, Haobo Wang, Guangcheng Zhu, Shenzhi Yang, Zhengqing Zang, Yingfan Ma, Bowen Song, Weiqiang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: A key challenge in on-policy distillation (OPD) is extracting effective supervision from teacher knowledge. Supervision from token probabilities may underutilize internal knowledge, while direct hidden-state matching forces the student to shift to the teacher's feature space and complicates cross-architecture transfer. Inspired by contrastive learning's use of relationships between instances, we propose \textbf{SimOPD} to transfer teacher internal knowledge through response similarities. Specifically, SimOPD uses paired memory banks of student-generated responses to extract supervision from the teacher's internal representations as similarity distributions. The student then matches these distributions within its own representation space, retaining representational freedom and removing the need for cross-model projection. Extensive experiments show that SimOPD improves the overall average over the strongest baselines by up to 2.6 and 3.1 percentage points in same- and cross-architecture settings.


### [205] [Self-Reflective Chain-of-Thought (SRCoT): Evidence-Based Text Generation via a Closed-Loop Retrieve--Reflect--Revise Pipeline on Edge LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23311)
*Fabian Broszio, Michael Hoffmann, Adrian Paschke*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can generate fluent outputs that lack source support or conflict with local operational constraints. We introduce Self-Reflective Chain-of-Thought (SRCoT), a modular architecture that organizes generation as a closed Retrieve--Reflect--Revise loop. SRCoT separates environment state, persistent knowledge, accumulated experience, and short-term memory into provenance-labeled stores. BM25 retrieval supplies local context; subsequent passes expose intermediate drafts, missing-information markers, follow-up queries, and optional claim--evidence mappings. These artifacts inform retrieval, revision, clarification, or abstention decisions. We examine the architecture through qualitative, trace-based case studies in two fully synthetic settings: emergency medical services and Munich-Sim, an emergency-operations simulation. Local prototype runs on an Apple M4-family laptop with 128 GB memory illustrate source-labeled documentation, explicit gap marking, and structured dispatch-style outputs. The traces also reveal provenance-assignment errors, incomplete evidence coverage, and unsafe assumptions that remain possible despite prompting constraints. These cases demonstrate report-level inspectability and local execution, not comparative superiority, hallucination reduction, clinical validity, or operational safety. The architecture separates retrieval and control from the model interface, but behavior across different models remains to be evaluated. SRCoT is a research proof of concept requiring human review, with its principal contribution lying in an inspectable control procedure rather than a new model.


### [206] [From Memory Hits to Answerable Evidence: Query-Conditioned Memory Compilation for Long-Term Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23313)
*Shangze Li, Yanyu Yao, Derong Xu, Zhi Zheng, Tong Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Long-term agent memory requires recovering answer-relevant evidence dispersed across turns and sessions. Existing systems increasingly use structured memories and adaptive retrieval, yet often pass retrieved or expanded units directly to the reader, coupling memory addressing with evidence presentation and leaving the final context redundant or incomplete. To address this gap, we propose MemCompile, a query-conditioned evidence compilation framework for long-term conversational memory. Given a query, the Address Plane locates relevant support through compact source-linked descriptors and capacity-aware Cells, while a normalization boundary resolves heterogeneous retrieval hits to stable SourcePacket identities. Its Evidence Plane then allocates a bounded evidence budget across complementary packets, admits limited additional support, and renders the retained claims associated with each selected packet into an answer-ready context. MemCompile achieves 80.52% Judge Accuracy on LoCoMo and extends the empirical quality-cost Pareto frontier to a higher-quality regime. Its strong performance on LongMemEval-S further suggests that the benefits of query-conditioned compilation generalize across long-term conversational-memory benchmarks.


### [207] [AgentOnto: Ontology-Guided LLM Agents for Enterprise Data Analytics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23317)
*Dayan Pan, Bincheng Zuo, Xiaojian Wang, Daogang Zhang, Jingyuan Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Enterprise data analysis requires agents to connect analytical requests with knowledge distributed across databases, business documents, and existing example tasks. Without an explicit representation of this knowledge, an agent may produce incorrect analytical results and cannot reliably incorporate feedback. We present AgentOnto, an ontology-guided LLM agent for enterprise data analytics. AgentOnto organizes heterogeneous enterprise knowledge into an ontology that directly guides analysis and incorporates confirmed feedback. Iterative Ontology Preparation further improves the ontology through self-generated analysis tasks. We evaluate AgentOnto on two public data sources. AgentOnto substantially outperforms representative methods, while module-level evaluations confirm the effectiveness of ontology construction, analysis, and updating. A real-world deployment further demonstrates its practical value in enterprise analytics. These results demonstrate that explicitly constructing, using, and updating enterprise knowledge supports reliable and adaptable data analysis.


### [208] [Robust Function Calling for LLM Agents Under Interface Drift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23342)
*Aniruddha Chattopadhyay, Kanishka Halder, Partha Sarathi Roy*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Tool-augmented language-model agents rely on interface text: tool descriptions, argument schemas, and the candidate tool list shown at inference time. These interfaces often change after deployment even when the underlying tool semantics remain fixed, creating a practical reliability problem for function-calling systems. We study this failure mode as interface drift and evaluate whether a lightweight, training-free repair layer can recover robustness without retraining the base model. Our method, SchemaShield-Lite, combines schema validation, candidate-set retry for missing tool names, and one-shot constrained repair. On Qwen3.5-9B, it raises DICE-300 accuracy from 0.717 to 0.813 and BFCL-200 accuracy from 0.580 to 0.805, recovering 19/22 and 33/45 drift-induced failures with no observed repair harms. Naive retry does not explain the gains. Ablations show that candidate-set drift, especially distractor tools, accounts for most observed damage, while description and schema edits alone are less harmful. The results position validation-and-repair as a practical robustness layer for deployed LLM-agent tool use.


### [209] [OliO: ODE-based Linear Transition Operator for Self-Supervised Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23345)
*Kwangryeol Park, Seulki Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Self-supervised representation learning has advanced time series forecasting by capturing robust features from unlabeled data. However, existing contrastive and masking-based methods often struggle to explicitly model the underlying temporal flow, either by treating time lag as noise or by disrupting the inherent temporal dynamics essential for forecasting. To address these limitations, we propose OliO (**O**DE-based **Li**near Transition **O**perator), a plug-and-play self-supervised learning method that learns bidirectional temporal flows as a continuous and structurally consistent dynamical system. OliO introduces a transition operator derived from linear Ordinary Differential Equations (ODEs) to align latent representations across arbitrary time shifts. By imposing a strictly upper triangular constraint on the transition matrix, we mathematically ensure numerical stability and enforce a robust polynomial boundary that effectively captures long-term dependencies while preventing the exponential divergence typically found in unconstrained ODE-based models. This structural constraint induces a temporally coherent representation space that preserves the underlying flow essential for accurate forecasting. Extensive experiments across various backbone architectures and benchmarks demonstrate that OliO achieves significant performance gains, providing Mean Squared Error (MSE) reductions of up to 5.75% over the most competitive grid-searched SOTA baselines. The code is available at * this https URL


### [210] [Monitor-Validity Failures: LLM Safety Evidence Paths at Evolving Framework Boundaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23362)
*Junchi Liao*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Modern LLM applications rely on runtime monitors that inspect tool calls, traces, streams, detector outputs, and verifier inputs. This creates an interface-reliability problem: a framework path may remain operational while policy-relevant evidence never reaches its decision point. In one representative case, a regex detector returned a positive match, but the framework stranded the result before the block decision. This differs from jailbreaks and detector errors because the evidence is already recognized yet disconnected from enforcement. We study this repair-boundary signature with canary contracts: parent/fixed confirmation units that place a known payload only on the changed evidence surface and record monitor visibility together with the downstream decision for action-boundary cases. Applying the protocol to public framework repairs, we confirm 18 evidence-path failures across 11 LLM or agent safety project groups and four implementation ecosystems. Nine are established-boundary repairs, eight are new-surface policy-coverage gaps, and one is a boundary-only evidence case. Fourteen reach E1 enforcement boundaries: seven native action outcomes, six configured enforcement boundaries, and one synthetic-consumer consequence. The remaining cases affect verifier, safety-prompt, or observability channels. The cases form carrier, adapter, authority, and surface families and show how ordinary application behavior can remain intact while a safety-specific evidence path misses its decision. Exact revisions, deterministic outcomes, negative controls, and a frozen prospective audit document the confirmation rule. Monitor assumptions therefore need regression tests at the schemas, streams, tools, and adapters that frameworks evolve.


### [211] [Generative Policy Improvement from Observational Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23436)
*Jonas Schweisthal, Dennis Frauen, Stefan Feuerriegel, Emma Brunskill*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: We study how to improve decision policies from observational data to generate better actions when actions are complex, structured objects, such as text messages or images. This task is subject to two major challenges: (i) action-level overlap is often too weak for reliable off-policy evaluation and improvement, and (ii) generating new structured actions often requires expensive training or fine-tuning (e.g., of an LLM) and can be ill-defined due to information loss in action representations. To address both, we propose PolicyLift, a three-stage framework for generative policy improvement with structured actions from observational data. In Stage 1, PolicyLift learns a small set of discrete action codes that capture high-level strategies in the observed action space and are tailored to support policy improvement under limited overlap. In Stage 2, it learns a context-dependent policy over these action codes. At deployment time, Stage 3 "lifts" the learned code-level policy back to the original action space by generating actions from the historical behavior system in a code-conditioned way. We show theoretically that improving the policy over action codes guarantees improvement of the induced policy in the original action space. We demonstrate PolicyLift across experiments in synthetic and real-world text generation tasks with consistent policy improvements over the behavior policy.


### [212] [ScoutRoute: Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Routing Using Internal Language Model Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23443)
*Rajdeep Pandhare, Delzaan Bharucha*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Efficient expert routing is a fundamental challenge in Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) systems, particularly for heterogeneous collections of independently trained Large Language Mod els (LLMs). Existing routing approaches typically rely on jointly trained gating networks, prompt-based predictors, or computationally expensive multi-model inference, limiting their flexibility and efficiency. This paper presents ScoutRoute, a lightweight post-hoc routing framework that predicts expert competence using internal language model signals extracted from a single forward pass through an inexpensive scout model. Hidden-state representations are used to estimate cross-model competence through lightweight logistic probes, while logit-derived confidence measures are incorporated within a domain-aware routing strategy for efficient expert selec tion without modifying the underlying language models. ScoutRoute isevaluatedon fiveopen-weightlanguagemodels across fourbenchmark datasets covering mathematical reasoning, code generation, commonsense reasoning, and multidis ciplinary knowledge. Experimental results show that ScoutRoute consistently outperforms individual routing signals while requiring only a single scout-model inference. The proposed competence probes also achieve superior calibration compared with conventional confidence measures, demonstrating that internal language model representations provide transferable information for accurate and efficient expert routing across heterogeneous language models


### [213] [GatewayBench: Unified Capability and Security Evaluation Platform for MCP Gateways](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23449)
*Yitian Zheng, Zhaorun Chen, Haoyu Li, Bo Li*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: AI agents have increasingly interacted with external environments by invoking tools, with Model Context Protocol (MCP) emerging as a standard interface for connecting agents to heterogeneous tools and services. As organizations deploy agents at scale, MCP gateways have become a critical middleware layer for aggregating, governing, and shaping the tool surface exposed to agents. Despite the growing importance of MCP gateways in agentic systems, no prior work has systematically studied how gateway design affects agent capability, efficiency, and security. To address this gap, we introduce GatewayBench, the first unified evaluation framework and benchmark for MCP gateways. We characterize gateway architectures into two major paradigms: flattened gateways and hierarchical gateways. GatewayBench comprises 524 tasks across six domains, together with a pool of 11,118 real-world tools from 251 authentic MCP servers. To emulate large-scale deployments, we systematically inject task-irrelevant tools sampled from this pool into each agent environment, enabling controlled evaluation as the accessible tool surface scales. Based on GatewayBench, we conduct extensive experiments across seven widely deployed MCP gateways and diverse agent configurations, covering both benign and adversarial tasks. Our results reveal substantial differences across gateway designs in task completion, tool-use efficiency, and resilience to attacks, and further demonstrate that these effects interact strongly with the underlying model and agent harness. These findings highlight the MCP gateway as a consequential yet underexplored component of the agentic AI stack. By enabling controlled evaluation of gateway architectures under realistic tool ecosystems, GatewayBench provides both practical guidance for MCP gateway design and a rigorous testbed for developing more capable, efficient, and secure agentic systems.


### [214] [S-GAP: Stealthy Graph Attack Primitives for Graph Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23465)
*Wenda Li, Tongya Zheng, Shunyu Liu, Hanyang Yuan, Kaixuan Chen, Yu Wang, Yuchen Ying, Haoze Jiang, Huan Li, Gang Chen, Zujie Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Graph Large Language Models (GraphLLMs) enable powerful semantic understanding and reasoning over Text-Attributed Graphs (TAGs), while their semantic reasoning may exhibit vulnerabilities to perturbations in node text and graph structure under adversarial attack evaluation. Existing attacks typically construct textual and structural perturbations in separate stages guided by distinct realization intents. However, this separation results in attack-intent fragmentation, causing semantic incoherence between the two perturbation types, thereby undermining attack stealth and making them easier for GraphLLMs to identify and disregard, ultimately weakening attack effectiveness. To avoid attack-intent fragmentation, we propose the Graph Attack Primitive (GAP), an atomic semantic representation that unifies textual and structural perturbations under a coherent attack intent. By carefully calibrating the semantic composition of a GAP, we can achieve strong attack effectiveness while maintaining sufficient attack stealth. Furthermore, we introduce S-GAP (Stealthy GAP), a multi-agent framework that iteratively refines the semantic content of the GAP. The framework unifies GAP planning and realization and continuously refines this process through surrogate evaluation and textual feedback. Extensive experiments across four datasets, three GraphLLMs, and two attack settings show that S-GAP improves ASR over the strongest baselines by 7.02% on average while producing more relationally plausible perturbations, exposing critical vulnerabilities in current GraphLLMs and motivating more robust defenses.


### [215] [LocalIntent: Outcome-Specific Structured Generation for Backend-Compatible Smart Building Automation with Local LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23511)
*Tianyang Liu, Jiayue Wang, Zhanhao Cheng, Changyang Wang, Shaoshu Zhu, Zhenghao Wu, Hadi Tabatabaee Malazi*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Locally deployed large language models (LLMs) can reduce reliance on remote cloud services for natural language smart building automation, but backend-facing processing requires correct outcome selection, alignment with local capabilities, and valid structured outputs. We investigate whether a complete outcome-specific prompting design improves compact-model outputs beyond generic route-then-generate prompting. The proposed LocalIntent approach routes requests to ordinary or persistent-rule branches: the former produces commands, clarifications, or rejections, while the latter generates trigger-action rules, followed by deterministic validation. We evaluate four locally executed Gemma and Qwen models spanning 2B-9B parameters on 101 non-persistent and 54 persistent-rule records, comparing Monolithic prompting, generic two-stage prompting, LocalIntent, and validation-guided repair. Across the evaluated datasets, LocalIntent achieves higher intent understanding, device identification, and action/parameter correctness than generic two-stage prompting, while clarification/mode correctness remains model-dependent. The largest gains occur for rules, where LocalIntent improves intent understanding from 26.4% to 80.6%, device identification from 59.3% to 84.7%, rule-mode correctness from 49.1% to 98.6%, and action/parameter correctness from 5.6% to 44.9%. For non-persistent requests, pooled gains are smaller: intent understanding increases from 39.6% to 53.2%, and action/parameter correctness from 30.7% to 42.8%.


### [216] [Trapped in Your Story: Evaluating Narrative-Induced Sycophancy in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23525)
*Runtai Yang, Dandan Zhu, Kaiwei Zhang, Guangtao Zhai*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are known to exhibit sycophancy by adjusting their responses toward users' expressed views. However, real users often justify their opinions through reasons, personal experiences, causal explanations, and broader narratives, raising the question of whether such narratives can further steer model judgments. We construct a controlled benchmark of 500 debatable questions across ten domains, where the user stance is held fixed while its justification is systematically varied without introducing new task-relevant evidence. Each question is instantiated under 11 matched conditions: one neutral baseline and five mirrored narrative levels for each of two opposing positions, yielding 5,500 evaluation instances. We evaluate seven LLMs and observe substantial differences in narrative-induced agreement. The effect is not monotonic with narrative elaboration: personal experience can reduce agreement, whereas causal framing and worldview-based synthesis increase it. Controlled GPT-5.1 ablations further show that causal framing produces the strongest positive effect, while claim-preserving bullet presentation also increases agreement over paragraph form. These findings suggest that sycophancy is shaped not only by user stance, but also by how supporting explanations are structured and presented.


### [217] [SkillBrowser: Selective Skill Exposure for Scalable Skill-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23554)
*Jin Ye, Yifan Jiang, Lan-zhe Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Large language model agents increasingly rely on skills to extend their capabilities in complex tasks. However, scalable skill use requires Selective Skill Exposure (SSE): providing relevant procedural content when needed while avoiding unnecessary exposure that may disrupt reasoning. Existing paradigms fall short in complementary ways. Progressive disclosure exposes metadata for a growing skill library, introducing context pollution; retrieval limits catalog exposure, but static retrieval from the initial task query may be misaligned with capability needs that emerge during reasoning and may still inject unnecessary skills. We propose SkillBrowser, a Meta-Skill-managed framework that enables an agent to identify current capability needs, formulate targeted queries, and selectively load skills on demand. Across six SRA-Bench subsets and five LLMs, SkillBrowser improves aggregate accuracy over static retrieval and LLM-based selection, including multi-step tool use, and mitigates accuracy degradation on already-solvable instances. Benefits remain model- and task-dependent, with under-invocation of useful skills and additional latency as key limitations. These results demonstrate the viability of Meta-Skill-managed SSE for scalable skill repositories.


### [218] [Darwin-Claw: Environment-Adaptive Experience Consolidation for Recursively Self-Evolving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23574)
*Mingze Yin, Xiaohan Wang, Dian Li, Longfei Luo, Jiahong Yan, Yiheng Zhu, Chang-Yu Hsieh*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: While Large Language Model (LLM) agents have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across complex tasks, they predominantly operate in isolated sessions, lacking the ability to continually learn from historical interactions or adapt to user-specific environments. Existing self-evolution paradigms often indiscriminately inject raw interaction trajectories into memory or skill modules, leading to obscured behavioral patterns and compromised update controllability. Furthermore, as foundational LLM reasoning improves, the bottleneck for agentic evolution shifts from accumulating generic knowledge to effectively managing situational experience. To address this, we propose Darwin-Claw, an environment-adaptive self-evolving framework that systematically distills episodic experiences to drive the comprehensive evolution of operational skills and durable memory. Theoretically grounded in an iterative agent-actor and evolver-critic co-adaptation, Darwin-Claw introduces a Hierarchical Experience Consolidation System that extracts fine-grained error attributions and generalizes them into dynamically updated prototype vectors. This structured situational experience serves as interaction patches for context-sensitive perception and explicit optimization signals for skill refinement. Practically, Darwin-Claw is a readily deployable, automated framework compatible with mainstream agent systems without requiring manual curation. Extensive experiments on WildClawBench demonstrate that Darwin-Claw yields substantial performance improvements, establishing a controllable and stable pathway for the continual, user-centric adaptation of autonomous agents in real-world ecosystems.


### [219] [RL-Guided Temporal Localization for Dual-Channel Retrieval in Long-Horizon Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23598)
*Dabin Sheng, Zhe Wu, Jinming Zhao, Junliang Xing, Yuanchun Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Time-aware retrieval is crucial for long-horizon LLM agents to effectively leverage ever-growing long-term memory. Existing methods focus on lexical or semantic similarity, leading to target memories being crowded out by temporally mismatched yet semantically similar candidates. Even time-aware approaches inject temporal cues only during memory organization or answer generation, overlooking temporal constraints at the retrieval stage, where candidate selection occurs - the root cause of the displacement. Inspired by the temporal contiguity effect in human episodic memory, we propose TIDER (Temporal Interval-driven Dual-channel Evidence Retrieval), a time-aware agentic retrieval framework. Central to TIDER is a reinforcement learning (RL)-trained temporal interval locator that infers the temporal windows in which the evidence lies. RL optimizes window localization as a discrete, non-differentiable retrieval decision, using composite rewards to convert sparse answer supervision into fine-grained retrieval feedback. These temporal windows first define the temporal retrieval channel; the retrieved candidates are then merged with results from the semantic channel and reranked by proximity to the windows. This temporal grounding enables TIDER to retrieve temporally aligned evidence that would otherwise be drowned out by the volume of semantically similar yet temporally mismatched memories. With a lightweight 1.7B locator, TIDER achieves state-of-the-art accuracy on both long-horizon memory benchmarks - 61.52% on LifeBench (+8.82%) and 78.96% on LoCoMo (+3.66%) - which indicates that temporal cues are crucial for pinpointing target evidence amid a vast pool of semantically similar historical events in long-term daily-life memory.


### [220] [From Trajectories to Differentiated Skills: Cognitive Differentiation for Self-Improving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23599)
*Yewei Zhao, Derong Xu, Wei Chen, Yin Xu, Peilun Zhou, Xiaohan Wang, Guojun Yin, Wei Lin, Mingjun Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents tackle complex tasks through reasoning and interaction with external environments. Agent skills provide persistent, transferable capabilities that guide task planning, inform contextual decisions, support tool use, and enable executable procedures. Yet existing skill-evolution methods largely organize knowledge around skill artifacts, overlooking the functional heterogeneity within individual skills and its implications for knowledge maintenance and use. Inspired by cognitive science, we propose Cognitive Differentiation in Skill Evolution (CDSE), which evolves a unified skill through four functionally distinct yet coordinated components: Meta, Experience, Tool, and Script. CDSE extracts evidence-grounded semantic gradients from successful and failed trajectories, consolidates them by functional role, and applies component-specific updates and validation with cross-component coordination and adaptive invocation. An analysis of 500 top-ranked skill packages shows that 96.8% contain knowledge spanning at least two functional roles. Across six benchmarks, three agent models, and two execution harnesses, CDSE achieves the highest scores in all 36 settings, improving over the Base agent by 7.6 percentage points and the strongest competing method in each setting by 2.6 points on average. Ablation studies further support the importance of functional differentiation and adaptive invocation.


### [221] [GEMS: Molecular Structure Elucidation as Learned Search over Isomer Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23617)
*Utku Umur Acikalin, Yingheng Wang, Goncalo J. Gouveia, Tyler J. Schwertfeger, Frank C. Schroeder, Carla P. Gomes*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Identifying small-molecule structures from molecular formulas and tandem mass spectra remains a bottleneck in untargeted metabolomics and natural-product discovery. Even when database retrieval or de novo prediction misses the exact structure, the resulting candidates can provide starting points for exploring nearby structures through a few bond edits. We introduce GEMS (Guided Editing of Molecular Structures), which refines candidates through learned 2-switch bond edits that preserve the molecular formula and each atom's total valence. GEMS uses MIST, a pretrained spectrum-to-fingerprint model, to predict fingerprint probabilities once per spectrum. A neural policy compares each candidate's fingerprint with these predictions, links present features to the atoms and bonds that generate them, and scores all valid edits in one forward pass. The policy is trained on shortest edit paths between structures, so it can be pretrained on PubChem isomers without spectra and then fine-tuned with spectrum-predicted fingerprints. At inference, GEMS searches from multiple initial candidates matching the supplied molecular formula and ranks candidates by fingerprint agreement and multi-start consensus. With the target structure excluded from PubChem initialization, three edit rounds achieve Hit@1/Hit@10 of 29.64%/50.81% on NPLIB1 and 30.22%/41.33% on MassSpecGym, exceeding published baselines under comparable settings. On NPLIB1, GEMS achieves higher identification accuracy in about 3.3 minutes end to end, compared with 26.2 hours for our FRIGID run. GEMS also improves identification from generated candidates, including those refined by FRIGID. Experiments with exact target fingerprints identify fingerprint prediction error as a bottleneck in target recovery and final ranking. Deeper search recovers additional targets without retraining, with larger gains from weaker initial candidates.


### [222] [OwlSight: A Panoramic Monitor for Chain-of-Thought Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23670)
*Lewen Yan, Yanxu Zhu, Jie Zhang, Jing Shao*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) monitoring offers actionable evidence for oversight AI systems, with the potential to enable early detection and intervention. However, existing approaches are focused on specific tasks, whereas the regulation of AI, particularly reasoning models, requires monitoring a broader range of tasks. We introduce Factor for CoT monitoring, a strategy that supports customizing monitoring requirements in the prompt, which enables a single monitor to cover different tasks by relying on different factors. Furthermore, we introduce two types of factors, fine-grained and coarse-grained (e.g., monitoring whether a specific email address has been leaked versus monitoring privacy leakage), to accommodate diverse user monitoring needs. Based on this, we develop a CoT monitor named OwlSight that relies solely on factor to monitor behaviors related to multiple dimensions, including capability, safety, and trustworthiness. Experiments across 15,971 reasoning trajectories show that OwlSight surpasses general-purpose LLMs by 1.32-30.57 and 11.71-27.81 points at fine and coarse granularity, respectively, and achieves state-of-the-art results on OpenAI's CoT monitorability benchmark. It also generalizes to unseen models' CoTs and remains robust to Factor rephrasings, CoT compression, and obfuscation, offering a promising paradigm for CoT monitoring that can contribute to the oversight of frontier AI.


### [223] [Beyond Large Language Models: A Mental World Model Framework for Mental Modeling, Simulation, Evolution, and Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23722)
*Qingshi Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Understanding and simulating how subjective cognition and mental states emerge, evolve, and shape behavior is essential for advancing artificial intelligence toward deeper social cognition and general intelligence. Existing large language models (LLMs) and Theory of Mind (ToM) methods primarily infer mental states from linguistic information, lacking unified mechanisms for subjective world construction, cognitive-psychological interactions, dynamic evolution, and historical dependencies. This paper introduces the Mental World Model (MWM) and its implementation, the Multi-Branch Mental Evolution Architecture (MMEA), which explicitly constructs subjective worlds, models interactions between cognition and mental states, and simulates continuous mental evolution shaped by historical experiences. On DynToM and RecToM, MMEA achieves 96.48% and 94.00% accuracy, respectively, outperforming leading LLMs and specialized ToM methods. Additional experiments demonstrate improvements of 28.7, 39.3, and 16.6 percentage points in mental state and behavior prediction, long-sequence prediction, and implicit mental state inference, respectively. The results demonstrate that MMEA provides an interpretable framework for continuous mental modeling, enabling AI to move beyond surface-level semantic representations to infer individuals' underlying mental states from linguistic information and dynamically simulate their internal cognitive processes and evolution over time. MWM also provides a promising foundation for causal reasoning, personalized AI, sustained human-AI interaction, and future advances toward artificial general intelligence (AGI).


### [224] [DrugHarness: An Evolvable Agent Harness for Computational Drug Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23791)
*Yuxing Chen, Zhisheng Zhang, Cheng Tang, Wei Li, Minghui Zhang, Ziyan Huang, Shaocong Wei, Jiawei Chen, Junru Hu, Hongze Zhu, Jianghan Shen, Min Cen, Xinyuan Zhu, Yaxue Zhao, Jubo Wang, Yuqiang Li, Chun-Mei Feng, Zongyuan Ge, Rui Su, Jie Xu, Yirong Chen, Xi Xu, Ming Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Computational drug discovery requires agents to compose scientific operations and adapt to changing evidence and constraints. Here we introduce DrugHarness, an evolvable agent harness coupling composable scientific execution with conditional skill induction. Its environment integrates over 80 scientific operations, biomedical resources and task-specific code, preserving associations among candidates, results and artifacts. From execution trajectories and outcome feedback, DrugHarness induces action hypotheses with observable applicability conditions, recommended actions and supporting evidence. An independent review agent evaluates candidate skills derived from these hypotheses through source verification and, when needed, targeted execution trials. Admitted skills guide subsequent tasks and are revised through feedback. Evaluation on MolBench and SMDD-Bench demonstrates capabilities in molecular screening, optimization and constrained design. Without evolution, DrugHarness exceeds reported MolClaw-CC results by 10.8 percentage points in affinity-comparison accuracy and 55% in mean top-three active hits on MolBench. Retrospective evaluation on skill-development tasks shows that evolution increases Qwen's affinity-comparison accuracy from 62.2% to 81.1% and optimization success from 79.5% to 87.2%. In a prospective HPK1 study, DrugHarness achieves a more favorable median docking score than Biomni among system-specific candidates. A synthesized compound achieves a cellular IC50 of 14.19 nM, a 2.64-fold potency improvement over the best input reference, with activity competitive with two selected compounds designed by experienced medicinal chemists. These results support conditional skill evolution for scientific agents and demonstrate a workflow connecting computational drug design with experimental validation.


### [225] [FuTransformer: FP8 prefill and INT4 decode for Llama-style inference on Hopper](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23796)
*Wei Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Transformer prefill reuses weights across tokens and is dominated by matrix multiplication and activation data movement; single-token decode has limited reuse and is dominated by weight and KV-cache reads. We present FUTRANSFORMER, a CUDA implementation of a Llama-style Transformer block for NVIDIA Hopper GPUs. It applies CODA's GEMM-plus-epilogue approach to inference, adding direct KV-cache placement, folding the fixed RMSNorm gain into weights, writing FP8 output directly with delayed scaling, and supporting prefill without padding. Decode uses INT4 projection weights and an INT4 KV-cache copy. Weights and keys follow Tensor Core fragment layouts for direct dequantization into MMA operands without full intermediate tiles. A block-diagonal Hadamard transform reduces cache quantization error from outlier channels. On one H100, prefill is up to 1.48x faster than compiled PyTorch FP8 and reaches 1.29 PFLOP/s. For gate/up projection with SwiGLU at the 8B shape, our BF16 path is 1.13x faster than CODA. Our FP8 path is 2.02-2.18x faster than the evaluated BF16 CODA path. Across 8B and 70B shapes at batches 1-32, INT4 decode is 1.3-2.4x faster than the faster of PyTorch's BF16 and INT4 blocks at each point: 2.1-3.3x faster than compiled, CUDA-graphed BF16 and 1.3-6.3x faster than the same block using PyTorch's INT4 projection kernel, which targets a single query row and loses to BF16 at batch 32. It is also 31-51% faster than our FP8 path. Complete Llama-3.1-8B decode takes 2.8 ms per step at batch one, versus 8.1 ms for the compiled transformers implementation. Decoding every WikiText-2 token through the INT4 weight path raises perplexity from 6.24 to 7.23 with round-to-nearest weights or 6.99 with GPTQ in the same format; the rotated INT4 cache adds 0.20. The kernels match a quantized reference computation within 0.001 perplexity.


### [226] [Beyond SAEs: Consistent Concept Discovery with Sparse Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23801)
*Jinho Choi, Hyesu Lim, Jaegul Choo, Steffen Schneider*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) explain foundation model representations by decomposing activations into sparse combinations of learned dictionary atoms. However, the learned dictionaries can vary across training runs, limiting the reliability and reproducibility of SAE-based analyses. We propose Consistent Sparse Dictionary Learning (ConSDL), which replaces the standard linear encoder with per-sample sparse coding. This is motivated by our synthetic analysis showing that coefficients from a single linear encoder pass are suboptimal compared with those obtained by per-sample optimization, suggesting that sparse coding is a reliable mechanism for dictionary learning. Across 16 synthetic settings, ConSDL achieves near-perfect dictionary and coefficient recovery. These gains translate to state-of-the-art cross-seed consistency across four vision and language settings, where ConSDL improves dictionary and coefficient consistency by 17.6% and 35.9%, respectively, over the average of six SAE baselines.


### [227] [Self-Building Labs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23826)
*Jiaru Bai, Yuchi Zhao, Han Hao, Miroslav Bogdanovic, Yang Cao, Varinia Bernales, Alán Aspuru-Guzik*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Self-driving laboratories (SDLs) accelerate scientific discovery by automating experimental execution and optimization. AI agents can increasingly propose new experiments, but existing SDLs can only run the procedures they were built for. Adapting them to a new experiment still takes extensive manual engineering, which makes laboratory flexibility the bottleneck for scaling discovery. We introduce the self-building laboratory (SBL), which designs, configures, and operates its own laboratory setup given a high-level scientific goal. SBL starts from whatever already exists, from an empty room to a working SDL, and builds the missing capabilities: the lab layout, its digital twin, robot skills, and experimental workflows. We demonstrate the system working across different robots and setups, scaling from a single workstation to laboratories with several robots working together. We validate SBL's end-to-end capabilities through a real-world quantum-dot synthesis experiment. By shifting the role of human researchers from manually configuring and programming device automation to specifying scientific objectives, SBL makes scientific automation more accessible, flexible, and scalable. Project website: this https URL


### [228] [AI-Driven Reproducibility and Analytical Robustness in Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23845)
*Shicheng Liu, Krista Opsahl-Ong, Andrew Drozdov, Julian Killingback, Arnav Singhvi, Ronak Pradeep, Michael Bendersky, James C. Dickerson, Monica S. Lam*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Reproducible and analytically robust research findings are essential to scientific progress, but evaluating these properties typically requires costly manual effort. We investigate whether LLM agents can help automate reproduction and robustness analysis, using a medical case study grounded in the Surveillance, Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER) database, a widely used U.S. cancer registry underlying tens of thousands of published studies. First, we introduce SEER-Repro, a benchmark of 20 published SEER-based papers together with the data and resources required to reproduce their findings. Second, we create an AI agent that successfully reproduces 77.4% of 2,190 atomic claims, while providing executable baselines for further analysis. Third, we develop an agentic analysis pipeline to test the robustness of the medical findings by identifying analytical choices underlying each finding, proposing defensible alternatives, and rerunning the analysis to determine whether those choices affect the conclusion. Across published papers in SEER-Repro, our pipeline identifies potentially consequential analytical choices in 9.5% of fully reproduced findings and flags them for expert review. These results show how LLM agents can support scientific review by reproducing findings, systematically testing their robustness, and directing expert attention toward potentially consequential analytical choices.


<div id='cs.DB'></div>

# cs.DB [[Back]](#toc)

### [229] [NL2SQL with Iterative Self-Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22968)
*Anjali Vemula, Akshat Dasula*

Main category: cs.DB

Abstract: Bridging the gap between human intent and machine logic remains a critical challenge in data access for non-technical users, yet Large Language Models (LLMs) remain unreliable: they hallucinate schema elements, generate syntactically invalid queries, or fail to capture user intent. These limitations are especially critical in domains such as insurance, where KPIs, regulatory constraints, and legacy relational systems demand high precision. We introduce a multi-agent, persona-driven NL2SQL framework that combines (1) domain relevance detection, (2) schema-grounded knowledge construction, (3) KPI-aware SQL synthesis, (4) syntax-level self-repair using sqlite execution feedback, and (5) semantic consistency checking via SQL-to-NL reverse engineering. Our system significantly reduces query hallucination and improves semantic fidelity compared to direct LLM prompting. Evaluated on 60 queries (50 from a HuggingFace text-to-SQL dataset and 10 custom domain queries), the system achieves 90% correctness using Mistral across seven coordinated LLM calls showing that this approach can substantially increase the reliability of NL2SQL pipelines in high-stakes environments.


### [230] [Data Provenance Aggregation and Structural Descriptors for Query-Template Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22705)
*Paulo Pintor, Rogério Costa, José Moreira*

Main category: cs.DB

Abstract: Runtime query similarity identification supports several activities, including workload analysis, identifying recurring behaviour, auditing, debugging, and anomaly detection. Existing methods for evaluating query similarity mostly rely on SQL text, execution plans, or result values but do not account for input data or how results are derived during query execution. This paper proposes a novel approach using data provenance for query-template identification. We construct query-level profiles that capture explicit data dependencies, occurrence frequencies, and structural properties by aggregating tuple-level Lineage and Why-provenance across entire query results. We also derive Provenance Structural Descriptors (PSD), which summarise these profiles using a small set of numerical features. We evaluate our proposals using these profiles to identify query templates over \(12{,}500\) distinct query instances generated from parametrised TPC-DS query templates. The experimental evaluation shows that query templates can be identified solely from their provenance footprints, and that accuracy improves as more historical data becomes available. It also shows that PSD achieve high classification accuracy with limited training history and low test-processing cost.


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [231] [Towards Adaptive and Real-Survey-Aligned Simulated Urban Mobility Behaviour of Generative Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22407)
*Yves Bru, Kamaldeep Singh Oberoi, Benoit Gaudou*

Main category: cs.MA

Abstract: In existing agent-based simulations of multimodal urban mobility, agents, representing humans, choose their travel modes either using discrete choice or machine learning models fitted on structured travel survey data, or with rules explicitly written by the domain experts. Such approaches fix a priori the agents' possible behaviour space. Hence, a factor absent from variables or rules cannot affect agents' decisions. In reality, choosing a transport mode is a multidimensional decision in which objective and subjective factors intertwine and change over time. Integrating such complexity into a city-scale multimodal mobility simulation remains a challenge. Generative agents (GAs) built on large language models promise to respond to this challenge because they carry decision heuristics that travel surveys do not record. However, to the best of our knowledge, aligning their decisions with real household travel surveys remains an open question. In this paper, we build an agent-based model of daily multimodal mobility for Toulouse (France). We compare the performance of fifteen decision-makers (simple baselines, models fitted on the survey, GAs with minimal and tuned prompts, and a typed classifier) on the real household travel survey. Our results show that GAs with tuned prompts come close to the fitted models on the modal split of a simulated day without transport disruptions, but not trip by trip. GAs also react when unforeseen events occur. The recently released Jev-1.13 TypeSafe classifier gives surprisingly great results, showing that generating output text is not necessary. The results presented in this paper would be of interest to transport policy makers and service providers looking to apply generative agent-based modelling for improving urban mobility services.


<div id='physics.flu-dyn'></div>

# physics.flu-dyn [[Back]](#toc)

### [232] [How the elastic axis folds the transonic flutter boundary: shock energy transfer and conformally calibrated active learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23298)
*Ashray Saxena, Sharad Shrivastava*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

Abstract: Isogai's case A, a pitch-plunge aerofoil whose elastic axis lies half a chord ahead of the leading edge, has a folded transonic flutter boundary in inviscid flow: near the bottom of the transonic dip several flutter speeds coexist at one Mach number. We map this boundary, and the family obtained by moving the elastic axis, with coupled unsteady Euler simulations, a Gaussian-process surrogate of the damping coefficient trained by batch active learning, and split conformal prediction on independent random simulations, which turns the surrogate into stability calls with a distribution-free error rate. Three failure modes of time-domain damping labels that leave no trace in the residuals are documented, and the half-degree mean incidence left by the common one-degree start is shown to raise the flutter speed at the dip by 2.8%, more than any numerical setting. With 80 simulations the learned case A boundary labels 64 of 65 independent runs correctly, and calibrated calls are wrong in at most 1.6% of cases. Over Mach number, elastic axis and flutter speed index, 193 simulations and 40 calibration runs show that the fold exists only for elastic axes more than about 0.4 chord ahead of the leading edge; it closes between 0.39 and 0.33 chord ahead, on two meshes. Chordwise energy budgets, closed against the solver loads and the growth rate within a few per cent, explain the boundary. The lower branch is crossed through the reduced frequency at unchanged shock phase. On the upper branch the shock is pinned at the trailing edge, and moving the elastic axis aft brings the pivot of the flutter mode towards the section, so that the damping of the supersonic surface falls faster than the residual shock input and the fold closes.


<div id='cs.CL'></div>

# cs.CL [[Back]](#toc)

### [233] [FederatedBytes: Tokenizer-Free, Byte-Level Small Language Models for Efficient Multimodal Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22906)
*Ali Sarabadani, Massimo Piccardi, Mehran Abolhasan, Farzad Tofigh, Ian Zhou, Justin Lipman*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) is a powerful machine learning framework that allows building a global model from the contributions of multiple, distributed clients, while ensuring the privacy of their local data. However, its heavy computational and communication overheads challenge its use for contemporary AI workloads such as natural language processing (NLP) and multimodal analysis. For this reason, this paper presents a novel small language model that leverages byte-level text and multimodal data processing, enhanced by a linear attention mechanism, to enable manageable Federated Learning of NLP and multimodal analysis tasks. The proposed model, nicknamed FederatedBytes, manages to process text and any other data types, including images, by treating its input as a mere sequence of bytes, while its lean linear attention mechanism allows abating the overall memory and computational footprint. To prove the effectiveness of the proposed model we have carried out an extensive performance evaluation on sentiment analysis tasks in multilingual and multimodal settings over the Multilingual Amazon Reviews and ScienceQA datasets. The experimental results show that FederatedBytes has been able to outperform comparable models such as ByT5 and LSTM, achieving higher accuracy while significantly reducing memory and communication overheads.


### [234] [AuditKIT: A Unified Evaluation Spine for Benchmarking, Judging, and Comparing Large Language Models and Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22511)
*Shreeyans Arora, Utsav Avaiya, Zera Lyngkhoi, Hem Gosalia, Ram Mohan Rao Kadiyala, Vinay Kumar Sankarapu, Pratinav Seth*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Evaluating language models and agents requires combining benchmark accuracy, judge-based assessment, runtime performance, tool-use behavior, and retrieval quality. Differences in interfaces, metric semantics, and trace formats complicate these comparisons and can hide failures in agentic workflows. We present AuditKIT, a source-available evaluation library that records these measurements in one result representation, with direction-aware metric contracts, configuration fingerprints, and extensible metrics and backends. For agents, it provides turn-aware tool-call matching, separates parallel from sequential execution, and distinguishes verified task outcomes from trajectory-level diagnostics. Missing or unobservable evidence is marked unscored rather than counted as failure. For retrieval-augmented generation, it combines ranked retrieval metrics, judged grounding, and deterministic evidence checks. Seven case studies spanning model comparison, serving runtimes, retrieval, tool calling, and interoperability show how answer extraction, runtime behavior, and metric selection change evaluation outcomes.


### [235] [Mitigating Demographic Disparities in Explanation-Human Alignment via Rationale Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22586)
*Suryansh Talukdar, Taha Sarwat, Jonathan Rusert*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: We study explanation fairness in hate speech detection. High classification performance does not always mean that a model produces equally plausible explanations for different demographic groups. Using the HateXplain dataset, we introduce Plausibility-EFG, a metric that measures the largest difference in explanation plausibility across demographic groups by comparing model-generated token attributions with human-annotated rationales. We evaluate BERT, RoBERTa, and ADV-RoBERTa, an adversarially trained model, using Integrated Gradients and SHAP. Our results show that ADV-RoBERTa improves classification performance and alignment between explanations and human rationales while reducing explanation disparities across demographic groups. These improvements are observed with both attribution methods and remain consistent across training, validation, and test splits. We also show that reducing prediction-level bias alone does not eliminate disparities in explanations, suggesting that predictive fairness and explanation fairness are related but distinct objectives. Finally, our fairness-performance analysis shows that stronger adversarial regularization can further reduce Plausibility-EFG, although it may cause a small decrease in macro-F1. Overall, this work presents explanation fairness as an important objective in hate speech moderation and introduces Fair-REF, a framework designed to reduce disparities in model explanations across demographic groups.


### [236] [A Plausible Explanation Is Enough to Mislead Multimodal Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22631)
*Anglin Cai, Zhiqi Wang, Xiaozhi Yang, Yurun Lin, Dongwon Lee, Yuchen Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) increasingly serve as judges in training and evaluation pipelines, often assessing answers together with explanations. Although AI judges are sensitive to textual factors, it remains unclear whether ordinary plausible explanations can sway judgments requiring visual verification. We introduce JUPE, a visual-question-answering (VQA)-style benchmark that fixes answers while varying explanations. Across 14 MLLM judges, ordinary explanations that sound plausible mislead judges, with effects comparable on average to deliberately persuasive arguments. Plain justifications and chain-of-thought rationales reduce rejection of incorrect answers by 12.7 and 13.8 percentage points, respectively. We call this failure plausibility capture. Using signal detection theory, we find a significant mean shift in decision criterion toward acceptance, without a clear change in mean discriminability. Explanations sway judgments across domains, explanation generators, and judging protocols, while tested mitigations offer limited, model-dependent protection. Such shifts can inflate performance estimates when judges evaluate answers with explanations.


### [237] [ToMStories: Interpreting Theory-of-Mind in Synthetic Narratives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22632)
*Melanie Subbiah, Chia-En Jacklyn Tsai, Kathleen McKeown*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Understanding people often requires inferring what they feel or want from how they behave rather than from what they state outright. We ask whether language models can make these inferences, and how they do so. We introduce Theory-of-Mind Stories (ToMStories), an evaluation dataset of ~6,000 short synthetically generated narrative vignettes, each paired with a multiple-choice or open-ended question about a character's emotion, fear, motivation, or emotional wound. These psychological states are conveyed implicitly (showing), explicitly (telling), or with conflict between dialogue and description. Evaluating thirteen open-weight models (1B-72B parameters), we find that the largest models match humans on multiple choice but not open-ended questions. Our analyses point to simplifications models make in both interpretation and generation of implicit psychological states, indicating that synthetic data can help us understand how models perform interpretation, but likely overestimates how well models interpret humans.


### [238] [Measuring Mechanistic Uncertainty in Large Language Models via Causal Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22636)
*Aneesh Komanduri, Majid Afshar, Timothy A. Miller, Yanjun Gao*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in critical domains, where their susceptibility to hallucinations makes reliable and interpretable uncertainty quantification essential. We introduce the concept of mechanistic uncertainty, which characterizes uncertainty through the stability of the internal mechanisms supporting a model's response. We propose the causal robustness margin, defined as the minimum internal intervention required to deviate the model's output, and operationalize it using Jacobian Lens (Jlens) concept steering vectors. Across three open-source models and five question-answering datasets, we show incorrect and uncertain responses exhibit smaller margins, indicating greater sensitivity to internal interventions, and the causal robustness margin complements conventional output-based uncertainty methods. Finally, in a controlled knowledge injection experiment, correctly learned facts show increased margins after training, suggesting that causal robustness provides a mechanistically grounded signal of uncertainty.


### [239] [Explore Separately, Adopt Selectively: Execution-Guided Tool Use over Large Tool Libraries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22688)
*Anna Mazhar, Seiji Maekawa, Nikita Bhutani*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Large language model agents must discover relevant tools from large libraries, e.g., thousands of tools, as new needs arise during task execution. When several candidates could satisfy the same need, execution may be required to identify the relevant one. Investigating candidates in a shared trajectory can leave alternatives unexplored or allow failed attempts to influence subsequent reasoning. To address this, we present BRIA (BRanch-Inspect-Adopt), a simple yet effective agent that temporarily isolates candidate investigations in separate branches. Each branch retrieves and executes tools to investigate its candidate; a task agent inspects the resulting execution evidence and adopts only useful results into its continuing trajectory. BRIA retrieves additional tools as new capability needs arise during task execution. We evaluate BRIA on PlanBench-XL and MCP-Atlas using a shared pool of 1897 tools, three language models, and static and adaptive retrieval baselines. Across all settings, BRIA matches or exceeds the strongest retrieval baseline. On MCP-Atlas, it achieves an average claim score of 89.77, compared with 75.40 for MCP-Zero, a gain of 14.37 percentage points. BRIA also lies on the Pareto frontier for task performance and input-token cost.


### [240] [ARBOR: Locating Reasoning Failures in Long Chains of Thought through Reasoning State Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22717)
*Huihan Li, Siyuan Wang, Naga Vamsi Ramana Dinavahi, Xiaohui Liu, Xiang Ren*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Large reasoning models solve complex problems through long chains of thought, in which prior work has documented failure modes such as overthinking, underthinking, and unsupported deductions. Existing methods detect these failures from local signals: textual similarity to nearby steps, switch tokens, or an LLM's judgment of a step's correctness. However, what a step contributes to the solution depends on the state that earlier steps have established, so identifying failures requires a global view that tracks that state across the trajectory. Therefore, we propose ARBOR, a reasoning-failure attribution framework that represents each trajectory as a tree of reasoning states, built as each step creates, verifies, prunes, or revisits a state, and attributes each failure to a relation between a step and the state preceding it. Using ARBOR, we refine the three failure modes into distinct subtypes that harm reasoning through different mechanisms and at different costs. We find that the tree state ARBOR maintains before a step separates failure steps from the at-risk steps at which the failure did not occur. We discuss how ARBOR integrates with existing mitigations at inference time and in training data. Code, data and interactive examples are available at this https URL .
