For precise control, the renderer is conditioned on an explicit defocus map and uses defocus-bias attention, which lets pixels at similar defocus share information without interference from pixels at other depths; in-focus content stays unchanged, and editing the map yields tilt-shift and two focal planes. To further test generalization to unseen cameras, we collect 3CReal, a three-camera benchmark of 89 pairs. FlashReFocus achieves the best perceptual fidelity for zero-shot bokeh rendering on EBB! and 3CReal, refocuses 148.3x faster than GenRefocus, and takes about half a second per further edit, 462x faster.


### [338] [Rethinking the design space of Vanilla Pixel Diffusion Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23854)
*Soumik Mukhopadhyay, Long Mai, Abhinav Shrivastava, Aniruddha Mahapatra*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Pixel-space diffusion models have recently re-emerged as a compelling alternative to latent diffusion, avoiding the lossy latent bottleneck and separately trained autoencoder. JiT shows that a vanilla diffusion transformer can generate directly in pixel space by replacing the commonly used velocity prediction ($\boldsymbol{u}$-prediction) with clean image prediction ($\boldsymbol{x_0}$-prediction). In this work, we revisit this choice and introduce MFlow, an adaptive parameterization that learns to interpolate between $\boldsymbol{x_0}$- and $\boldsymbol{u}$-prediction across principal directions of the data, learned end-to-end with diffusion training. We find that the preferred parameterization varies systematically with the data representation and model capacity -- notably, increasing model capacity shifts preference toward velocity prediction. We evaluate MFlow across ImageNet, UCF-101 video, and large-scale text-to-image generation, demonstrating competitive or improved generative performance across diverse settings. In addition, we find that vanilla pixel diffusion transformers, irrespective of parameterization, retain a persistent high-frequency deficit relative to real images. To address this, we introduce ConvRefiner, a lightweight convolutional module trained post-hoc with an adversarial loss while keeping the diffusion backbone frozen. With only a small amount of additional training, ConvRefiner restores the high-frequency statistics of generated images while preserving FID. Project webpage: this https URL .


### [339] [Towards Reconstructing Geographically Diverse Architecture with 3D Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22514)
*Nicky Kriplani, Yiwen Zhang, Angelina Wang, Hadar Averbuch-Elor*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Internet-scale photo collections of real-world landmarks have driven progress in 3D computer vision yet remain highly challenging for modern 3D foundation models (3DFMs) that estimate scene structure in a single feed-forward pass. In this work, we introduce ArchWorld, a benchmark of 3D architectural landmarks with high geographic coverage and rich metadata. We use this benchmark to conduct a thorough error analysis of current 3DFMs to understand where they fall short and how they can be improved. We examine socially relevant disparities in model error and find that model performance varies by geographic region; however, unlike typical fairness regimes, the trends only partially match training data representation. Our investigation reveals that the observed geographic disparities reflect not only differences in data representation and scene size but also differences in region-specific architectural features and background distractors that make scenes from different regions inherently vary in difficulty. Leveraging insights from these model failings, we introduce an intervention scheme that improves 3DFM performance while simultaneously reducing geographic disparities.


### [340] [Learning the Shared Latent Structure of Cryo-EM Inverse Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22523)
*Nithin Chalapathi, Theo Sternlieb, Ethan Han, Sonya M. Hanson, Pilar Cossio, Aditi S. Krishnapriyan, Ritwik Gupta*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Understanding biomolecular function requires characterizing three-dimensional structure and conformational variability, for which cryogenic electron microscopy (cryo-EM) is a powerful tool. Cryo-EM produces noisy two-dimensional particle images in which molecular structure, particle pose, and imaging conditions are jointly encoded. Recovering biological information from these observations spans inverse problems including pose estimation, heterogeneity analysis, and reconstruction. These tasks are usually addressed independently despite shared protein structure and image formation, motivating a common representation across inverse problems. Since biologically meaningful differences may be spatially localized, we study self-distillation with complementary global and masked patch objectives. Because experimental dataset discrimination can reflect acquisition-specific cues, we develop controlled synthetic and experimental evaluations and find that structural discrimination benefits from dense spatial features. We introduce the EMber family of vision transformers, pre-trained on >50 million experimental particle images from 214 EMPIAR datasets together with simulated projections from 268,000 molecular assemblies. EMber improves upon existing methods at conformational and compositional discrimination by 14.85 and 14.26 percentage points, estimates conformational populations to within 1.59pp error with a linear probe, and produces particle assignments yielding reference-quality reconstructions in fewer epochs. We demonstrate how to learn representations that capture biological information for use across multiple cryo-EM inverse problems.


### [341] [Shift-Reliance Alignment: Ranking Vision-Language Models by the Cues They Trust](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22610)
*Faisal Mohammad, Mohamed Younis*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) are usually chosen from leaderboards, which give one ranking for every application. On questions about objects that a scene suggests but does not contain, however, the model with the lowest error on the POPE hallucination benchmark is not the one with the lowest error on such questions. For yes/no questions about object presence, we show that the best model for a target domain can be predicted without running any candidate on that domain, given 650 to 1,200 labeled target items that calibrate a detector. We measure each model's reliance on a cue, a feature such as scene context or text in the image, by editing 100 labeled source images so that the cue suggests the wrong answer, and comparing with a matched control edit. Reliance predicts which VLMs are misled by physical sticker attacks (Spearman .93 on nine models not used to design the score) and which hallucinate on unedited COCO photographs, beyond what their tendency to answer yes explains. Shift-Reliance Alignment (SRA) scores a model by its source error on the labeled source questions plus its reliance multiplied by the shift. The shift is the proportion of target questions on which the cue misleads, and a detector estimates it on the unlabeled target images. SRA selects the model with the lowest score. In pre-registered tests on new images where most questions mislead, it lowered the error of the selected model from 30.4% under selection by source error to 18.8% on Objects365 and OpenImages but, against its prediction, not on COCO. On CueDial, a benchmark we release that varies how often a cue misleads, selection by model confidence falls more than 40 percentage points behind the best model when overlaid text denies a visible object, and SRA stays within 3 points.


### [342] [Replay Pruning and Few-Bit Integer Inference for Hardware-Efficient Speed-Sign Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22811)
*Se Un Park, Kyungkeon Chung, Huigeol Kim, Junyoung Park*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Speed-limit sign recognition on an automotive camera system-on-chip (SoC) must fit a detector and a classifier (the reader) into the memory, arithmetic, and energy budgets of the device. In our two-stage recognizer, the detector, a YOLO-family network with ReLU and hard-sigmoid operators, is compressed by channel replay: an iterative pruning cascade identifies the channels to remove, the recorded deletions are replayed on the original task-trained network, the parent, and one continuous knowledge-distillation recovery follows. On a YOLOv5n, replay removes 60.3% of the parameters at the accuracy of the parent and, in validation mAP50-95 over five matched seeds, outperforms iterative pruning and training from scratch, and its per-step variant (per-step replay) is the most accurate pruning method. On a YOLOv8n, replay and per-step replay outperform every other method and the unpruned parent. The reader, a five-layer CNN with 18k parameters, and the post-processing of the YOLOv5n and YOLOv8n heads use integer arithmetic only. With 4-bit weights and 8-bit activations (W4A8), the reader matches FP32 accuracy in 12.1 KB of on-chip memory, and an executable streaming model reproduces the INT8 class predictions of its runtime on all 9,850 test crops. On a Jetson AGX Orin, INT8 is faster than FP16 only in the implicit formulation of TensorRT, and the replay detector runs fastest on the deep-learning accelerator (DLA). Trained on public data only, the combined system of 0.72 M parameters recognizes 95.2% of the signs of a held-out automotive camera at 90% precision and 94 frames per second.


### [343] [How Murky Before a VLM Knows It Cannot See? Accuracy, Confidence and Abstention on a Physical Visibility Axis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22981)
*Mochamad Rizal Fauzan, Irgi Surya*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Robustness studies of vision-language models (VLMs) grade degradation on nominal severity scales ("level 1-5") that carry no physical units, and they rarely ask whether a model's confidence follows the loss of visibility. We measure accuracy, internal (token-probability) confidence, verbalized confidence and an explicit abstain option on a visibility axis in metres: fog follows the Koschmieder model, an underwater arm scales the absorption spectrum of pure water, both are applied with a monocular metric depth map, and the visibility range V is swept over eight log-spaced levels from 250 m to 1.95 m. On 1000 COCO scenes and five small open VLMs (2-5 B parameters), accuracy on a boxed-object identification task falls from near its clean value to chance within a narrow band (half-way visibility V_acc of 12.1-15.9 m in fog and 20.5-38.6 m under water) while, for the four models that are confident on clean images, the confidence stays high: at V=1.95 m in fog their accuracy is at chance but their mean internal confidence is 0.68-0.84 and their verbalized confidence 0.61-1.00, a blind-spot width W=log10(V_acc/V_aware) of at least 0.79 decades, i.e. a factor of 6.2 in visibility. The remaining model is under-confident throughout and its internal confidence follows accuracy. An explicit abstain option follows accuracy when it names the reason (W from -0.21 to -0.06 in fog) but, for one model, lags with a generic "I don't know" (W up to 0.18). ImageNet-C fog severities 1-5 correspond to only 55-110 m of physical visibility, outside the failure band, and leave the accuracy of the models within 5 percentage points of its clean value. Blank-image and shuffled-image controls, a prior-matched answer set and a 200-image pilot that preceded the study are reported. Findings are descriptive: one draw of 1000 images, depth of assumed scale, and models of 2-5 B parameters.


### [344] [Can the Parts Fool the Test? Counterfactual Pairing Cycles for Relational OOD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23248)
*Yibo Zhou, Yirong Xiang*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Relational out-of-distribution errors are easy to describe and hard to evaluate cleanly. An image may be natural, a caption may be fluent, and all mentioned objects may be common, yet the caption may describe the wrong relation in the image. Many evaluations intended to test this behavior can be solved without checking the pairing at all, because negatives differ in caption style, template, length, object frequency, or image statistics. We propose counterfactual pairing cycles, a blocked evaluation primitive that reuses the same two images and the same two captions on both sides of the comparison, changing only which image is paired with which caption. This makes image-only, text-only, and additive component shortcuts cancel exactly in finite samples. The same contrast also gives a training loss for relational outlier exposure and a diagnostic for bilinear vision-language scores. Across synthetic controls, GQA, and COCO, cycles separate coarse image-caption matching from directional role-binding failures, while also identifying geometric size relations that remain strongly visible in global features. In a shortcut-contaminated COCO outlier-exposure stress test, a model selected by a near-perfect shortcut validation AUROC retains substantial relational signal but remains below cycle training on exact cycles; cycle training improves CycleAcc from 0.882 to 0.927. The main lesson is simple: to test whether a model understands a pairing, the benchmark should keep the parts fixed, change the pairing, and ask only about coupling. The official implementation is available online.


### [345] [Look, Reason, Draw a Region: Recursive Refinement for Spatial Context Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23302)
*Wajeeha Tahir, Anna Tegon, Lionnus Kesting, Gamze İslamoğlu, Kristóf Karacs, Luca Benini, Thorir Mar Ingolfsson*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Spatial context prediction requires visual grounding and spatial reasoning to identify vacant regions relative to objects in a scene. Spatial relationships and physical constraints remain challenging for large vision-language models (VLMs). Recursive reasoning has demonstrated strong performance on discrete and abstract reasoning tasks, motivating its use for improving spatial context prediction in natural images. Weight sharing enables higher accuracy with a small trainable parameter count. We introduce Spatial-TRM, a compact recursive model with frozen vision-language features, a convex region decoder, and just 11.2M trainable parameters. This weight-tied reasoner supports six spatial relationships, three reference frames, and open-vocabulary anchor objects. On the RoboSpatial-Home benchmark, Spatial-TRM achieves 29.51% context accuracy, a gain of 9.02 percentage points (44.0% relative) over a separately trained single-step model with the same parameter count. Including the frozen encoders, the model has 877.8M total parameters, approximately $15\times$ fewer than the reported 13B size of RoboPoint. These results show that repeated refinement improves spatial context prediction from pretrained vision-language features at a fixed trainable parameter count on this benchmark.


### [346] [A Language-Informed Loss for Visual Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23653)
*Suchir Agarwal, Keshigeyan Chandrasegaran, Ziyu Chen, Kyle Sargent, Juan Carlos Niebles, Li Fei-Fei, Jiajun Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: End-to-end visual generation with pixel-space loss has recently achieved competitive performance without the need for learned image tokenizers. In this work, we study whether language-informed supervision can provide a richer training signal than pixel loss for visual generation. We introduce LILIE (pronounced "lily"), a Language-Informed and Lossless Image Encoder. LILIE is an invertible image transform trained such that a vision-language model can recover captions from noised features. The encoder uses a normalizing flow transformer trained jointly with a vision-language model via next token prediction. We then use the encoder to extract a simple language-informed loss for text-to-image generation. We train JiT models with LILIE loss, reducing gFD by 14 points (66.84 $\to$ 53.01) against a pixel-only baseline on the GPIC dataset. Our code, encoders, and generative models are available at this https URL .


### [347] [An Efficient Approach to Beetle Damage Detection of Banana Using Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23661)
*Md. Mehedi Hasan Naeem, Ishtiyak Ahmmad Araf, Md. Arefin Haque Mahir, Tushar Kanti Saha*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Visible surface damage of banana fruits by Basilepta subcostata (the banana fruit scarring beetle) can reduce their market value. Here, manual inspection of Banana fruits is subjective and difficult to scale. This study presents a lightweight framework based on YOLOv8n deep learning model for locating individual bananas and classifying their visible surface condition as healthy or unhealthy. The term beetle damage in the paper title is used operationally for the locally observed scar-like surface condition; the model does not identify a specific beetle species or biological cause. A custom dataset of 2,473 images containing 5,827 annotated banana instances was prepared from local-market imagery in Bangladesh. The reported experiment used an 80/20 protocol with 1,982 training images and 491 test images. A pretrained YOLOv8n model was trained at 640x640 resolution for 50 epochs with a batch size of 4. On the test partition, it achieved 81.36% precision, 74.30% recall, 77.67% F1-score, 87.04% mAP@0.50 and 59.33% mAP@0.50:0.95. A YOLOv8s comparison gave slightly lower F1 and mAP while using a substantially larger checkpoint. The YOLOv8n checkpoint occupied 5.96 MB and required 6.54 ms/image for inference on an NVIDIA Tesla T4. The results support YOLOv8n as a compact baseline for instance-level banana surface inspection within the studied acquisition conditions.


<div id='cs.IT'></div>

# cs.IT [[Back]](#toc)

### [348] [GPU-Accelerated Robust MA--RIS Pre-Optimization for Communication and Target Illumination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22431)
*Tran Le Dung, Ho Viet Duc Luong*

Main category: cs.IT

Abstract: Movable antennas (MAs) and reconfigurable intelligent surfaces (RISs) offer strong spatial control for integrated sensing and communication (ISAC), but repeatedly adapting MA positions and RIS phases to each channel realization can be impractical. This letter develops a GPU-accelerated robust pre-optimization framework that jointly selects slow-timescale MA geometry and RIS phases and returns a reusable Pareto set balancing expected worst-user rate and transmit-side worst-target illumination. The illumination objective measures directional transmitted power rather than complete echo-detection or parameter-estimation performance. The proposed Regime-Aware Multiobjective Pre-optimization (RAMP) preserves regime-specific Pareto populations, regulates cross-regime mating using Pareto-survival feedback, and constructs a worst-regime robust archive. Numerical results demonstrate competitive robust Pareto quality against the considered evolutionary baselines while avoiding online physical re-optimization.


<div id='cond-mat.mtrl-sci'></div>

# cond-mat.mtrl-sci [[Back]](#toc)

### [349] [Scalable long-range machine learning interatomic potentials with electrical response for atomistic material modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23643)
*Yicheng Chen, Xiaoyun Yu, Hyejeong Song, Xintian Wang, Zihui Li, Zixi Xia, Junmin Chen, Peichen Zhong*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

Abstract: Accurate atomistic simulations of materials under electric fields require long-range electrostatics and electrical-response modeling at length scales beyond the reach of conventional first-principles methods. Yet these capabilities remain difficult to reconcile with the scalability of machine-learning interatomic potentials (MLIPs). Strictly local models enable efficient spatial parallelization but neglect long-range interactions, whereas existing electrostatic extensions introduce global dependencies that hinder distributed computation. Here, we overcome this limitation by analytically separating the long-range electrostatic forces and Born effective charges (BECs) into a global electrostatics calculation and strictly local derivatives of learned latent charges. This formulation preserves the spatial decomposability of equivariant MLIPs while enabling distributed evaluation of energies, forces, stresses, and BECs across different boundary conditions. Implemented as DPA4C-LR on the \texttt{nvalchemi} platform, the framework scales to 5.2 million atoms on four GPUs at approximately twice the computational cost of its short-range backbone. Trained on energies and forces, DPA4C-LR accurately infers BECs and reproduces the field-dependent infrared spectra of water. We further demonstrate its capabilities by recovering the characteristic slim polarization hysteresis of relaxor \ce{Pb(Mg_{1/3}Nb_{2/3})O3}, which is obscured by finite-size effects in smaller simulations; predicting a room-temperature field-driven ionic conductivity of $6.8 \pm 0.7$~mS/cm in amorphous \ce{Li_{0.9}NbO_{0.9}Cl_{4.1}}; and modeling interfacial thermal transport across Si/diamond interfaces with slab-corrected electrostatics. By combining long-range physics, electrical-response inference, and distributed computation, this framework extends predictive atomistic modeling toward device-relevant length scales.


<div id='cs.SC'></div>

# cs.SC [[Back]](#toc)

### [350] [Diverse Reasoning Paths Matter: Symbolic Equivalence Augmentation for Enhancing RL Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22665)
*Xiaoou Liu, Tiejin Chen, Hanyang Chen, Yuan Ling, Hua Wei*

Main category: cs.SC

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has emerged as a powerful paradigm for improving the reasoning capabilities of LLMs. However, the role of supervised fine-tuning (SFT) data in enabling effective downstream RL remains underexplored. In this paper, we investigate a key hypothesis: the diversity of reasoning steps in chain-of-thought (CoT) during SFT is a critical enabler of RLVR performance. To test this hypothesis, we introduce EquivAug, a data augmentation framework that uses symbolic equivalence transformations to generate diverse yet provably correct reasoning paths. By converting CoT solutions into formal symbolic representations, applying equivalence transformations, and reconstructing natural-language explanations, EquivAug produces training data that differs in reasoning steps rather than surface-level lexical variation. Experiments on GSM8K and FOLIO across model scales demonstrate that EquivAug consistently achieves the highest reasoning path diversity after SFT and the strongest accuracy after GRPO. Our results establish that CoT diversity during SFT is positively correlated with downstream RLVR performance.


<div id='cs.DS'></div>

# cs.DS [[Back]](#toc)

### [351] [Hard Instances on Demand](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22637)
*Jamie Fravel, Martin Koutecký, Pavlo Tsitsei*

Main category: cs.DS

Abstract: The conclusions of an experimental study of algorithms are only as good as the instances they are run on. Benchmark libraries for well-studied problems took decades to curate, while evaluations of new problems often fall back on random instances, which are known to be misleading: they may say more about the random generator than about the algorithmic question at hand. We treat instance generation as an optimization problem: parameterize the instance space, take the solver's running time as the objective, and let a black-box optimizer search for hard instances. We develop a widely applicable workflow built on the Nevergrad optimization platform and demonstrate it on four NP-hard problems: satisfiability, traveling salesperson, Hamiltonian cycle, and non-permutation flow-shop scheduling. The search needs only the solver, an instance encoder, and a compute budget. Our evolved instances beat the hardest instance of a budget-matched random draw in all four case studies, by an order of magnitude or more in three. They are often competitive with long-curated benchmark sets: our SAT champion is harder (for a three-solver portfolio) than 78% of the 498 SAT Competition formulas in its size class; our 20-city TSP champions take several times longer than the hardest measured classical TSPLIB instance (229 cities); and our flow-shop champion takes 238.6 seconds of CP Optimizer time to solve to proven optimality (versus 4.5 seconds for the hardest of 100,000 random draws) while denying CP-SAT an optimality proof. The optimizer even recovers known structure from running time alone: it locates a SAT clause/variable ratio near the phase transition and finds structurally different champions for the two scheduling engines.


<div id='math.OC'></div>

# math.OC [[Back]](#toc)

### [352] [On the possibilities and limitations of LLM-based multi-agent systems for multi-criteria decision-making in healthcare operations management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23673)
*Marco Boresta, Tommaso Giovannelli, Sepehr Namirad, Giulia Riccardi*

Main category: math.OC

Abstract: Multi-criteria models for healthcare operations need weights that express how stakeholders trade off patient waiting time, provider workload, and operating cost, but these weights are difficult to quantify. Teams of large language model (LLM) agents, each prompted to represent a stakeholder, have been used to supply them. We propose a framework for assessing the reliability of LLM-generated preferences as inputs to multi-objective optimization and quantifying how their uncertainty affects decisions. We study explicit preferences by asking agents to rate the importance of the criteria in an outpatient appointment scheduling problem, and implicit preferences by inferring trade-offs from the agents' pairwise choices between emergency department configurations. The computational experiments show that the explicit preferences respond to the clinic's operating conditions and differ across roles, but deliberation among the agents can erase these differences, whereas simple averaging preserves them. The pairwise choices used to reveal the agents' implicit preferences depend largely on which option is presented first, and we find limited evidence that a statistical model using contextual information alongside presentation order predicts the agents' choices on new comparison pairs better than a model using presentation order alone. We then formulate robust weighted-sum optimization models to account for preference uncertainty. In most cases, these models select different schedules or configurations from those obtained using average preference weights. These results indicate that LLM-generated preferences need an audit of this kind before they are used as optimization weights in healthcare decision support.


<div id='cs.NE'></div>

# cs.NE [[Back]](#toc)

### [353] [Upper bounds on the number of causal pieces in spiking neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22484)
*Dominik Dold, Dennis Elbrächter, Philipp Petersen*

Main category: cs.NE

Abstract: The input space of single-spike neural networks decomposes into causal pieces, and the number of causal pieces has been shown to provide a lower bound on the approximation error for some spiking neural networks. While this decomposition is applicable to a wide range of spiking neuron models, the limits of how many causal pieces can be realised by spiking neural networks are still unknown. In this work, we derive two complementary upper bounds on the number of causal pieces in feedforward single-spike neural networks. First, we establish a combinatorial bound given by the poly-Bernoulli numbers, which applies to a broad class of spiking neuron models. The bound is of order $e^{2(L-1)N\log N+\mathcal{O}(N)}$, with network depth $L$ and maximum layer width $N$. Second, for a specific set of neuron models, we derive a bound based on hyperplane arrangements. Applying the latter framework, we obtain bounds of order $\mathcal{O}\!\left(N^{(4L-3)(N_0-1)}\right)$ for the non-leaky integrate-and-fire model with exponential synapses and the ReLU-type spike response model, and $\mathcal{O}\!\left(N^{L(N_0-1)}\right)$ for the leaky integrate-and-fire model with Dirac-$\delta$ synapses, where $N_0$ is the input dimension. For the non-leaky integrate-and-fire model with Dirac-$\delta$ synapses, additional temporal structure allows us to sharpen the general combinatorial bound to one that depends only on $N_0$, independently of the network depth and widths.


### [354] [SpikeQ: Spike-Guided Post-Training Quantization for Spiking Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23046)
*Kai Sun, Peibo Duan, Zhanglu Yan, Guowei Zhang, Yongsheng Huang, Levin Kuhlmann*

Main category: cs.NE

Abstract: On neuromorphic chips, spiking neural networks (SNNs) consume far less energy than artificial neural networks (ANNs), but the limited memory of these chips requires low-bit synaptic weights and membrane potentials. Existing post-training quantization (PTQ) methods provide them without retraining and, like those for ANNs, minimize numerical errors such as the mean squared error (MSE). In an SNN, however, a quantization error reaches later layers only through the spikes it flips. We find that errors of equal MSE can cause very different accuracy losses, whereas errors that flip the same fraction of spikes cause the same loss. We therefore propose SpikeQ, a spike-guided PTQ framework. For weights, we select the level of each weight by simulating the downstream neurons and minimizing their spike errors. For membrane potentials, most existing methods quantize the state after the reset, whereas neuromorphic chips compare the threshold with the membrane potential stored at a fixed bit width. We target this pre-fire quantization and build a reset-aware grid that places the threshold on a rounding boundary, so rounding never flips the spike at the current time step. We evaluate SpikeQ on seven benchmarks of image classification, semantic segmentation, and object detection. With 2-bit pre-fire membrane potentials, every existing grid collapses, whereas SpikeQ retains most of the full-precision accuracy. With both at 4 bits, the models stay within 2.59 points of full precision, shrink their memory footprint 7.8--7.9 times, and use 6.4--6.9 times less energy.


<div id='eess.SY'></div>

# eess.SY [[Back]](#toc)

### [355] [Distributed Performance Boosting through Neural Control for Plug-and-Play Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23292)
*Danilo Saccani, Leonardo Massai, Giancarlo Ferrari-Trecate*

Main category: eess.SY

Abstract: We address the problem of improving the performance of pre-stabilized networked systems subject to plug-and-play (PnP) operations, namely additions or removals of subsystems. While distributed baseline controllers can preserve stability after a PnP transition, augmenting them with expressive neural policies can invalidate the formal stability guarantees. To overcome this issue, we build on recently proposed Performance Boosting controllers based on the Internal Model Control (IMC) principle, which reduce closed-loop stability to a finite $\ell_2$-gain requirement on an auxiliary operator. In the distributed setting, this operator is formed by interconnecting local neural operators that exchange information only between neighboring subsystems. We then derive free parametrizations of this interconnection, namely explicit maps from unconstrained trainable variables to interconnected neural operators satisfying a prescribed network gain bound for every parameter value. The obtained distributed neural controller can therefore be trained by unconstrained gradient-based optimization without stability constraints or projection steps, while retaining the prescribed gain bound at every training iterate. We develop two complementary parametrizations. A neighbor-based compositional parametrization uses local coupling bounds, so that after a PnP event only subsystems whose bounds change need to update their gain parameters. A network-norm parametrization instead uses a bound on the overall coupling matrix and can reduce conservatism, at the price of requiring network-wide information. A PnP DC-microgrid case study shows that the proposed neural layer improves voltage transients under topology changes and load variations while preserving nominal closed-loop stability.


<div id='cs.HC'></div>

# cs.HC [[Back]](#toc)

### [356] [PersonaCUABench: Benchmarking Computer-Use Agents Serving Diverse Personas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22990)
*Haoming Li, Defu Cao, Xu Hu, Yingtao Luo, Yiyang Wang, Xianghe Xu, Jiashu He, Wanting Jiang, Yifan Zhang, Yunhan Zhao, Siyang Wu, Chen Yang, Haixin Wang, Zheyuan Deng, Taoran Li, Jingwen Zhang, Zhejian Peng, Shushu Liang, Yue Zhao, Bingzhe Li, Feng Chen, Yifan Wang, Jintao Huang, Yijun Wang, Yuexing Hao, Xiaomin Li*

Main category: cs.HC

Abstract: Computer-use agents (CUAs) are commonly evaluated against fixed task specifications, but users may omit requirements, add constraints, or require approval before consequential actions. Completing the benchmark instruction does not guarantee serving the person who asked. We introduce PersonaCUABench, which holds 74 tasks across 11 domains fixed and varies only who asks. A persona agent, playing one of 44 personas in four groups, rewrites each instruction in that user's own words and answers the CUA's questions, while the CUA sees only the dialogue, never the profile or the original instruction. Across nine backbones (3,330 runs), mean task completion falls by 36% relative to the original requests. The losses have two sources: in 59.9% of cells that lose points, the agent engages with what the user asked for and still gets it wrong, and 11.2% are stops the user requested that the checker scores as failures. Even when a persona request is fully completed, the user gives the top satisfaction rating in only 62.7% of cells, against 84.1% for the original requests. CUAs should therefore be evaluated not only on task completion but also on whether they meet the requirements and constraints users express. Code: this https URL . Project page: this https URL


### [357] [Designing for Imperfect Prompts: A Simulation-Based Evaluation of an LLM-Based Enterprise Data Assistant Using Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23304)
*Niklas Wagner, Alexander Mädche*

Main category: cs.HC

Abstract: Enterprise data assistants using large language models (LLMs) promise democratized data access in organizations. Yet in our real-world deployment within the CFO unit's data lake at an industry partner, we observed that business users frequently pose imperfect prompts that include domain-specific vocabulary and lack schema references or structural information. This Design Science Research study examines automatic resolution using a semantic mediation layer. We design and evaluate two design variants: schema-based mediation via semantic similarity, and knowledge graph-based mediation leveraging an automatically constructed knowledge graph from the data lake. Our simulation-based evaluation using real-world data, supported by LLM-as-a-Judge assessments and human validation, shows that knowledge graph-based mediation outperforms the schema-based approach in Precision, Recall, F1, and preserving user intent. We propose a design principle for knowledge graph-based mediation, demonstrating that structured knowledge representation bridges user intent and database structure and guides the design of enterprise data assistants across skill levels.


### [358] [PresenceBench: An Exploratory Evaluation of Large Language Model Adaptation to User State](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23625)
*Peña Arroyo Gallego, Mauro Ruiz Moreno, Alejandro Acien, Sergio Romero-Tapiador, Teresa Arroyo-Gallego*

Main category: cs.HC

Abstract: Presence-aware intelligence is the capacity of an AI system's intelligence layer to adapt appropriately to the functional state of the person it serves (here readiness, flow, and stability) while preserving task quality and safety. We introduce PresenceBench, an instrument for measuring it. PresenceBench evaluates how the intelligence layer uses a supplied state, not how that state is sensed, and breaks the capability into measurable properties. Two are tested here. Receptivity: does the model take up the state and give a better response than with no signal? Specificity: does any improvement depend on the correct state, rather than on extra structured context? A third, adaptation across states, is a required endpoint not measured in this release. A five-arm causal design answers both questions, against each model's own baseline and an equally structured mismatched-state control, scored by a correctness-gated human paired comparison. The exploratory evaluation covered four models across eighteen single-turn, six fixed three-turn and six simulated natural-conversation scenarios. Supplying a synthetic state, raw or translated into a response policy, gave human-preference point estimates above 0.5 for all four models (Claude Sonnet 5, DeepSeek V4 Pro, Gemini 3.6 Flash, GPT-5.6 Terra), with three of eight 95% intervals excluding chance. Specificity was not resolved: no per-model interval excluded chance, and the design cannot yet separate the state values from a generic instruction to adapt. Policy delivery reduced priced cost and output tokens against each model's baseline; these are resource-use changes, not efficiency gains, because correctness non-inferiority was not established. Safety-floor failures were rare (13 of 360 cases), with a non-significant excess under policy delivery. We report the instrument, the frozen results, and the controls a confirmatory study needs.


<div id='physics.comp-ph'></div>

# physics.comp-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [359] [Physics-Transfer Learning: A Physics-Constrained Theory for Zero-Shot Generalization Along the Ladder of Complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22376)
*Yingjie Zhao, Zhiping Xu*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

Abstract: Reliable out-of-distribution generalization remains a central challenge in machine learning, particularly when models are deployed in data-scarce high-fidelity domains. Existing approaches often rely on statistical distribution alignment, or invariance assumptions that can fail when domain shifts reflect changes in underlying mechanisms. Inspired by a longstanding paradigm in physics that understands complex systems by starting from tractable idealizations and progressively incorporating additional mechanisms and levels of fidelity, we propose physics-transfer (PT) learning, a physics-constrained learning theory for zero-shot generalization along a ladder of complexity. PT constructs chains of anchor domains (CADs) that connect low-cost source domains to complex targets and introduces computable feasibility and reliability indicators for assessing transfer before target-domain labels are observed. Under physical-consistency and stable-representation assumptions, we derive an operational upper bound on target risk in terms of source empirical risk, the PT indicators, and finite-sample errors, and establish convergence as adjacent complexity gaps shrink and data coverage improves. We evaluate PT on excited-state energy prediction in quantum systems and dynamical-transition prediction in classical systems using neural networks trained without target-domain labels. Across substantial distribution shifts, PT yields accurate high-fidelity predictions, while the indicators identify incorrect physical mappings before target-domain prediction fails. These results provide a model-agnostic framework for designing, diagnosing, and controlling generalization in data-scarce learning problems.


### [360] [An Adaptive-Horizon Graph Neural Operator for coupled surrogate morphodynamic modelling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22432)
*Ying Cao, Yifan Yang, Junqiang Xia, Julia C. Mullarney, Mário J. Franca*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

Abstract: Morphodynamic simulations require repeated coupling between hydrodynamic solvers, sediment transport modules, and bed updating. For long duration, multi-scenario simulations on large unstructured meshes, such coupling is computationally demanding. This study proposes an Adaptive-Horizon Graph Neural Operator (AH-GNO) that formulates bed evolution as a graph operator learning problem from recent hydrodynamic and topographic states to predict future bed elevation increments. The model operates directly on the native unstructured meshes. The approach integrates area-weighted graph learning, historical state selection, and forecast horizon estimation, allowing the effective history length (K) and update interval (B) to vary with the evolving flow and bed state. Using Telemac2D/GAIA solutions as supervised numerical references, the framework is evaluated on the official Bump and Yen bend benchmarks and on a field-scale application (Waiho River, New Zealand). Results show that the AH-GNO reproduces the main patterns of bedform migration, bend-scale erosion and deposition, and localized bed adjustment. Across the three cases, the tested CPU-GPU deployment reduced post-training bed-update workflow time from 12~1204 s with GAIA to 0.08~3.34 s with AH-GNO. This improvement corresponds to deployment-specific accelerations of approximately 150~360 times relative to 4-core GAIA CPU configurations, and with 105~186 times gains for 20-core CPU configurations. These results suggest that, while retaining hydrodynamic feedback from TELEMAC-2D, a learned operator for bed updating can provide an efficient acceleration component for morphodynamic scenario analysis and rapid coupled simulation.


<div id='quant-ph'></div>

# quant-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [361] [Encodings and Node Architecture: Lessons from the Fault-Tolerant Distributed Transversal Fanout](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22966)
*Seng W. Loke*

Main category: quant-ph

Abstract: An encoding choice specifies the quantum error-correcting code used to encode logical qubits at a given node. Different encoding choices may differ in the number of physical qubits, the number of encoded logical qubits, the code distance, and the available fault-tolerant operations. We study different encodings for a distributed transversal fanout operation and look at its implications on node architecture. Our experiments suggest that an encoding with higher distance, even if requiring more ebits, can yield a lower logical error rate for distributed operations, and that ``upgrading'' an encoding prior to distributed operations and having a cache, as part of node architecture, for qubits can be useful for distributed operations.


### [362] [Quantum-Classical Neural Networks for Solving Lindblad Master Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22691)
*Rafael Florencio Díaz, Julio Guerrero García, Elvira Romera Gutiérrez*

Main category: quant-ph

Abstract: Variational quantum algorithms have emerged as a promising approach for simulating open quantum systems, yet their expressive power and trainability remain poorly understood. We propose a variational framework for solving Lindblad master equations by minimizing a residual cost function using a compositional ansatz obtained by combining a classical neural network with a parameterized quantum circuit. The density matrix is represented through a purification on an enlarged Hilbert space, ensuring positivity, Hermiticity, and unit trace by construction, while all gradients are evaluated exactly via the parameter-shift rule. Beyond the algorithmic formulation, we derive an upper bound for the covering number of the proposed compositional model and compare it with that of purely quantum neural networks. Our analysis shows that the classical parametrization enlarges the effective hypothesis class while providing finer control over its complexity, thereby establishing a theoretical connection between expressivity, covering numbers, effective dimension, and the barren plateau phenomenon. Numerical experiments validate both the accuracy of the proposed method for representative Lindblad master equations and the theoretical predictions regarding gradient concentration. These results provide a unified variational and theoretical framework for the simulation of dissipative quantum dynamics using compositional quantum machine learning models.


### [363] [Learning angles for quantum approximate optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23296)
*Johannes Jakubik, Isabelle Wittmann, Zaheed Gaffoor, Gciniwe Simphiwe Baloyi, Craig Mahlasi, Etienne Vos, Thomas Brunschwiler, Daniel J. Egger*

Main category: quant-ph

Abstract: The Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) and its variants are leading algorithms to help solve challenging combinatorial optimization problems. QAOA maps these problems to Ising Hamiltonians such that their ground state is the desired solution. Candidate solutions are then drawn from a variational quantum circuit. The performance of QAOA hinges on a set of variational angles. However, finding good angles is an instance-specific and computationally expensive non-convex classical optimization task. Here, we learn the mapping from the problem instance to high-quality QAOA angles. Our learned models replace the per-instance optimization, that we call the solver, with a single forward pass that is several orders of magnitude faster than the solver with a near identical quality. Our models further generalize to unseen topologies such as Barabási-Albert and 2D grid graphs. Experiments on quantum hardware confirm that QAOA samples with angles produced by our open-source models are indistinguishable from solver angles. Crucially, models sensitive to the topology of the problem instance increase the quality of the QAOA angles. Our work amortizes a major bottleneck in QAOA by an up-front data generation and training cost. Importantly, this cost can be amortized over time in settings where many similar problems are optimized on a regular basis, as is often the case in applications for industry.


### [364] [Expressivity of IQP Feature-Map Quantum Kernels via Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23692)
*Valeria Repetto, Stefano Melchionna, Romina D'Aurizio*

Main category: quant-ph

Abstract: Quantum kernels are among the most promising methods in quantum machine learning, providing a natural way to embed data into high-dimensional Hilbert spaces via quantum circuits and capturing non-linear relationships in data. Their learning behaviour is governed by the function class they represent, which is formally characterized by the structure of the associated Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space (RKHS). However, a theoretical characterization of this function class, and of how it depends on the choice of feature map and its hyperparameters, is still lacking. In this work, we focus on the Instantaneous Quantum Polynomial (IQP) feature map and derive a Fourier representation of the associated RKHS generators as finite sums of trigonometric functions evaluated on polynomially transformed input variables, with a finite set of accessible frequencies determined by the circuit construction and scaled by the bandwidth hyperparameter. This representation identifies three bandwidth regimes with qualitatively distinct expressive properties from localised, through multimodal, to periodic. Numerical experiments on synthetic datasets with controlled periodic and multimodal structure, across data dimensions and feature-map configurations, reproduce the three regimes in both supervised classification and unsupervised anomaly detection, benchmarked against a classical Gaussian kernel. These results make the relationship between expressivity and data structure explicit for IQP kernels: expressive power translates into a practical benefit only when the accessible frequencies match with the structure of the data, which provides a criterion for selecting the bandwidth when the periodic or multimodal structure of data is known.


### [365] [Quality-of-Transmission Estimation in Quantum Networks: Compute, Simulate, or Learn?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23827)
*Anis Bekri, Amar Abane, Van Sy Mai, Abdella Battou, Saddek Bensalem*

Main category: quant-ph

Abstract: Future quantum networks will rely on control-plane decisions such as routing, scheduling, entanglement provisioning, purification, and memory allocation. Each decision depends on a timely and reliable estimate of the quality of the entanglement that a candidate path can deliver, with end-to-end fidelity as a central metric. Inspired by the optical-networking experience with quality-of-transmission (QoT) estimation, this article asks when a quantum controller should compute fidelity analytically, simulate it, or learn a surrogate model. The optical analogy is useful but incomplete: optical QoT estimation often predicts a scalar transmission margin, whereas quantum QoT estimation must account for probabilistic entanglement generation, random memory waiting times, decoherence, state consumption, and measurement constraints that can make fidelity a stochastic target. We propose a compute/simulate/learn framework for quantum QoT estimation and illustrate it with a simulation-based proof of concept. Our study shows that analytical composition can be sufficient in simple regimes, that simulation becomes necessary when stochastic memory effects shape the delivered fidelity distribution, and that lightweight learned surrogates can approximate simulator labels at much lower runtime cost for online control. We close with open challenges for deployment, including stochastic targets, sim-to-real transfer, data-generation cost, physics-informed learning, and generalization beyond individual paths to full network topologies.


### [366] [Task-aligned spectral inductive biases in parameterized quantum circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23834)
*Felix Frohnert, Adrián Pérez-Salinas, Sebastian Lange, Marcus Schwaebisch, Evert van Nieuwenburg, Vedran Dunjko*

Main category: quant-ph

Abstract: Parameterized quantum circuits are widely investigated in quantum machine learning, yet how their architecture can provide task-aligned inductive biases remains poorly understood. We study data reuploading quantum circuits with two distinct inputs: a signal that varies between samples and a fixed, task-defined spectral query that specifies how the signal should be probed. We show that encoding the query can change the circuit's hypothesis class over the signal by modulating how its existing signal frequencies are combined, without introducing new sample-dependent information. This mechanism matches tasks built on nonuniform Fourier transforms, whose evaluation frequencies specify the query and whose sampling positions guide its encoding. We examine this connection in a classification task inspired by synthetic aperture radar, where acquisition geometry determines a query for classifying unfocused spectral patches before image formation. Across small-scale spectral and radar-like tasks, aligned queries improve learning from limited data over parameter-matched circuits with mismatched or absent queries. These results provide a route from known spectral task structure to the design and validation of inductive biases in parameterized quantum circuits.


<div id='nlin.CD'></div>

# nlin.CD [[Back]](#toc)

### [367] [From Flow-Box Invariants to Global Conservation Laws: Machine Learning-Assisted Detection of Singularities and Near-Integrability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23760)
*Jimmie Adriazola, Ioannis G. Kevrekidis, Panayotis G. Kevrekidis, Wei Zhu*

Main category: nlin.CD

Abstract: Conservation laws reveal invariant structure in dynamical systems and constrain their evolution to lower-dimensional level sets. Locally, however, per the flow-box theorem, every $d$-dimensional system generically admits $d-1$ functionally independent conservation laws near a regular point even though, generically, only a few such laws persist globally. Data-driven methods typically learn conservation laws on a prescribed region of phase space from which training samples are drawn, leaving their behavior beyond that region unresolved. We formulate this local-to-global continuation problem and develop an {expanding-domain neural deflation} framework that tracks the number of independent conservation laws over nested domains. The method recovers the expected $d-1$ local invariants and detects their successive breakdown as the domain expands, relaxing to the expected global conservation law limit. It distinguishes the local invariants (associated with Frobenius integrability) from persistent global structure and shows that the domain scale over which additional laws survive reflects proximity to Liouville integrability. Pointwise losses can localize isolated obstructions, whereas in more complicated dynamics the breakdown of our loss functions is gradual and spatially diffuse. The framework thus provides a data-driven approach for studying the persistence and breakdown of (so-far-learned) conservation laws across phase space.


<div id='stat.ME'></div>

# stat.ME [[Back]](#toc)

### [368] [Conformal Policy Selection: When to Deploy AI Decisions for Targeted Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22930)
*Junyu Cao, Ruijiang Gao, Maytal Saar-Tsechansky, Mingzhang Yin*

Main category: stat.ME

Abstract: Artificial-intelligence (AI) policies are increasingly used to make consequential decisions in medicine, finance, education, and public services, yet deploying them responsibly requires knowing not only whether a policy helps on average, but for whom. Existing tools for off-policy evaluation and safe policy learning provide population-level guarantees, but some individuals may experience adverse treatment effects. We recast targeted deployment as a multiple-testing problem: for each candidate unit, test whether the outcome under the AI policy would exceed a user-specified threshold, and control the false discovery rate (FDR) among the selected set. Our method, Conformal Policy Selection, combines weighted conformal $p$-values that account for the distributional shift from behavior to target policy with a two-step selection-and-pruning procedure. We prove finite-sample FDR control and establish a doubly robust asymptotic guarantee, where FDR remains controlled if either the outcome model or the propensity model is correctly specified. Synthetic experiments verify these guarantees across four misspecification regimes, and a semi-synthetic study built on real electronic-health-record covariates illustrates how the same FDR control can act as a deployment safety gate, bounding the harmful treatments that a globally trained policy would otherwise administer to a high-risk subpopulation where it misgeneralizes. The framework offers a principled method for safe AI deployment in high-stakes applications.


### [369] [Causal Discovery beyond Conditional Independence: Identifiability, Algorithms, and Statistical Inference for Directed Acyclic and Cyclic Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23543)
*Yang Ni*

Main category: stat.ME

Abstract: Learning causal structure from data requires assumptions about mechanisms, noises, observation schemes, and interventions. Historically, causal discovery has relied primarily on conditional independence. This review focuses on modern causal discovery methods that exploit properties of distributions beyond conditional independence. Our organizing principle separates the source of identification from the statistical estimator and its computational implementation. This perspective connects conditional-independence methods with distributional asymmetries, conditional-moment restrictions, and variation across environments or individuals. We examine discovery under latent confounding and feedback, distinguishing causal information about observed variables from reconstruction of latent structure and emphasizing the additional semantics required by cyclic models. We also distinguish population identification, frequentist and Bayesian consistency, uncertainty quantification, and algorithmic guarantees. Throughout, examples show why the same observational distribution can support different conclusions under different model classes. We conclude with guidance for comparing methods at their identifiable targets and a research agenda for uncertainty, heterogeneous populations, non-scalar variables, and the intersection of discrete data, feedback, and hidden confounding.
